## EINLEITUNG. — INTRODUCTION.

§ 1. What philosophy is in general cannot be explained or learned prior to or outside of it; for it exists only in its development and in the knowledge of it. Only the complete exposition and unfolding of its content — an exposition proceeding from philosophy itself and guided by the method inherent in and adequate to its concept — can produce both the subject-matter of philosophy, which is not encountered in representation or experience, first for cognition and together with it, as well as the true proof for the correctness of a definition which might perhaps, as in other sciences, be given at the very outset and at the head of the whole.

Anmerkung. In comparison with other (non-philosophical) sciences — which customarily begin at once with definitions and explanations of a determinate and presupposed subject-matter, known at least by name in general and present in representation, and which in this as well as in their further procedure presuppose the ordinary doctrine of the methodology of Logic — the difference of the beginning at which philosophy stands in relation to those sciences may be made apparent at the very outset. Cf. Hegel's Encyclopaedia of the Philosophical Sciences §§ 1 ff. Since philosophy has nothing presupposed of this kind, nor can have any, and must rather first produce the systematic character of its procedure itself: since also every representation of it, its subject-matter and content, its mode of cognition and method, must prior to its actual cognition be rejected and negated by it as something necessarily untrue — already for the reason that it is only representation, not scientific cognition — so the particular difficulties to which its beginning is subject become apparent already to a preliminary consideration and general reflection. For the beginner there is the added difficulty that not even the most commonly employed expressions — such as concept, representation, reflection, essence, Idea, and the like — can appear for him in their true significance, which lies only in knowledge, before they emerge of themselves at their proper place through the progress of philosophical cognizing, and only then are understood in their truth and necessity. The objection is of no avail here that others have already gone through the whole — namely at least the teachers and compilers of textbooks — and have found its definition as well as the systematic arrangement. For, quite apart from the fact that in textbooks what is most necessary — namely the inner necessity of the progression itself — is frequently lacking, and the arrangement therefore appears as something arbitrary or as something only externally considered: it is for the beginner in philosophy, who is not to learn by rote but to think and to cognize, even with the most systematically perfect textbook, a matter of cognition itself and of insight into the inner necessity of the self-developing and further self-determining progression; he must therefore produce the matter along with it, or rather only follow it in its own development, through which it produces itself. Only through this necessity is philosophy capable of being true science; and only in knowledge does its subject-matter also have its force and significance — outside of knowledge it is something empty. Into the element of mere representation and opinion about philosophy, and prior to its actual beginning, there also falls that attitude toward it which finds the study of it particularly difficult or rejects it entirely on the ground that there are so many different philosophies and different views of individual philosophers, and one therefore cannot know which philosophy is the true one, or which view is the right one. One overlooks the fact that it is philosophy in which the particular systems have their common root, and whose exposition they attempt. If the discussion is merely of diversity in individual subject-matters, this diversity will, upon closer examination and investigation, at once be traceable to a determinate difference in which the universal is simultaneously recognized; but if the diversity affects the whole principle and standpoint of different systems, then in part the foregoing also applies here, and in part the history of philosophy exhibits not so much a varied multiplicity of views and opinions as rather, in everything that truly deserves the name of philosophy, the necessary course which philosophical Thinking has taken. This Thinking, however, is what can be spared no one who approaches philosophy, and the actual study of it can be begun with the resolve to think alone rather than with the superficial reflection which stops at the mere observation that various philosophers have said various things, and which holds to this most external manner of speaking about philosophy while thereby forgoing Thought. To a philosophical system, however — and philosophy has its necessary system through itself — which has the universal of Reason in and for itself as its principle, that diversity can least of all be objected, since from it the deviations of other philosophers and the subordinate standpoints of particular attempts are themselves recognized in their ground.

§ 2. On account of this nature of the matter, any definition given straightway at the outset — even if it subsequently proves correct — for example, that philosophy is the science of Reason insofar as this becomes conscious of itself as of all Being (after Hegel, Encyclopaedia of the Philosophical Sciences § 5, p. 6), or the science of Reason as self-knowing Truth, and similar explanations, can and should impart only a preliminary historical acquaintance, and serve especially for the beginner only to indicate to him the subject-matter in general for the time being: whereby the occupation of one's own reflection may hold to the fact that one is to deal throughout with what is rational — consequently with that which everyone has and finds within himself — yet with this rational element in its pure form, essence, and Truth.

Anmerkung. In the same sense philosophy could be called the science of the Idea (of Ideas in general, after Plato) — i.e. of self-identical Reason, or of Spirit knowing itself as Spirit — using the simple expression of Aristotle (Metaphysics XII [Lambda], 9): the science of Truth; further, the alone and thoroughly rational Knowing; also the science of freedom (see Hegel, loc. cit. p. 7; cf. Aristotle, loc. cit. I, 2.) etc. — were it not that at its beginning, or rather still before it, such a naming were altogether something purposeless and contradictory to the nature of the emerging science. Such names are therefore at this point still merely empty words. Every further explanation added to the definition would, however, necessarily lead straightway into the midst of the matter — with which it is first a concern on the way of science — and would, without cognition, presuppose the cognition of its essence. The common phrase in the manner and language of ordinary representation — by which one gives someone a concept or a representation of something — has there the sense that something unknown is brought before the representation, or at least approached more nearly, by reference to something otherwise known, seen, heard, and the like. Of philosophy, however, no such representation can be given — as little as of God — even if the representation itself were not already what is crooked and incorrect in the matter; and what in that phrase one calls concept is still nothing more than a representation. If one were to say that philosophy is pure Thinking, this is in so far correct as that outside of philosophy there is no pure Thinking, and everyone is also conscious of the formal activity which he calls his Thinking. But since this is a concrete Thinking, he, pointed to pure Thinking, will not know what to make of it — i.e. will have no representation of it, or will perhaps equate it with thinking-nothing — because just the content of pure Thinking (the pure Thoughts) is for him something entirely unknown, and this content can also be acquired for Consciousness only within philosophy and through philosophical Thinking, and then, moreover, exists only in knowledge and not without it.

§ 3. A like state of affairs obtains with the divisions prefaced to the actual matter and its exposition. Philosophy can find, produce, ground, and justify its division and inner articulation — as well as the autonomous method adequate to its concept — only within itself and on the path of its development. Only at the goal of its completed way is it — in contrast to its earlier immediacy, i.e. in contrast to its former immediate emergence and comportment in the mode of mere (such and such) Being — the knowing of itself and the inner necessity of its development that fills its concept, and it returns, surveying and comprehending itself in its totality, mediated with itself, to its beginning.

Anmerkung. It is nothing unusual in philosophical textbooks that, as introduction, one speaks first in general about what philosophy is, according to external determination and reflection; that thereafter, in like fashion, the allegedly necessary parts of philosophy and their subdivisions are found; and the whole philosophical material — as something already present and given — is treated in just the same way as when, for instance, one has before one a collection of coins or of stuffed animals and birds, and the question arises as to the most expedient arrangement and classification for easy and convenient survey: whereupon a proper catalogue of all the pieces is compiled and issued. In a similar way, such catalogues and surveys of philosophy — so-called schemata, furnished with many newly invented, especially Greek, names — are devised and fabricated; indeed, issued without the execution of the matter, or at least without the detailed one, as if they were sample-cards for the display of the system and for the free selection of the buyer, since there are several philosophical establishments of this kind that bring this tabular understanding to market, each wishing to appear before the world with a particular and distinctive classification and nomenclature of its articles. Concerning such universally current divisions, cf. Kapp's Encyclopaedia of Philosophy, Part 1, pp. 25 ff. This Appearance has something remarkable — less on account of the content, which has already for the most part been consumed in the division, than as a phenomenon of which the history of philosophy has testimony and account to give. It may be left to the latter to determine and demonstrate more precisely what philosophy has been for some philosophers in this period, and therewith also to look into the very ground of these philosophers' philosophical inwardness, and to measure the height or depth of their Consciousness equal in measure to its subject-matter and content. For the present, however, one should almost believe that, since classifying and schematizing — and in part also constructing, dividing from above and from the whole down to the smallest pieces — is the first thing with which some begin and also close philosophy and every part of it, for them this dividing itself is also the first and highest thing of philosophy in general, the principle of the whole and the Absolute itself, so too their own highest standpoint. Even if dividing is everywhere else so ordinary and traditional that in all sciences and in every written discourse, at first and immediately — or at least straightway after the definition — a neat division or disposition is given or required: the methodology of Logic may everywhere else be presupposed and reference made to the ordinary doctrine of divisions. Philosophy, by contrast, can presuppose nothing whatsoever — least of all at its beginning — or it must accept that everything presupposed by it is rejected and negated. If, accordingly, the assertion is opposed to any prefaced division that not merely this particular division but division in general is something entirely erroneous and false which ought not to obtain at all: then philosophy, in justification of its procedure and in proof of the contrary, would have absolutely nothing but the mere assurance of the same, or an appeal to what is traditional — i.e. something presupposed — and would in general itself immediately begin with the contrary of what it intends to be. So entirely must philosophy first conquer every inch of its domain. These remarks may provisionally serve to forestall the demand upon the author that, for the attainment of a guild-conforming mastership, he ought above all else to have also furnished the masterpiece of a division. Such a division will follow later. In general, however, all the divisions and subdivisions given in this book, as such, lay no claim to particular significance, and may in the end justify themselves only through the whole.

§ 4. Since, however, philosophy — as a science to be communicated to others or produced within them — has natural Consciousness and ordinary representation as its given presupposition, as its starting-point and point of departure, while natural representation in its manner is still very remote from the manner of philosophical Knowing and Thinking, and is indeed opposed to it: philosophy has in this regard also the task of conducting Consciousness — not yet philosophically formed — along the way leading to it, and of mediating to it the element, not immediately given to it, in which philosophy as pure science moves. This way to science — which is not yet science itself but has science as its inner guide, and cannot be accomplished without the method of science — is shown and traversed by the philosophical Propaedeutic.

Anmerkung. The philosophical Propaedeutic is therefore an education of the subject to philosophy or to pure philosophical Thinking, just as philosophy itself — from the side of what it affords the subject — is the school of its absolute Education. What makes such a mediation as that indicated in § 4 first necessary rests upon the difference between philosophical Thinking and Cognizing and the merely natural Thinking and ordinary representation of concrete, subjective, or empirical Consciousness in general, which must first be purified and liberated from this natural concreteness and from all opinions and representations grown up in and with it — indeed from the very mode of representation itself. In all other things, crafts, arts, sciences, it is acknowledged and established that without learning the rudiments, without a gradual advance, and without much practice and study, one can acquire neither proficiency nor judgment concerning the subject-matters of the field; only in philosophy does everyone readily believe himself entitled to a judgment and capable of getting on with the subject-matters of philosophical Cognition. Yet the incapacity of natural Consciousness and ordinary representation for this — which seeks to cognize philosophical, i.e. absolute, Truth without the discipline of Thinking — shows itself to the philosopher already in the questions it addresses to philosophy for answer, and in the fact that it is concerned above all with the results of philosophical inquiry for this or that matter dear to its heart, as though such results, for themselves alone, without the Cognizing and without the movement of the Thinking that mediates them, constituted the Truth and could be of any help to it. Besides, the assumed difference between natural Consciousness and philosophical Thinking and Knowing is here of course itself still only an assertion whose truth could be denied. But since the difference between knowing and not knowing something — before one has practised and learned it — subsists of itself and is self-evident, and since even he who comes to philosophy as a beginner thereby acknowledges his hitherto ignorance in it, the difference between philosophically and non-philosophically formed Consciousness may be granted without further ado. If natural Consciousness denies the difference, let it come and see how it is received and treated by philosophical formation with the manner of its comportment. This very thing, however, the Propaedeutic itself shows, for it takes that Consciousness at first only as it gives itself, and conducts it further step by step. — Of the subject, moreover, nothing further is required and presupposed than that it be a rational one — i.e. one in which universal Reason in itself [Latin: potentia], even if not yet recognized in its Truth by the subject itself, is present; for the latter is what philosophy is first to afford it.

§ 5. Through its particular determination — to elevate the beginner across the various stages and modes of natural and appearing Consciousness to the element of philosophical Knowing — this preliminary school of philosophy also obtains its particular, necessary character; the direction of its way prescribed by purpose and goal; the determinate manner of its procedure and progression. Apart from this necessary content, it may, however, also take up within itself much that — while not immediately necessary for the proximate purpose — is nevertheless not superfluous merely as historical knowledge provisionally, insofar as it contributes to the expansion of general acquaintance with philosophy and with the views to be formed of it and its content, or subsequently demonstrates at its proper place the serviceableness and expediency of its inclusion and preliminary treatment. General remarks and elucidations, explanations of words and matters, as well as references from the history of philosophy and historical remarks of this kind are to be counted therein; even subject-matters from scientifically later particular parts of philosophy may in the indicated respect already find a provisional place here, only that they cannot here yet appear in the significance and necessity which they have at their proper place in the context of actual science.

Anmerkung. Of the four sections into which this Propaedeutic falls — of which the first treats of Consciousness in general, the second of the necessary stages of Consciousness falling into Appearance, while the third is of psychological and the fourth of encyclopaedical content — only the first two, which therefore also form the first main division of the whole, are propaedeutic in the strict sense according to the indicated concept; the last two contain more what is otherwise or according to external consideration expedient and useful for the beginner, and the last therefore includes also a provisional survey of the whole of philosophy. <sup>∗</sup> As for the third, psychological section in particular, however, its content — which presents the doctrine of Spirit in outline, no longer in an educating manner but already expounding and treating — has been included in the Propaedeutic because in the presentation of theoretical philosophy it cannot well be avoided that, for the sake of others' philosophical views, one takes account here and there of the psychological, and it is therefore expedient to acquaint the beginner in advance in a determinate way with the activities and so-called faculties of theoretical Spirit. By no means, however, was the intention connected therewith of paying homage to the view of those philosophers who deem it necessary to give Logic a psychological or even anthropological foundation. That Logic — as the science of pure Reason — is a purely objective science that requires no psychological or anthropological material for the cognition of its pure Thoughts and Concepts (for which also Kant — Critique of Pure Reason, 1st edition, p. 54 — allows it, despite all other subjectivity, declaring that it draws nothing from psychology and is a demonstrated doctrine in which everything must be perfectly certain a priori), and that it therefore also has nothing to do with the subject in the sense that through particular limitations and conditions of human Cognition, objective cognizing in general is specially modified or entirely prevented, and actually only a subjective Cognition — i.e. one renouncing Truth and the Cognition of that which is in and for itself — is left over: this is to be demonstrated in greater detail elsewhere, or rather has already been demonstrated by Hegel through the entire execution of his Logic. Psychology, however, together with Anthropology and Phenomenology, belongs — as science — to the doctrine of subjective Spirit, and thereby to the doctrine of real Spirit in general (practical philosophy), which as the third principal part of the whole of philosophy presupposes at the very least theoretical philosophy as the first.

<sup>∗</sup> The author has left the last two unexecuted. (Note of the editor.)

§ 6. Since, however, philosophical Knowing and Cognizing does not merely appear as one kind of Cognizing among and beside other kinds, but at the same time comes forward with the claim to be the sole Cognition of Truth: it must prove and justify this claim, which as a mere assurance would have no advantage over the assurance of the contrary.

This, however, it is able to accomplish only by turning against all other (natural and concrete) representation and Knowing itself, and exhibiting its inadequacy, in that it refutes it out of itself, and demonstrates, of every mode of Cognition other than the philosophical, that and how it sublates itself in itself. This demonstration — which also falls to the philosophical Propaedeutic — coincides, however, with the execution of its indicated purpose on one path. From a considered stage or shape of concrete Consciousness one proceeds further because it has shown itself to be a Knowing that sublates and contradicts itself in itself, and thereby itself destroys its assumption and presupposition of Truth: this constitutes the negative side of the progression. But immediately from this negative result a new shape of Consciousness arises — one to which the series of consideration now comes — or rather has already arisen: herein consists the positive of the progression.

Anmerkung. 1) Ordinary or empirical Consciousness — to remark preliminarily something about it — shows its naturalness, i.e. its lack of philosophical formation, especially in this, that it concerns itself at first only with objects, keeps and pursues these alone in view, and contends about their truth or untruth in the opposition of true and false once fixed as firm, without reflecting upon itself in the process. Philosophical Consciousness, by contrast, has also Knowing — or the side of the I — as its subject-matter, and knows that the truth of the object does not obtain for itself, as it were on the other side, but is conditioned by Knowing and exists only in Knowing, not in mere representation. The refutation of every other mode of Cognition claiming Truth therefore consists not merely in the fact that philosophical Consciousness allows the object asserted to be true to develop dialectically in and from itself, and thereby lets it show itself what is involved in its asserted truth — whereby the asserting Consciousness can easily be brought to the point where it no longer knows how it stands with the matter (instances of this are found, for example, everywhere in the Platonic dialogues) — but still more in the fact that Consciousness itself, for which something holds as truth — along with its object, and insofar as it has thereby characterized itself in a determinate way through the manner in which it has apprehended the object and reflected it within itself — is itself considered, and is led through that dialectic which it has within itself in this determinate way to its untruth and dissolution. How many assertions holding as truths and views and opinions flowing from an untrue mode of Thinking would straightway dissolve if the quality of its own Consciousness thereby conditioned and simultaneously given were held up before the subject of those assertions — as if in a mirror — for its own contemplation of itself! "So you yourself look, in that this is truth for you. Now see how you please yourself therein!" From this side, the ancient commandment Know thyself! [Greek: Gnothi seauton] seems least of all to have come into application.

⁠2) The particular mode of Cognition of religion is here still left out of account. Although the subject-matter of religion — the highest and absolutely holy Being — is also the highest subject-matter of philosophy, it is nonetheless the case that, since in religion Spirit encompasses it in its totality but in an immediate manner, as feeling-soul, the very mode in which this subject-matter enters for religious Consciousness as such is distinguished from the philosophical mode of Cognition proceeding by the thread of mediation.

Since in religion the subject-matter confronts Consciousness within itself, and is also not as subject-matter submitted to the I but remains infinitely exalted above it, the mode of its comportment therein is inner intuition, immediate Knowing or faith, feeling, devotion, also imagination — and therefore religion is in general more nearly related to art. This holds, however, only of the actual religious act which the Consciousness of the individual — in recognition of its abandonment and need of salvation, and driven by its feeling and the longing for reconciliation with itself through a Higher than it knows itself to be — performs and celebrates. For the science of religion is likewise subject to the general requirements to be imposed upon a science as such. Since, however, religious Consciousness itself also falls into historical Appearance, it can from this side also become the subject-matter of philosophical Cognition. Since peoples — as also individuals — always express and exhibit in their religious views and in their cult, according to the level of their Education and Consciousness, the highest and best of which the essence and Consciousness of their Spirit is capable, and conversely, from that wherein they place the essence of the Divinity and of its worship — which therefore holds for them as the essence in general and as the Absolute — the Spirit's own standpoint, essence, and inwardness can be recognized: it would be very interesting if a scientific history of these religious Appearances, or of Spirit itself from the side of its religious development, were worked out in such a succession of the various stages and shapes up to Christianity, that the necessity of the general progression as well as of the transitions and of the particular emergence of each individual shape and Appearance, and their grounding contained in the general essence of Spirit and its necessary development, were demonstrated and established: whereby it would have to become visible how, with each later stage, the developing religious Consciousness goes back deeper into the essence of Spirit and approaches nearer to its concept as well as to the absolute Idea. Even in Christianity it would be demonstrable how the eternal Idea underlying it has, in its temporal Appearance in the Consciousness of Christendom receiving it and in the Spirit that holds it enclosed within itself, its necessary development and further formation — in general its necessary history. Such a treatment of the subject-matter would deliver to us not merely a history of religion such as we do not yet have, but also for mythology quite other results than in part the hitherto prevailing views thereof and the controversies concerning it have so far yielded. The scientific element pertaining thereto — which would only need to be completely carried through the concrete of Appearance — is found already, however, in Hegel's Phenomenology, VII: Religion, from pp. 625–741; and besides what has already been accomplished for mythology in what is akin thereto, several spirited — yet still undertaken without science — depictions of individual times and peoples by spirited historians would be available for the matter.

§ 7. This progression is the course of all Education in general — including the non-philosophical — and can be demonstrated in the life of an individual as well as in the life of universal Spirit (of humanity) and its history of formation.

A particular age — for example, the spirit of a time — is the system of a determinate world-view, the determinate stage of Education at which the natural, immediate Consciousness of universal Spirit presently finds itself. But the spirit of the time is this: what it is, its Being, it has not yet itself recognized; with the recognizing it would have ceased to be it. Only to a following age, a new spirit of the time, does the preceding come to Consciousness — or, what is the same, to universal Spirit it comes to Consciousness in its preceding shape — and the preceding view, the earlier prevailing mode of Thinking and representation, is thereby simultaneously rejected as an untrue and null one.

Separated from itself, Consciousness has become for Consciousness — content has become object, Being has become Knowing; simultaneously it has attained a new stage, a new condition, which now constitutes what it is.

Anmerkung. It is something tasteless and commonplace when a present-day ordinary Consciousness — itself a product of Spirit as it now is and lives — subjects the histories of past times and peoples to its judgment of yesterday and today, regards and treats former and foreign things as present and its own, and barely has an inkling of the Spirit of other times and peoples — which has nevertheless driven and borne forth the Appearances — let alone knows how to grasp and determine its essence scientifically. One should rather regard the spirit of a people — which is often only named rather than recognized — as itself the Substance and as the first (or, if one prefers the reverse, also the last) cause from which all historical Appearances in the people proceeded, since all otherwise derived and external causes, circumstances, and occasions — by which frequently the knot is more displaced upward or sideways than resolved — can produce and accomplish nothing without the Spirit that first gives them life, significance, and shape, and is the necessary first condition that even an apparent contingency can become consequential and significant. Equally incorrect is a not infrequently encountered representation of the progressive development of the human Spirit — which is indeed believed and asserted, but without more precise determination and demonstration of its moments, without explanation as to the whither and whereto, is usually regarded only as a quantitative progress into the infinite toward an indeterminate better, not as a qualitatively self-determining advance, and is least of all taken from the side that a quite new Consciousness of Spirit, a mode of Thinking qualitatively distinct from the earlier, a totally changed new world-view, dawns on a new age, and Ideas enter into Consciousness of which in the preceding there was no inkling or trace to be found.

The revived Idea of the philosophy of nature, the Idea of life, of the state, of world-history are just so many new suns that have risen in our time for the first time in the supersensible heaven for the Consciousness of Spirit going back deeper into itself; indeed, one may well say that the Idea par excellence [Greek: kat' exochen] and the absolute Concept — as the newly won or rediscovered spiritual possession and property of our time, but simultaneously a key for all — is in general what makes, not only in philosophy but also otherwise, even if recognized with less distinctness and unconsciously, in wide extension, the dividing wall between the old and new time, and that over which the present still has to contend with those for whom the deeper Cognition of Spirit is not yet present, and who — unable to detach themselves from the earlier subjective mode of Thinking — still persist in it and continue to speak from it. What is customarily called the spirit of the time is, as such, more a Being than a Knowing — the prevailing new view and mode of Thinking more still enclosed only in general feeling than raised to Cognition and science. When one speaks of demands of the spirit of the time, one thereby actually appeals to nothing more than a certain general opinion — many to only their own — but not to what the matter is in and for itself. Likewise, so-called public opinion — however important it may often be in practical matters — already indicates by its very name what it is: not a Knowing but an opinion. It falls to philosophical formation to examine scientifically the content of public opinion and the spirit of the time, and to bring to Cognition whatever may be true and correct therein in its absolute ground; for only in such Cognition can something hold for the philosopher — not as the mere demand of a spirit of the time that does not understand itself.

If, therefore, those who wish to pass for philosophers set themselves up as mouthpieces of the spirit of the time and of public opinion, and in so doing proceed no more soberly and rationally than the instinctively driven others who speak from what they opine but not from what they know: then they pursue a business unworthy of the philosopher, and place themselves in danger of being numbered among the mere public criers.

§ 8. The progression of Consciousness and its Education is therefore, already according to this general consideration, a movement from Being to Knowing, whose end appears at first sight incalculable.

Every transition, however, contains the negative and positive side indicated (§ 6). Over the transition itself — which has taken place within it — and over its necessity, natural life has no Consciousness; it does not know how it has come to pass; it was this and is it no longer; just as little does it know what it now is. Philosophical Consciousness, by contrast, recognizes the necessity of these transitions. As a Knowing or shape of Consciousness, in the apprehension and closer consideration of its content and of the self-contradicting sides therein, sublates and annihilates itself in itself — there is given to philosophical Consciousness, along with the cognition of this sublation and the ground of its necessity, simultaneously in the negative moments themselves and in the lifting-up and comprehending of what constitutes the determinateness of these moments, a new positive comportment of Consciousness, which it now considers in itself just as it did the preceding mode.

Anmerkung. What has been said in this and the preceding section concerning the movement from Being to Knowing can easily be exemplified for the individual by instances from the most common experience, or everyone can be referred to his own experience. He who, for example, finds himself in the condition into which some affect has placed him, will emerge from this condition just as soon as he comes to Consciousness of it and begins to reflect upon it. An example of a more noble kind — to which reference may be made, and indeed from the moral and religious side, carried through several stages and alternations — is found in Goethe's Confessions of a Beautiful Soul in Wilhelm Meister's Years of Apprenticeship. For the best illustration our own most recent contemporary history may serve. To our present age, what the immediately preceding age was has come to Consciousness; it stands thereby above the preceding, and from its standpoint criticizes and condemns in the former time especially that wherein its Consciousness has taken the opposite direction: the levity in morals, the self-seeking, the shallow and worthless religious views, unbelief, the empty and null character of mere Enlightenment and the destruction of the positive, the spiritless and unsatisfying character of mere reflection and understanding-views, the utility theories, the vacuity of Ideas, etc. It is unmistakable that the spirit of our time has turned away from what formerly held good — which could no longer satisfy it — and everywhere seeks a deeper satisfaction more adequate to its true essence and concept. But since this spiritual revolution — which prepared itself for a long time inwardly and in stillness before the new day broke — necessarily first announced itself in immediate life, as it were in the material element of Spirit, and therefore belonged more still to indeterminate feeling and the feeling-soul than to determinate insight and a self-transparent self-Consciousness: so it also came about that the first — often eccentric and falling into the opposite extreme — stirrings and Appearances, while everywhere betraying the Spirit that had annihilated the previous mode of its existence and broken through the barriers of its no longer containing abode, yet everywhere still made visible the turbid, unfinished fermentation, the seeking and striving after the better. Everything in particular that — from the essence and life of Spirit and its dark regions, without the mediation of Thinking — can be immediately grasped through inner intuition, sensation, feeling: thus also presentiment, dream, somnambulism, magnetism, and in general everything pertaining to the feeling-soul, received in this period in part a special significance, in part first its existence. Thereto belongs also the general reawakening of religiosity and the new reception into which religion — but also Pietism and Mysticism — came, as Consciousness, pointed back inward to itself and the salvation within, also by the external distress of the times, again required, for its earlier vacuity, a deeper, more heartfelt satisfaction of Spirit and feeling-soul and the absolute reconciliation with its dividedness and with the outer world through eternal Truth. And lest one believe that a new developmental period of Spirit is introduced purely externally through the medium of formation and instruction and through views whose origin, further development, and propagation can be historically demonstrated and recounted — as if universal Spirit, pervading the whole, were not capable of also immediately preparing for itself in a new age and a new generation the new site of its dwelling and its actuality — it was youth in particular, in whose earnestness and religiosity, in whose scientific striving, in whose idiopathic disdain of the preceding mode of Thinking and the contentless understanding-views — which had not been instilled in them by their teachers, at least by no means universally — the new Consciousness and the deeper stirrings of the now emerging Spirit were at once given. Of the reprehensible political tendency — in particular of the intrinsically shallow democratic views — nothing is said here, although this Appearance too belongs to the character of the whole. What, however, under such contemporary phenomena of general formation and progressive development is present for the philosophical thinker and observer, consists not merely in the fact that he knows how to comprehend the — however manifold and scattered — Appearances of a preceding as well as of a following age belonging to one hearth, and recognizes their common origin, but also in the fact that the simple Thoughts and determinatenesses of Spirit underlying them — the pure essences and Concepts — present themselves to him, which are contained in the Appearances as what they are in and for themselves, and along with the Idea of Spirit he also recognizes in their inner ground or in their absolute ground the necessity of why the one passes away in time while another emerges.

§ 9. Since simultaneously at every mode of our representation or Knowing the subject-matter thereof and its truth — what holds as true in each case — is at first only our Knowing, for which the subject-matter has determined itself in such and such a way: and since this our Knowing of the subject-matter, which we call its truth, is at every change of Consciousness — with which necessarily the subject-matter as the known also changes (cf. § 18) — itself again object for our Knowing: from this it follows, to cite this provisionally here: 1) that the first Knowing — which, in comparison with the second (higher or truer), presents itself only as Appearance of Knowing — finds in this second its examination or its measure, by which Consciousness (the knowing I) compares them with each other; 2) that all Knowing and Cognizing falls within Consciousness or the I; hence the progression can also be presented as a self-developing self-cognition. — It will emerge that the end and goal of the progression is there where Knowing no longer needs to go beyond Being, because both have become equal to each other. Since it is Reason that unites these, philosophy itself can be grasped as the science in which the Knowing of Reason equals its Being, the Substance of Reason — is reached by the former — or as the Knowing of Reason of itself, filling its Substance. (Cf. § 2.)

Anmerkung. The word Truth is taken here and still further on at first — as also indicated above — only relatively, as that which holds for a Consciousness from its standpoint as the True. Truths therefore appear that also pass away one after another. To the question what Truth is — not counting from Pontius Pilate down to our time the dismissal of noble and common scepticism that leaves the riddle a riddle — the answers have presumably turned out so various because into them there immediately mingled the particular hue of the philosophical Consciousness giving them and of its general view. Not long ago it occurred that a philosopher gave three answers at once to this question, each of which is presumably itself to be Truth — namely, that it is: 1) agreement of our representations among themselves, 2) agreement of them with their objects, 3) agreement with the divine Ideas or the Absolute. If we can find none of these answers — and equally little the reduction of the last two through analysis to the first — sufficient, insofar as one is asking for something more than some truth of a relative and lower sort, the simple reason is this: because Truth can be contained only in Knowing, not in representation. A very simple explanation — against which, properly understood, nothing can be objected, and which is also maintained in the present investigation and development — has long been given: that Truth is the agreement of Knowing or Cognition with its object. That Kant (Critique of Pure Reason, 1st edition, p. 58) nonetheless grants this explanation — calling it only a nominal explanation of Truth — and finds it insufficient or unusable for his investigation, lies equally in the fundamental view of his entire philosophy once taken up by him, concerning which the more detailed account will be given in its place. Provisionally one may — as is also indicated above — draw attention to the fact that, even if Truth is agreement of Knowing with the object, and the latter therefore appears to representation as the first and the preceding to which Knowing must first make itself adequate, yet it must equally be said: the object is as it is known. If this is contrary to ordinary representation — which places Knowing for itself on one side, and the likewise for itself already finished object on the other side, and then allows them both to come together perhaps by accident (whereby the ordinary manner in which one, for example, hears a piece of news, or comes across an object of sensory intuition, hovers before representation) — it is precisely the demand of philosophy that he who approaches it should give up mere representation and opinion and make the beginning of Thinking; but it can also be shown, already in the sense of this representation, through a light reflection, that Consciousness can be brought to the recognition that, do what it may, it cannot escape the fate of having objects only as it knows them. One grants this; but representation against it conjures up — as if one were intentionally seeking an evasion of Knowing and would take special pleasure in the limitation of our Cognition — a thing-in-itself, which absolutely dwells beyond our Knowing, of which one therefore also cannot know what it is. Since this misunderstanding of the matter, widespread since Kant — by which Consciousness misleads itself — is confronted by the entire investigation following immediately, and it is necessary to return to it frequently, it may provisionally suffice to object only this much: that the thing-in-itself, or the being-in-itself of things, is itself nothing more than a particular Knowing of the thing — namely an abstraction for which it would first be necessary to furnish proof that something actually corresponds to it in reality, i.e. that there is a thing-in-itself — namely one of which we cannot know what it is — and indeed exists in the same sense that this philosophy is accustomed to connect with the words actuality, reality, and Being of things. That one has no need in any case of abstract talk about the abstract representation of a thing-in-itself, as soon as one only grasps a thing in concreto immediately, is already shown by daily experience and all practice — which takes things immediately just as they give themselves to it, and according to which the person to whom all the determinations of a thing have actually been stated and pointed out would merely be laughed at were he then, still dissatisfied with all explanation, to wish to ask what this concrete thing-in-itself is. To the above assertion, however — that the object is as it is known — the following more precise determinations may provisionally be added: firstly, that therein is certainly contained the Truth, when Knowing is also an actual pure Knowing [Greek: episteme], and what is spoken of are thereby determinations of the object that belong to it through this Knowing — which is simultaneously pure Thinking; secondly, however, that when Knowing of the object has not yet reached this stage, the known (relative, provisionally valid) Truth is by no means something static and permanent, but that both — Knowing and object — have within themselves this dialectical restlessness, through which they are led beyond their imperfect determinateness. It is therefore Knowing or Consciousness itself that, within itself, rises above itself — i.e. above its self-unable-to-maintain determinatenesses — thereby annihilates its untruth, and also has within itself the universal and certain criterion of the Truth of every Cognition demanded by Kant (loc. cit.).

## ERSTER ABSCHNITT. VON DEM BEWUSSTSEIN ÜBERHAUPT, UND SEINEM VERHÄLTNIS ZUM GEGENSTAND UND ZUM WAHREN. — FIRST SECTION. ON CONSCIOUSNESS IN GENERAL, AND ITS RELATION TO THE OBJECT AND TO THE TRUE.

§ 10. Consciousness in general is Knowing — or more determinately, knowing I. Knowing in general (likewise Cognizing), as necessarily a Knowing of something, of an other than I (the known, the object), immediately contains within itself the distinction of two distinguished but — in their distinction — mutually related determinations, one of which is the Knowing, I, the other the known, the object; while their relation, like their distinction, is Consciousness or I itself.

I distinguishes itself from its object; thereby it becomes a Knowing of the latter — or rather, equally I and its object first come to be in this distinction; prior to it there is neither I nor object. Knowing, therefore — or Consciousness — as unity and simultaneously distinction of both, is just as much the distinction of the Knower and the Known as the relation of the one to the other, of I to the object and of the object to I.

Anmerkung 1. The word Knowing is taken here at first in a quite general sense, and used almost synonymously with its subject, Consciousness — although otherwise a considerable difference obtains, inasmuch as Consciousness is often without proper Knowing, and, for example, the truths of immediate sensory certainty cannot be called Knowing.

The beginning taken here from Consciousness and Knowing already requires no justification for the reason that it is determined by the propaedeutic purpose, and therefore does not need to be an absolute beginning. Since not merely the whole of philosophy is enclosed within Knowing, but everything whatsoever first obtains its determination and significance in Knowing, it lies — as is self-evident — nearest at hand to investigate Consciousness analytically, which is something immediately given, and to ask what is thereby already necessarily given or contained therein. Fichte, in the introduction to his Doctrine of Science, takes it — like most others — as immediately certain that the beginning of science is to be made from an absolutely first, unconditionally unconditioned, and proof-requiring-nothing principle, and himself puts forward such a principle with this demand. This too is a misunderstanding that — like many others — is to receive its clarification through a more correct insight into the nature and course of Thinking. Cf. Hegel's Logic, Vol. I, pp. 6 ff. How much that is prior does the mere demand for such a principle already presuppose — whereby it, instead of being something truly and absolutely first, becomes rather something last, a result! As indeed Fichte also in the ground first deduces his first principle and allows quite a great deal to precede it. But the act [Thathandlung] of which he speaks — which this first principle is to express, "which does not occur among, nor can occur among, the empirical determinations of our Consciousness, but rather underlies all Consciousness and alone makes it possible, and which one must absolutely think as the foundation of human Knowing" — is nothing other than simple Consciousness itself, and, as I = I, more precisely self-Consciousness. This he could indeed take immediately with his self-positing I as something immediate, and to that extent what is said here concerning Consciousness and what is immediately given along with it through its concept agrees with the Fichtean development. Yet, as much as the beginning here made could be made on account of the immediate certainty which every I bears within itself of itself and its Knowing — and according to the purpose of the Propaedeutic had to be made — the immediacy of this beginning presents itself at once as an in-itself-mediated subject-matter; as indeed philosophy in general, according to the nature of the thinking and knowing I, can have no other than mediated subject-matters.

Anmerkung 2. That prior to the distinction indicated in § 10, neither I (namely as such) nor object exists for Consciousness — indeed that Consciousness as such does not yet exist at all — and that consequently one and the same act of diremption (original act as division) first lets I, object, and Consciousness emerge simultaneously: for this, I believe, one could — if further demonstration were still needed — also appeal to experience. New-born infants, for example, see and do not see — i.e. even though they have eyes, and those eyes also open and external objects mirror themselves in them, they nevertheless do not distinguish objects, neither from each other nor from themselves; both are the same, i.e. with the same act by which a distinguishing of objects from each other occurs, the distinguishing of them from the infants also occurs, because otherwise an object whatsoever would not yet be possible for them. Prior to this distinction and in relation to it, an individual is therefore still undivided one with its object; as soon as the object — namely as object — detaches itself from it, or it detaches itself from the object, which is one and the same thing, that very moment the distinction also enters, and Consciousness — previously only being-in-itself or possible — becomes thereby actual Consciousness. Although in this the Knowing of I by itself is already given and contained according to the concept, yet — just as self-Consciousness is in general the higher stage in the progression and development of Consciousness — so also in the child the Knowing of itself, insofar as it has its I as apprehended and hence itself objectified, enters only later. The question in general of what Knowing, what Consciousness is or means, does not yet seem to have been thoroughly elucidated and brought to an absolute definition by many. Taking Knowing as a matter otherwise already known to everyone, the investigation directed itself rather toward the question of the object: what can we know? — as, for instance, the Kantian Critique was an investigation of Consciousness in this sense (see Hegel's Encyclopaedia, p. 224); or also that other — connected with the first and become famous — question: how do representations of external things arise in us? (Cf. for instance Schelling: Ideas toward a Philosophy of Nature, Part 1, 2nd edition, pp. 8 ff.) As for this latter question, the ordinary physiological, anthropological, or psychological modes of explanation are known; but for the Thinker it is equally known that precisely what is to be explained is not explained by them. If, for instance, a nerve-tension (according to David Hartley, vibrations of the infinitely small medullary particles, first in the nerves, then in the brain; something similar according to Priestley, Bonnet, and other Materialists) — produced by the impression from without (already this representation and phrase of impressions received from without is unphilosophical, even though it occurs frequently already in some of the Ancients), or more precisely through the image in the eye, is stated as the cause (more correctly, the exciting cause) of the representation in the Soul: we have still gained nothing more than a tensed nerve, from which the leap to the Soul's inner awareness and Consciousness — to the actual representation — is still just as great as before, without the interposed intermediate term of nerve-tension. One must, however, regard it already as a special concession in this mechanical mode of explanation that the external object — without itself contributing anything further to the matter than its mere existence (unless one wishes to assume with Epicurus an invisible efflux of images or atoms) — nevertheless at least produces a nerve-tension; whereas in reflecting glass, metal, or water nothing of the kind takes place. In that assumption there is therefore also the self-activity of the living — or rather the whole matter to be explained: seeing, representing, etc. — already presupposed; and the interposed intermediate term of the explanation — which therefore explains nothing — would be a mere medium, active or helpful in the matter only to the extent that the Soul — which is in any case already in itself determined to sense, intuit, represent — also in the individual case already has, does, or is what is being asked about; whereby the medium could in any case also be entirely absent or could be another, as indeed the Soul, already according to experience, is not unalterably bound to its sense-organs or fixed in them for its external sensations. If, as noted above, new-born infants with open and healthy eyes nevertheless see nothing so long as they do not distinguish — and consequently also so long as they have no Consciousness of an object (state of mere pure seeing, still equal to not-seeing, the developmental period in which only the habituation of the eye to general, still undistinguished light, or the subjective generation of light, falls) — what then does nerve-tension accomplish here, which here, without doubt, as conditioned by the impression from without, is presumably also present? Such reflections have presumably also been made repeatedly elsewhere; it seems, however, that just now — when Anthropology and Psychology, to the detriment of a thorough philosophical Knowing, again stand in special esteem — such attempted explanations together with other opinions about the Soul are the order of the day of philosophy or un-philosophy. What then is, or how does there arise, representing, Knowing — in general a Consciousness of external objects? The answer is that the matter is an incomprehensible secret — at least for those who, since only one who knows can know what Knowing is, wish to apply something quite other than Knowing itself to the solution of the riddle. And in truth, one must either recognize and comprehend that and how the Soul — once it has become I, at which stage Spirit has already left itself behind as Soul or as natural determinateness — is Subject for itself, and as such is necessarily determined and open against an other and toward an other (object in general), but is simultaneously the negative power that closes itself with itself in this other and thereby in its own negation, and also again becomes the identity therewith that it is (see Hegel, loc. cit., p. 222): or one must renounce the recognizing and comprehending of what Soul, I, Consciousness, and Knowing — then Spirit and spiritual activity in general — are, and at least not touch and afflict the secret with those non-explaining explanations. Before the Soul becomes an I — and therewith the realm of Consciousness opens up — quite a lot precedes it, namely itself properly, Spirit as Soul, i.e. as natural determinateness. But the scientific development thereof belongs neither here nor in Logic, but in Psychology, or rather Anthropology — and this, however, in the doctrine of real Spirit, which comes only after the science of Nature; something more detailed about it is found below in the third section, from the higher standpoint there attained. Why there is in general an other, a world, nature, objects — this question, however understood, also cannot find its resolution at least at the beginning of philosophy, and must rather produce itself in its true significance at its proper place. For Consciousness — for finite Spirit in general — there is a Nature, at least an other that is not at first itself; for infinite Spirit there is no other, or it is freely released as other by Spirit itself.

§ 11. Since I in Consciousness has its object directly over against it, this is a Being for I — or rather, Knowing itself is this Being of an object for I. (Or expressed more speculatively: I is itself the Being of an object for it.) Through this Knowing, I finds itself immediately determined — or it has certainty, i.e. the Knowing of the object, taken up into I, belongs to it just as much as I belongs to itself, and is immediately its own determinateness, which it acknowledges as its own, and which is just as much as I itself.

Anmerkung. Instead of "for it" — namely for I (an expression whose brevity, like that of many others in philosophical language, will readily be permitted) — one could in § 11 also say: for itself; only that this would stand here in a different sense from that which "for itself" — being-for-itself — otherwise has. Moreover, the proposition could also be expressed in the first person, where it reads as follows: I am myself the Being of an object for me; this Being is "my" determinateness, "my" Knowing. If one wishes to call the self-certainty of I — like its certainty of an object — a fact of Consciousness, because this name has at present attained great currency and significance in much that calls itself philosophy: there is not much to object to against the mere name; only it is to be observed that with such facts not much is yet gained, since others follow that do not leave the first as they were. One must then at least concede to the further and deeper cognitions — under which the so-called facts of Consciousness often shrink to something quite meagre — the right to be called higher or also deeper facts of Consciousness.

§ 12. As long, however, as Knowing is not yet itself in agreement with or equal to the object, certainty is not yet Truth. Between the first immediate certainty and Truth — which is certainty consolidated at a higher stage (as it may at least be provisionally determined here) — stands uncertainty or doubt.

In doubt, Consciousness — not trusting itself — goes beyond its first immediate certainty and the object thereof, is divided against itself, and has in its Knowing a non-being or otherness of the object, and only from this does it return to unity with the object and with itself. This movement of Consciousness (considered here only in general or according to its form, and so that doubt does not yet lead to a change in Knowing within the object itself) thus runs through these three moments: 1) immediate certainty — the immediate Being of the object; 2) negation of certainty — the non-being or otherness of the object; 3) return to the first unity of certainty, but now as a certainty mediated through negation — i.e. Truth. (Subjective Truth, holding-for-true.) 1) Something is so; 2) no, it is not so; 3) yes, it is so after all.

Anmerkung. Where others (e.g. Herbart) make the arousal of doubt into the necessary beginning of philosophical Thinking and for this purpose make use of propositions of scepticism — especially the modern — the nature of doubt itself has here been considered instead, and this consideration has been preferred precisely because thereby a cognition is already gained at once — even if not yet a significant one — which everyone finds confirmed in his own Consciousness when he wishes to attend closely to the procedure of Consciousness when it doubts, and to distinguish the moments of its movement occurring therein. Those sceptical propositions, however — when left without resolution just as given, and allowing even doubt to remain at doubt — afford no cognition, and the beginner will scarcely know much what to do with them, perhaps not even allowing himself thereby to be moved to a proper heartfelt and thorough doubting — as he should according to the prescription — or even to a despairing.

To this is added that, in order to make Hume's scepticism comprehensible and intelligible to the beginner, one must first transpose him back to the standpoint of the common world-view and the Lockean Empiricism presupposed thereby — which in our time has greater difficulties than some perhaps may believe, since our studying youth is by the immediate formation of our age already naturally beyond it, and lives already far too much in the element of Thinking and Spirit for the thoughtless surrender to mere experience — combined nevertheless with the violent effort of attention in the isolated and singular apprehension of individual temporal and spatial Appearances — to afford it an adequate satisfaction. To be sure, that is not what is aimed at in the first place; but the painful feeling aroused — or that is to be aroused — which consists in the fact that doubt and the division of Consciousness is held fast as something rigid and unmoved, appears precisely for this reason, also psychologically, as something purposeless, whereby only the teacher — who knows the matter better from the outset — reserves for himself the pleasure of redeeming his students from doubt at another time with victorious truth in the decision, and thereby also bringing joy to them. If, however, the Pyrrhonian propositions of the oldest scepticism — the ten tropes directed against the truth of sensory perception, which Sextus Empiricus (Pyrrhoniae Hypotyposes I, 14, 38) reduced to three — are easily comprehensible even for the beginner because he can be referred to his daily experience concerning the uncertainty and inequality of sensory perception: yet the mere historical knowledge of them yields little gain, and for actual cognition none at all, unless one wishes to lead them through a further dialectical development of the moments contained in them beyond the rigid and dead fact of mere differential perception, and to genuine gain of a cognition for Consciousness in its experience over itself. From this side, however, dialectical developments and exercises in Thinking in general — carried out on the nearest objects and commonly occurring propositions and principles of theoretical and practical Consciousness, which would already be expedient in schools — would be far better preparations and preliminary exercises for the beginner; and if there should be a lack of the requisite guidance by a teacher of perfected formation (for whoever does not have it corrupts more than he advances), one who has read a few of the easier Platonic dialogues thoroughly and with close attention to the course of the conversation and the development of the subject-matter already has a very good preliminary exercise. Of a different kind and incomparably deeper content than Hume's scepticism are the five (or also seven) later tropes of the ancient scepticism directed against Thought itself and all Dogmatism in general. (Sext. Emp., loc. cit., chap. 15.) They presuppose, however, knowledge of the dogmatic philosophy they destroy and annihilate. But just as this sceptical Consciousness itself in its negative position — in which it was not merely a doubting deliberation for which something might still become truth, but rather allowed no cognition whatsoever as Truth in a decided manner, allowing everything only as Appearance and semblance — was thereby altogether given over to external contingency, from which it had first to expect the dogmatic material in order to return it destroyed, and in this shape itself constitutes a particular Appearance in the series of shapes of Consciousness (see Hegel's Phenomenology, pp. 134 ff.): so also in all the sceptical propositions mentioned above that are to be a necessary beginning of philosophical Thinking, precisely the necessity of the beginning is to be missed — (in the dialectical development of a proposition taken up even contingently, at least the progression is something necessary) — and rather the contingent and unconnected is the deficiency from which this beginning, and still more the progression — which is actually entirely lacking — suffers.

While, therefore, in the present Propaedeutic a progressive development of Consciousness is furnished from experience to experience, from cognition to cognition, proceeding rather in necessary inner connection from the concept of Consciousness, the essential content of those doubts also falls of itself into this progression, and is there considered according to its necessity as well as its Truth; whereby also the likewise necessary contradictions — in whose discovery and demonstration the force and strength of ancient scepticism consisted — are indeed not "improved" through Thinking, but are nevertheless brought to Reason and unity. Concerning the true essence of ancient scepticism, as well as concerning the quite modern — but entirely descended to the most common Dogmatism — scepticism calling itself such, cf. especially the treatise: Relation of Scepticism to Philosophy etc. (in Schelling's and Hegel's Critical Journal of Philosophy, Vol. I, No. 2, pp. 1–74), in which there is to be found an equally thorough instruction as thorough correction of the most recent scepticism.

§ 13. Since what holds for Consciousness as true is taken to be what the object is in-itself, while this is still distinguished from what it is for Consciousness — i.e. from the Knowing and Cognizing of the object — the representation readily arises at this distinction that the True or the being-in-itself of the object and Cognizing, each for itself and separate from the other, are both absolutely divided from each other as if by a boundary falling between them. And since it seems uncertain whether Cognizing — when it goes directly to the matter itself, i.e. when it wishes to cognize — can actually take possession of what is in-itself, and might perhaps seize error instead of Truth: the question arises whether Cognizing itself should not first be examined and investigated as to its capacity and range, or its mode of operation — in order thereby, through addition or subtraction, through improvement and correction of what Cognizing does to the matter, to be able to derive the True itself all the more purely.

Anmerkung. However much modern philosophy has occupied itself with a preliminary investigation and examination of the cognitive faculty in relation to the question whether it possesses at all the capacity or ability to grasp Truth or what is in-itself — and Kant, for example, despite the fact that in the Categories he vindicated for the cognizing I itself the necessary forms of all Cognition, nevertheless through a strange misunderstanding cast Truth and the in-itself into an unknowable beyond, because it is merely something subjective that I through its Cognizing does to the material — yet it at first brings little gain to the beginner to be long entertained with such philosophical doubts and the incorrect representations and presuppositions on which they rest. It must immediately strike one that a critical investigation of the cognitive faculty itself cannot be undertaken without Cognizing — indeed, the very assumption of a cognitive faculty before any Cognition has taken place must collapse in itself. It is therefore in this and the following — regarding whose content the more detailed treatment may be compared in Hegel's Phenomenology, pp. 1 ff. — that the matter has been treated only in a more cursory manner, particularly since, as already noted above in the remark to § 9, the following exposition itself contains a refutation of such views. Still coarser and truly unworthy of German philosophy are the views — in part bordering on Materialism — of some more recent thinkers who make Thinking and Cognizing dependent upon all manner of particular psychological and anthropological conditions, leaving for him who, amid belief in such limitations, yet looks around for somewhat more comfort and salvation for his Soul, nothing remaining but to elevate and edify himself with all manner of amiable sentiment and beautiful presentiments. Yet all such endeavours and investigations seem to have as their basis the view or presentiment that in the subject itself the treasure must lie concealed; only where it is to be sought and raised is the question.

§ 14. Yet, however much a prior examination of the cognitive faculty — in relation to doubts of this kind — commends itself at first sight as necessary and worthy of approval, these misgivings and doubts, when subjected to their own closer consideration and examination — to which they themselves must first be submitted — show themselves to be as little something resting on the Truth of the matter and advancing Cognizing itself. Whether Cognizing is thereby taken as an active instrument or as a merely passive medium: this is already an arbitrary presupposition and assumption that brings and introduces into the matter something foreign from without — which neither appears on its own justified nor proves its applicability upon closer consideration — and changes the matter rather than leaving it as it immediately is.

Equally arbitrary and blindly seized is the assumption and representation that — in the above question — separates and distinguishes us from our Cognizing; and as entirely unjustified and presupposing or importing everything that would first have to be ascertained appears the presupposition that — in complete separation — first places our Cognizing on one side, the True or Absolute on the other, and then asks how both come together: a presupposition that becomes all the more untrue when it simultaneously allows our Cognizing — which, according to this assumption, for itself is not yet a Cognizing of the True, or is first to wander about outside of the True and Absolute — to pass nevertheless as something true and real and valid for itself.

Anmerkung. That the indicated presuppositions — however natural they may appear to representation, or to Consciousness philosophizing from its standpoint — are yet only presuppositions that import something foreign to the matter and immediately assume something first to be demonstrated, and therefore properly need only to be negated: this emerges upon some closer consideration. If Cognizing is compared, for example, with seeing, there may indeed be bleary, short-sighted, or otherwise defective eyes through which seeing is particularly conditioned and modified for an individual — but for seeing in general and for healthy eyes it would surely pass as a strange notion to find a special investigation and examination of the faculty of sight necessary as to whether it is really in a condition to perceive the shape and colour of external objects, while seeing itself immediately sees them and adheres to seeing. If, however, a further brooding wished to make it doubtful whether objects are also really constituted as they are seen — whether the round, for example, were not angular, the green not rather red — there would nevertheless remain not merely shape and colour in general, but also the mutually firmly determined and delimited distinctions or kinds thereof, which subsist for themselves independently; so that, if indeed a hidden world behind what we see wished to play a deceptive game with us and to hold the things before us outwardly in a different shape and colour from what they have within it, we would firstly know nothing of such a world (except what of it falls to our own Knowing — i.e. here our own fabrication), and secondly would also have no need whatsoever to take notice of it, since even in that secretive and malicious world round would have to remain round and green remain green. The application to Cognizing will easily be made — yet with the difference that, even if Cognizing goes immediately to the matter itself, it and its progression can only be something mediated; whereas, on the other hand, if many an untruth is also still to be removed before recognition of Truth, yet Cognizing — as was already noted above in the remark to § 9 at the end — carries within itself and has already within itself the criterion of its Truth.

§ 15. Since therefore every mere presupposition that deals with a True subsisting for itself and outside of Cognizing is here first set aside as such, we have rather to occupy ourselves — as with the immediate subject-matter we seek — with the Truth of our Knowing and Cognizing itself, and to assure ourselves of this through closer consideration and investigation. If, however, our Knowing and Cognizing is a true one, it follows of itself that it is also a Knowing and Cognizing of the True — for in the contrary case it would rather be an untrue one.

Anmerkung. It might seem as though the investigation demanded in § 15 — through which we are to assure ourselves of the Truth of our Knowing and Cognizing — were nothing different from the prior examination of the cognitive faculty previously rejected on account of its presuppositions as erroneous; but the progression will make it recognizable that the investigation here undertaken and the development given has an entirely different direction, and that a very essential difference enters — in that, to indicate it provisionally, in the present investigation: 1) without any of the presuppositions mentioned in the preceding, only Consciousness itself is first considered and developed analytically according to its necessary and universal concept; 2) this consideration of Consciousness takes it up only within itself and in its own determinatenesses, and — without regard to an object present outside Consciousness and Cognizing, or to a True subsisting for itself — directs the question rather to whether the determinateness of Consciousness itself, or what it knows and what constitutes its determinate content, has Truth in and for itself; and 3) in the following section — which shows the Consciousness of Appearance in the necessary development through its various stages — this particular Consciousness itself, in its determinate comportment and Knowing in relation to the object of which it knows and how it knows of it, is taken up and examined as to the Truth that belongs to its Knowing thereby — not to the object — in and for itself. For the elucidation of the matter the following may provisionally serve. According to Kant, as is well known, the Categories or pure concepts of the understanding of quantity, quality, etc. are original and necessary determinations or rather determinatenesses (forms) that I itself has within it, and according to which it receives, judges, and determines the given material. How I in its unity comes to this multiplicity of determinatenesses or forms — or how this multiplicity is contained in the unity, and how these determinatenesses cohere with each other in I — Kant has not demonstrated; their presence as well as their number is merely taken from experience and ordinary Logic. (The understanding, he says, is completely exhausted by these functions, and its faculty thereby entirely measured out.) Since inner necessity and a deduction thereof is lacking — (Kant's so-called transcendental deduction of the pure concepts of the understanding has to do with something quite other than the necessity here demanded, and seeks only to establish in general the necessity of the assumption of such concepts a priori) — everything may already on that account be subjected to doubt, and it is by no means brought to insight why precisely only these and no other concepts should pertain to pure understanding, or "belong in the household register of the understanding": since Kant — who has criticized Aristotle for having empirically gathered up his Categories — has equally failed to demonstrate it, and gives just as little information about it as about how I in the unity of its Consciousness comes to distinguish and split itself into so many diverse and unequal forms of its pure apperception — such as quality, quantity, etc. But let these pure concepts in any case be original determinatenesses of I just as well as the innate concepts and necessary truths of Reason of which Leibniz speaks: here at first — to illustrate the above by an example — our consideration and question would not be directed to how I comes to such determinatenesses (for they are given with I itself), nor why it proceeds according to them in its pure activity (for the activity of I would in general be no other than only this one equal to itself), casting itself now into this, now into that form, nor how this multiplicity can find room in I without prejudice to its unity, and how it coheres with I itself as well as among themselves; but rather we would wish to know what content I has — when it has such a determinateness, e.g. quantity, within it, and thereby itself finds itself determined in a particular way — that must therefore also be a determinate one, which I for that very reason must also be able to distinguish (objectify) from itself or from its universality, just as it has to distinguish it from other of its pure determinatenesses, e.g. from quality. When this now comes about, and the pure content of the pure concept of the understanding is thereby won, I will without doubt not merely know of this content in general, but will therein also have within itself an object — from which, by virtue of its delimitation and distinction, it must also be able to state what it has in it, or what it is. It emerges that this content — grasped in its purity and Truth — can be nothing other than the pure determination of Thinking, or — if one already wishes to call this a concept — the pure concept of quantity, of quality, etc. itself, or what they are in and for themselves: concerning which the more detailed treatment belongs in Logic.
