From stating such a determinate concept of the individual pure concepts of the understanding, Kant has likewise abstained, treating these stem-concepts as something given and merely stopping with the fact that I in the pure activity of its relation to objects proceeds according to these forms. Even now those who know nothing determinate to state concerning quality, quantity, and other pure determinations of Being will perhaps seek a way out by calling them simple concepts, or also — using a favoured expression — simply allowing them to hold as facts of Consciousness without further ado. Our question, however, remains here entirely the same. For granted that something is such a fact, we wish to know — just as before with the Categories — what determinate content Consciousness has in it; and we consider that Consciousness for which its facts suffice simply as such, itself in what it thereby is, and in the limitation of its standpoint that it has thereby given itself, into the bargain. (Cf. Anmerkung 1 to § 6, and Anmerkung to § 11.)

§ 16. As certain, however, as it is in Consciousness that the knowing I is certain of itself, and has certainty of an other thereby — in that it knows the Knowing of it as its own determinateness (§ 11) — so certain is it also for Consciousness that everything we call an object, of which we know, is at first only our Knowing of it. Whether the object distinguished from Consciousness is also present outside Knowing: the question of whether it is or not does not concern us here at first; at first this much only is certain, that it is present in Knowing, and Consciousness must acknowledge this Being of the object for it — whereby it is precisely its Knowing — as its own determinateness.

Anmerkung. Of the presence of external objects outside itself, the individual Consciousness has the same immediate certainty with which it knows of itself and of an other in general. This certainty lies for it in its thorough dependence upon these objects and the necessity thereby imposed upon it, and in the fact that it still distinguishes the images and representations it has of them within itself from the objects themselves, and — while conscious of its freedom over the former — is unable with all power and effort to change anything in the latter and their found order and determinateness. To common Idealism — which makes objects into mere representations — ordinary and natural Consciousness therefore rightly objects: it is conscious of its freedom and does not wish to let itself be made into what belongs only to things in unconscious necessity. But it is also in itself something deficient, inasmuch as it not only involves itself in strange incongruities and contradictions, but through it not the slightest is gained for actual Cognition. For whether the outer world, as it appears, is truly outside me, or whether — according to the common Idealist representation — I merely carry it in my head or in my intuition, and wherever it may be placed and into whatever space: it is all the same, when here as there the ordinary contemplation of things and the common world-view remains unmoved. Not a step further has been taken toward the question of what things are in and for themselves, or what they are in essence and Truth — or in other words, through this Idealism not the slightest in things is comprehended; rather the same demand remains standing that representation is to be abandoned and is to transform itself into a Knowing. But precisely on account of this indifference of mere representation concerning external things, it has been reminded in § 16 — and will have to be repeatedly reminded — that at first nothing depends for us on whether the known object is also present outside our Knowing, since we have rather only to occupy ourselves with whether our Knowing of an object has Truth in itself or not. If we succeed herewith in satisfying ourselves with our Knowing, and no longer find it necessary to alter it or to go beyond it from any side, we shall be content therewith, and shall then also know what is involved with the object. That Consciousness, however, for which immediate sensory certainty holds as its Truth, or the object thereof as the essence, is itself considered below according to its Truth — as it is the first stage and shape of appearing Consciousness.

§ 17. This determinateness constitutes for Consciousness simultaneously the Truth and the essence of the object; it is only that as which it is known. Whatever particular determinations (moments, marks, qualities) the object may have — which Consciousness finds and distinguishes in it — the Being of the latter is here equal to the Knowing of it.

If Consciousness finds itself not satisfied in its Knowing of the object — or in what it is for it — or, what amounts to the same thing, if the determinateness in which it knows it appears to it insufficient, not reaching the Being and essence of the object (or also thus: if it attributes to the object a Being still unknown to it, which has not yet become a Being for it): if it consequently goes beyond this its determinateness — which first and hitherto held as the object for it, and in which its Knowing was equal to the Being of the object — then it will also change its Knowing accordingly, or rather has already changed it — precisely in and with this going-beyond.

Anmerkung. The Being of the Object in Consciousness for Consciousness is the Knowledge of it. This Being distinguishes Consciousness within itself from itself, whereby it becomes something objective; yet this is at the same time its own Determinacy. How Consciousness arrives at this, and however one may psychologically name or explain the genesis of a determinate Object for it, is here entirely indifferent. In so far as Consciousness immediately receives such a Determinacy within itself, and is not itself conscious of its production, it comports itself in any case passively, or it finds itself, as one has called it, affected. What is more closely to be kept in view, however, is the universal What — the content of Determinacy in general, or, if one will so call it, the matter of determinateness corresponding to Knowledge — and its relation to the subject of Knowledge. According to this, however, the Object (cf. above what has already been said thereon in the Anmerkung to § 9) is absolutely nothing other than that as which it is known, or as Consciousness has apprehended it, or — to employ this expression already here — as it appears to the latter within itself. To this extent the Being of the Object is wholly equal to the Knowledge of it, or in the Determinacy of Consciousness the knowing and the known are the same. What difference enters between them — which, as the difference of the two, is precisely the unrest and the inner impulse for the dialectical advance toward real and complete equality or toward Truth — the further development makes manifest.

Consciousness, however, goes beyond its Determinacy — in which the Object first is for it — of itself, and thereby alters its Knowledge of the Object. Both are one and the same; with the one the other has already occurred. For even if in such an advance no new positive side or Determinacy were won in the Object — if consequently the advance were still only a pure, merely negating advance (doubt, cf. above § 12) — so that, apart from the still-valid or rather already no-longer-valid (negated) Determinacy which first constituted the Object, either no further Knowledge of the Object were present at all, or the Object were attributed by Consciousness with yet another Being unknown to it (a Being-in-itself), which for that very reason would be no Being for it, or Being only as non-Being for it: yet through this pure negation Knowledge has already become other, since now to the Determinacy of this Knowledge there belongs not merely the partially still-valid or also wholly sublated first Being of the Object for Consciousness, but this negation itself, through which a Being of the Object as non-Being for Consciousness — and therewith also the non-Being itself as a Being for it, or as something known — is present. Even the mere negation of the Determinacy first holding as the essence of the Object would have that sublated Determinacy itself — yet as sublated — as its content, and would thus not be an empty Nothing, but rather, as the Nothing of a Determinacy, itself a determinate — that is, content-bearing and therein distinguishable from others — Nothing. It may also be remarked that Consciousness, in knowing any of its Determinacies as a Determinacy, has thereby already gone beyond it, because it is a Determinacy — whether by itself alone or alongside other Determinacies — for such a Determinacy is not equal to the universality of the knowing I, or I does not recognize its universal nature therein. Moreover, even the beginner in philosophy ought not to neglect these pure movements of Consciousness and the pure, abstract content thereof, which dialectic itself knows how to win in negation.

§ 18. Thereby, however, the Object has necessarily and immediately likewise changed with respect to that as which it previously held for Consciousness; it is that which Consciousness now knows it as.

Were it to fail in this — in its present Determinacy, which constitutes both its Knowledge and equally its Being for Consciousness — again to satisfy Consciousness, or, which is the same thing, were it again to appear untrue to it, then Consciousness would once more alter its Knowledge; yet therewith the Object would not remain the previous one, but would become another or altered one, and so forth. In every case the Object itself, in the Determinacy of its Being for Consciousness, alters simultaneously with Knowledge.

Anmerkung. As little as it is possible for any Consciousness to have its Object otherwise than as it and what it knows of it — or otherwise than as the Being of the Object for Consciousness is constituted — (and that to the content of the known there belongs also negation, and that, as Determinacy thereof, this equally constitutes the Determinacy of the Object itself for Consciousness, has been shown in the Anmerkung to the preceding section): just as certainly does there follow therefrom the proposition set forth in §, that with an alteration of the Knowledge of the Object, the Object also becomes another for Consciousness; for both are the same.

For the sake of greater clarity, in view of the exposition already given, it will no longer be necessary first to remove the common representation which, when Objects are spoken of, has to do only with the solid and palpable nature of external things — with the Objects of sensuous Certainty in general, the lowest class of all — and repeatedly to recall that our consideration concerns itself only with that which, within Consciousness, as distinguished from it in its Determinacy, is or becomes Object for it — that is, in general the known, at a higher level the recognized and thought.

§ 19. — Or, let us call that Being or essence of the Object — as the Certainty of Consciousness has first immediately apprehended it — that which it is in itself; and if Consciousness, altering its Knowledge of it, goes beyond this Being-in-itself of the Object — (with which movement, according to § 18, the Object itself no longer remains as it was before, but becomes another, namely as Consciousness now knows it): then, inasmuch as it can no longer hold fast as the in-itself that which has become untruth for it, it likewise ceases to regard its first Knowledge as a true one; it recognizes that that in-itself is not truly in itself, but held as such only for this Consciousness as such. With this recognition and distinction, Consciousness has made an experience within itself, in which that which was first for it the in-itself of the Object presents itself as a mere Appearance (τὸ φαινόμενον, distinguished from the Object in itself and the νοούμενον) — and indeed as an Appearance both of Knowledge (appearing Knowledge) and of the Object known only in this manner.

Anmerkung. It is impossible that a Truth or view already perished for Consciousness itself should be held any longer by it as the essence and Truth of the Object; from which it follows in turn that it is a matter of such Knowledge as has its Certainty and Truth within itself in such a manner that Consciousness has no further need to go beyond it from any side. Only in the element of such Knowledge can real Science move — primarily, for example, Logic — although, as will be shown there, it moves therein in such a manner that its Objects, the pure essences and Concepts, do not remain something unaltered or immovably fixed — as the Understanding, fixing them one-sidedly, would make of them — but equally have their pure dialectic within them; yet it is nonetheless the form of pure Knowledge, or pure Certainty, that uninterruptedly accompanies these movements and wherein they immediately have their Truth; and this pure movement too arrives at last in its absolute rest — namely in the Idea, where it returns to its beginning and the circle is closed. Experience is here taken in the manner indicated in §, according to that determinate sense in which Consciousness makes it in and over itself, when that which had held as the essence of the Object for it presents itself to it only as an Appearance of its Knowledge, or as a Being that was only so for it. Regarding the significance of the word experience in the ordinary sense, usage does not appear to remain consistent. The Certainty from a single act of sensuous intuition, or a single perception, does not yet deserve to be called experience; nor is what is ordinarily understood thereby the connection of a manifold in a determinate relation, and that cognition which draws its Certainty from the self-identical repetition of the connected. One sees the reproach made here and there against modern philosophy that it treats experience with contempt. A strange reproach to be made against Science! It lets experience hold as what it is, and comprehends it. It may surely be accepted as a thing long settled that in experience no universality and necessity is contained; and that Science — which first becomes Science precisely through the universality and necessity of its cognition — should seek these essential requirements from experiential cognition? It may occur in practical and empirical sciences that one must still make do with many mere experiential cognitions, because the necessary connection of what is given through experience has not yet been scientifically penetrated and recognized; yet for that very reason such a science will also be the more imperfect as a science. But if experience is truly pure experience, and what is or what occurs is apprehended purely — that is, without a particular subjective addition — and likewise Science is genuine Science, then one must on the contrary assert that both — however different in the ground of cognition — are by no means opposed to one another in the things themselves, and cannot contradict one another either; and that Science is confirmed by experience, while experience — which is blind on its own — is first made seeing through Science. To this place also belongs the frequently misconstrued opposition of theory and practice, according to which one assumes that something might well be good in theory but have no applicability for practice. If this latter is truly the case, this is likewise a proof that the theory which is meant to relate to a practice is worthless. But it appears that something else lies at the root of the reproach mentioned above. For even ordinary experience is by no means something merely immediate which would only be sensorially intuited or perceived, but already belongs itself to intellectual activity which goes beyond the immediate and arrives, by judging or inferring, at an abstractly comparative-universal content separated from it — which constitutes precisely the gain of experience — as Kant himself begins his Introduction to the Critique of Pure Reason immediately by calling experience the first product of the Understanding, and proceeds further through the application of his Categories to the material of experience given through intuition, or to Appearances, to arrive even at an objective, universally valid, and necessary synthetic experiential cognition. In such a manner there has formed itself a manifold — to which belong also the so-called facts of the empirical Consciousness — that counts as experience, and at which common thinking, without further examination and investigation, rests as at an ultimate; one did not leave things as they were, and the semblance of a universally valid and necessary subsistence was illicitly won for propositions and assertions that rest as little on pure experience as on pure scientific cognition; indeed, mere hypotheses — especially in natural science — have mingled themselves among the pure facts to such a degree and become so firmly established in the opinion of their truth and validity that the one goes and counts for the other, and they can scarcely any longer be distinguished from one another. It is easily recognizable that what is blameworthy in this procedure pertains not to the pure facts themselves or to real experience, but only to the deficient intellectual manner and mode of their apprehension and treatment, and to the inadequate form of reflection with which one seeks to give to the content of experience the shape of the thought. Now since modern philosophy has purified itself of this unhappy intermediate substance and intermediate knowledge, has gone beyond the deficient Empiricism as beyond mere reflection-universalities, and Hegelian philosophy in particular — which grants and lets pass nothing, and everywhere asks for the rationality not merely of the form but of the content, and has therefore submitted to scrutiny and sharp criticism in this regard, i.e. according to its inner rationality, everything at which one had previously rested without further examination — the content of the Categories as well as the content of that which counted as experience and therewith as an ultimate: so may this appear to those whose experience is of the described kind as if everything possible and objectionable were being said of experience in general. Let one see and examine which among the philosophical systems put forward in modern times stands in greatest accord with experience and with actuality, and whether it is not precisely the Hegelian — for that very reason and in that very degree in which its Logic and Metaphysics are deeper and more thorough and more scientific than that which previously bore that name, or is otherwise so called.

§ 20. What is contained and present in this movement and transformation taking place within Consciousness consists in the following moments: 1) the (immediate) Knowledge of an Object, or the Being of the Object for Consciousness in the Determinacy of Being-in-itself or of Truth; 2) the becoming of this Being-in-itself into the mere Being thereof for Consciousness (into mere Appearance), or the Knowledge that the Being or essence of the Object — as it first was in Consciousness — is not the in-itself thereof, but held as such only for this Consciousness (the experience); 3) the transformation of Consciousness as well as of its Object already thereby entered. Inasmuch as the Object has thereby become another, it consists at first only in this: that its initial in-itself, its Truth, is now known only as Appearance; this constitutes its present Determinacy and new form; this is now its Truth. —

Anmerkung. The Truth of Knowledge consists here, accordingly, in the gained insight that the Knowledge and the known of the Object is only an Appearance in Consciousness. Before this recognition, the known held for Consciousness as that which the Object is in itself. If we reflect herewith provisionally on what is called Appearance, it presents itself as something that sublates itself — negates itself — precisely because it is only Appearance; as an Untruth at which Consciousness cannot remain, but is compelled to proceed further. Also in a somewhat different sense — in which further below, as also in Logic in the doctrine of essence, Appearance will be spoken of — it has this negative within itself, in so far as it is not an independent being for itself, but points beyond itself toward an Other, a non-appearing, as its Truth.

Different from this is that which Kant understood by Appearances, which in his account relate only to external Objects and are nothing other than the subjective empirical mode of intuiting them. Since, according to his view, by virtue of the nature of sensibility — which receives the impressions from external Objects (receptivity) and modifies them according to its subjective mode — Appearances can be constituted in no way other than as they present themselves to the intuiting subject: they are therefore in truth only relatively Appearances, and for the universal human subject rather self-subsisting and necessary truths of the same. From this standpoint it then lies near at hand to investigate psychologically and anthropologically the organization of the subject more closely, in order at least somehow to discover why things appear to us in just this way and not otherwise, and to find some consolation and compensation at least in the explaining, amid the one-for-all accepted subjective limitation. But since the faculty of thinking is also something present and given in experience, it does not even require a great expenditure of it to see that in this philosophizing not only is nothing gained, but the main point is missed. The entire sensible world as it presents itself to us is to be a mere Appearance. Be it so; but what do we then know, when we know nothing further than this? Where with this has even the slightest cognition been won? What rational content is there in the knowing of this given, and in it itself, whether it be called Appearance or Actual? For if the sensible world as it once gives itself to us is in any case left as it is, he who takes it only as Appearance sees no less and no more than he who believes that things are also truly so; in neither case has a single step further been taken toward a recognizing and knowing of it. (Cf. Anmerkung to § 16.) But that is also not what we should do; rather, one demands that we remain with this restricted knowledge — that the things with which we have to do are only Appearances — and be done with everything at once; for what things are in themselves, we can never in all eternity discover through either experience or our thinking. Now, just as was recalled above in the Anmerkung to § 15 regarding the Categories — that one could by no means remain with the claim that the activity of the Understanding in its relation to the empirical material of intuition merely proceeds according to these forms as given and immediately present in the I, but that one must also ask what they are in themselves, or what pure and rational content they have in them: so also does there lie nearest at hand to the last-named result of the Critique of Pure Reason (although here we are still far from the point where pure Reason is at issue, the question initially concerning only the faculty of representation — as Reinhold also took it — or the sensuous Consciousness, and then Consciousness in general) the further question of what, or what universal content, is given in and with the Appearances; or, in other words, what is to be undertaken is a criticism in this sense — not of pure Reason, but for the present only of the Appearances themselves, i.e., of what is given with the Appearance in Consciousness and of the Knowledge of Appearance in general: which is the task of this and still more of the following section. For it is surely something very simple and natural — and especially to be demanded of philosophy — to consider something once given, however and through whatever it may be given, according to its own and universal content, and in such a manner to arrive at further determinations regarding it, wherever they may lead. The same holds also of the thing-in-itself already mentioned earlier, which Kant, as it were by way of comfort and compensation for the fact that we have only to do with Appearances, left still subsisting in the objective world over against these — but unhappily at the same time took away from us all hope (for something other than an unphilosophical mere dictum, proceeding from a preconceived fundamental error, one will not readily discover therein) that we shall ever learn anything more definite of it, because it is not to be encountered in any experience. All the sublime content which lies in the thought of the Categories in and for themselves — through which the Idea of Reason and of philosophy still shines forth, as in many other excellent thoughts and utterances of Kant's — the critical philosophy has annulled again with this result of the application of the Categories. While Kant, however, through that which in his philosophy is genuinely philosophy, retains also his great merit for it, the unphilosophy of his successors has, on the contrary, adhered to the flat doctrine that one cannot know what lies hidden behind and beneath the Appearances as to the highest truth, and greeted and proclaimed it — as a new dawn, or already as the finally-risen sun of the philosophical world — with jubilation; indeed even modern Skepticism so far forgot its kind that it made this doctrine its dogma, with which it sought to combat the rational truths of philosophy. But as for Kant himself, something truly singular befell him with this result of his Critique. If what things are in themselves is given in no experience, nor encountered therein: how did he then discover something about them? How does he come to know something of the existence of such things that are to remain unknown? How can he speak of them at all? But if assuming them was a necessity of his thinking (and only this can remain, if the positing of an unknowable thing-in-itself is not to remain a mere idle fancy): why did he not investigate this necessity — over which, as belonging to his inner experience, he must have had better command and knowledge — and pursue its ground more closely? Did he require, for the manifold of Appearance in order not to let it fall apart, a substrate, a binding unity: why did he not recognize as this necessary unity Thinking itself, in the same way as, according to his view, the manifold connected into a (formal) unity of Consciousness yields a Concept? Why did he not rather, like Kant's somewhat further-going adherent Jakob Sigismund Beck, take the thing-in-itself entirely into the subject as well, or even make it into a Category? He would at least — even at the risk of complete Idealism — have advanced one step further in Thinking, the gain of which, properly retained, could have led to still further ones; just as he himself, through his transcendental Object X — which cannot at all be separated from the sensible data — through the intelligible cause of Appearances and through his Noumenon, came upon the trace of the matter by just as much as he again moved away from it through these same things as mere thought-things and through an incomprehensible misunderstanding. Whether external things are in themselves such binding unities of the manifold within them, and thus correspond to their thought unity — this question might well for the present have remained unanswered, if only Thought for itself, according to its inner Truth, had first been brought into correctness. The mere abstraction, moreover, to which the thing-in-itself as dead ground or as form- and content-less unity of the manifold reduces itself, by no means needed to be represented to the world as something unknown of which we cannot know what it is; for how Thinking arrives at such an abstraction, but also, because it is only abstraction, itself sublates it again — this is, like many other forms and movements of Thinking, something very well known.

—

§ 21. This movement and transformation can repeat itself — or must rather repeat itself so long until the Object has determined itself to the point where Consciousness has no further need to go beyond it (cf. above §§ 3–9); but since each new form in which it appears to Consciousness is again a particular Determinacy thereof — whose content does not here yet come into consideration — and accordingly the course would each time be formally the same: so in the consideration of this course the sole essential thing for us here is the recognition of the moments each time necessarily contained within it, or of its essential form, as well as the recognition of the further determinations lying therein.

Anmerkung. This repetition of the same course of the matter is not — in order to forestall here at once a possible misunderstanding — to be conceived as if each individual Object, or every single Object, had in this manner to pass through its stations in the Knowledge Consciousness has of it. Such a mode of treatment would indeed also be possible, and for the contingently and empirically given treatment of any single Object or particular relation there can in general be no higher prescription — even in the practical sphere — than to take the matter purely objectively in all its sides as it is, and thus to determine what it is in and for itself: yet such a procedure also necessarily carries the consideration at once beyond the single Object, and transposes it into a universal domain in which its individuality becomes something insignificant. Thus from this mode of treatment there would at once result the same conclusion as when the Object is taken immediately in its universality — that is, for the present in the universality of what it is as a determinate Object for a determinate mode of Consciousness — as the development to be given here will show. Since, for example, immediate sensuous Certainty is the lowest level of Consciousness — with which, therefore, the beginning must also be made — any Object whatsoever with which it has to do, no matter which single or particular one, is thereby at once determined as a sensuous Object in general. Consciousness is dependent upon it in so far as it immediately receives it and finds itself determined by it; but on the other hand the Object is, by virtue of Consciousness's comporting itself toward it as sensuous Certainty, likewise determined — namely to be only a sensuous, empirically felt or intuited Object. And although for this mode of Consciousness it is essential to apprehend the Object in its most determinate individuality as this single one, yet this individuality itself is the same for each one, and is thus universal. But since what is at issue is the Truth of the sensuous Object as well as of the Knowledge of it, and the universal abiding content which would be the Truth in both, the further development of this relation will show what the situation is with this and with the Truth of sensuous Certainty in general. Another level of Consciousness is Perception. In perceiving, it comports itself differently toward the Object, and the Object differently toward it, than in the case of mere sensuous Certainty; the Object which is and in which is perceived is another than the Object of immediate Certainty — not in the sense that, for example, the same tree that was previously Object of the latter could not now also be an Object of Perception, but as an Object of Perception it is another and altered one, by virtue of the fact that the determinations constituting the content of Perception are entirely different in kind from those under which Consciousness first had it. Thereby Knowledge itself also receives another content and character; conversely, however, the Object is here also determined by Consciousness, in that it would present itself differently to the latter if the latter comported itself otherwise than as perceiving toward it. But the investigation pertains also here not to the single Object, but to the Object of Perception in general, and to the content and universal Truth of the perceiving Consciousness. In the same manner, at the following levels as well, each new relation of Consciousness to its Object can only be grasped in its universality.

§ 22. Here, however, is at the same time the point at which the most important distinction between natural and philosophically cultivated Consciousness enters. While the former, without knowing how, repeatedly alters its view and comes to apprehend its Object differently and differently, it may well come to pass that it knows thereof — often not even this — and even speaks of the alteration of its view; yet it does not come to reflect upon what has therein gone on within it, and to gain the insight that the Knowledge of an Object on each occasion also constitutes this very Object for Consciousness — (as for Consciousness in general nothing other than its Knowledge and the Determinacy of the Object present in this Knowledge is there) — and that through the alteration of Knowledge, the initially present Being-in-itself of the Object (in the assertion: Such and such is the Object or the matter), as well as this Knowledge itself, sinks to the level of mere Appearance.

Anmerkung. In the immediate manner in which natural Consciousness has to do with its Objects, it escapes it that the Object is conditioned by Knowledge in general, and more particularly by the mode and manner of knowing it. Since it does not reflect upon itself, nor distinguishes the various modes in which Consciousness comports itself, it supposes itself to have the Object immediately — while it has it only in its own manner — and does not know that the same necessarily changes and presents itself differently to it depending on whether it comports itself toward it as, for example, perceiving or understanding or rational Consciousness; still less does it know upon what these differences rest. — The various views of one and the same matter — which in its purely objective and absolute nature cannot be dependent upon such an external diversity of views — arise largely from the diversity of the particular forms of Consciousness that have fixed themselves for themselves into total modes of intuition. The altered views in the same subject are for natural Consciousness merely something that has occurred, something historical; the inner connection of the earlier with the later view and the inner necessity which allows the one to pass over into the other remains hidden from it. Natural Consciousness in general is submerged in its material, while the philosophical withdraws from it, and thereby apprehends and regards itself. It carries, to be sure, a manifold content of Knowledge, views, opinions, customs, and ethical principles within itself; but if in terms of this mere material it were as rich as the fully cultivated philosophical Consciousness, it would nonetheless — since it does not also have and know itself therein — have each of its Objects only incompletely, just as it in any case also has its manifold material and wealth without order. In this colorful abundance and manifoldness a rich natural Consciousness may be compared to a beautiful landscape in which the most manifold and diverse natural objects have their existence alongside one another. But just as Nature does not therewith also simultaneously reveal its necessity, its inner connection, its rank-ordering, and its higher or lower significance, so too from the wealth of Spirit, from its substantial content and essence, its various sides, modes, and forms — higher and lower, more significant and less significant — enter without necessity, order, and mutual relation into a natural life within natural Consciousness. What in ordinary life counts as culture is often nothing more than precisely this abundance and manifoldness of natural Consciousness when it has — to employ a fashionable phrase that arose some years ago — absorbed very much into itself, whereby immediate intelligence in judging and rationality in acting is often not even reckoned as part of culture. Moreover, Spirit can indeed emerge in individuals even to its most developed Appearance and highest form, yet this culture remain nonetheless only an immediate and natural life of the same.

§ 23. The philosophical Consciousness distinguishes itself from this natural Consciousness, therefore, first of all thereby (cf. § 8) that not only does it, in the Knowledge of an Object — which the latter has and knows only immediately — simultaneously also have and regard itself, and for it only the Object together with its knowing Consciousness constitutes the true whole Object; but that also Consciousness in general, in its Appearance and formation as well as in the inner necessity of its moments and their advance, becomes an Object of consideration for it.

Anmerkung. The true whole Object of philosophical consideration is 1) the otherwise-given Object and 2) the Consciousness for which it is and by which it is conditioned, taken simultaneously therewith. Some sensuous Object, for example, is perceived: ordinary Consciousness has here only the Object; we, however, bear in mind that it is conditioned only through our intuition for us, and the Knowledge of it through this intuition. Something is white, sweet, warm for empirical Certainty: the Truth of this Certainty and the whole of what is present is not the being-white, being-sweet, or being-warm, but the seeing-white, the tasting-sweet, the feeling-warm. (Protagoras.) Likewise also at the higher levels of Consciousness. But since natural Consciousness already develops for itself, through the inner necessity of the advance and the inner activity of Spirit, toward every higher form of its immediate existence — only that this inner advance and its necessity remains hidden from itself, and the higher level equally enters only as something immediately present, not as resulting from the insight into the untruth of earlier modes and their necessary sublation: so is the most important thing in philosophical consideration the scientific execution of the entire series of these forms, which allows each earlier one to exhibit in itself its own Truth — or rather, untruth — and, by thus showing at each one its own downfall grounded in itself, at the same time moves it to that point where the next-higher Appearance of Knowledge arises and emerges of itself. It may further be remarked that the philosophical mode of treatment — which traces natural Consciousness's manifold material wealth back to simple Determinacies and Concepts — is accustomed to appear to natural Consciousness only as an occupation with abstract thought-things, while natural Consciousness believes itself to possess the most real. On the one hand it already follows from the foregoing that it is rather natural Consciousness that is the poorer, since it by no means has its Object completely; on the other hand, he who receives the consecration of philosophy suffers, to be sure, a very great loss — namely in all external and untrue Knowledge. And while the sensuous eye is closed for him, yet the spiritual eye — turned inward, still imperfectly opened — it may well befall him that he does not yet recognize in the simple Concepts and essences the all-encompassing, truly infinite wealth of Spirit and of Truth, or that he only first believingly surmises their deep significance — which is to be contained for him in equally simple, still uncomprehended propositions and expressions — and sees Hieroglyphs and riddles before him. It has even befallen such as already come forward as speakers in philosophy, but have entered the temple of Reason and Spirit no further than its vestibules, that they wished to see in precisely the deepest and most speculative — and for that very reason the most content-rich and comprehensive — that philosophy has to exhibit, only empty logical formulae, and in the simple movement of the absolute Concept — which reaches through the entire spiritual and natural universe — nothing but a dialectical game.

§ 24. There emerges therefore for us in Consciousness, in addition to the moments indicated in § 20 — in so far as these also occur in natural Consciousness — more distinctly the moment of the Knowledge of this constitution of the matter, through which Consciousness (the knowing I) becomes objective to itself in the Determinacy of its Knowledge of an Object that is as in-itself for it, and accordingly Consciousness comes to itself in the whole relation of its reference (§ 10) to Consciousness.

And just as in Knowledge in general I distinguishes itself from its Object, so here I distinguishes itself from itself, and is the Knowledge of this relation — that for it (in a determinate form) something is the in-itself or Object in general. Or it considers in general the Consciousness for which Something is and holds, and, retaining both sides of this relation — both the side of the knowing I and that of the Object — it investigates what Consciousness is herein, or what it then knows, and to what it further determines itself.

Or it needs rather only quietly to attend to how and to what it develops itself of its own accord.

—

Anmerkung. Just as Consciousness in general is already in itself Self-Consciousness, and there can be no Consciousness without Self-Consciousness, so also does it here, through the distinction indicated in §, already become to that extent Self-Consciousness in that it becomes Object to itself and reflects upon itself in a determinate mode of its comportment. Yet I is here not yet as such Object to itself, but only first in one of its Determinacies or determinate modes; whereas Self-Consciousness in the proper sense is I = I.

But to develop itself to this is the tendency of Consciousness and its dialectic.

§ 25. From this consideration there results, according to the foregoing, the following universal: 1) that the in-itself — which is only a Being for Consciousness — belongs also only to this Determinacy of Consciousness whose content it constitutes, and disappears of itself therewith in the alteration of Knowledge; the essence or True of the Object as it holds for Consciousness therefore also falls within Knowledge itself, and is equal to it; 2) that Consciousness or I is a Universal over against each of its Determinacies, and as such maintains itself in the going-beyond every Determinacy (in the negation thereof); 3) that I, in so far as it recognizes itself only in its particular Determinacy, is therein not satisfied and contented with itself, and for that very reason goes beyond the same — or rather has already gone beyond it; and 4) when it does not satisfy itself, this occurs on the one hand because the insufficiency or the deficiency lies in the content of the known (in that which the Object is for Consciousness), and on the other hand this deficiency is a deficiency precisely because I has herein not yet attained — or is not yet — the Knowledge equal to itself (exhausting and satisfying it) as Universal.

Anmerkung. It becomes apparent here somewhat more closely how Consciousness or I already has the criterion of its cognition within itself, and carries within itself the measure of its Knowledge. That which the Object is to be in itself is surely equal to its essence and its Truth. As this, something of the Object holds for Consciousness, or is known by it in this manner. This Knowledge or known is the proper Determinacy of Consciousness, and it is of this that one initially speaks (cf. §§ 15–17), and the question is what Truth it has within itself. That Consciousness can have to do with nothing other than its Knowledge and the Determinacies thereof has already resulted earlier. If indeed an Object outside Knowledge is represented — which is for itself — and a Knowledge of this Object — which is likewise for itself — and Truth is to be the correspondence of Knowledge with this Object represented outside Knowledge: then Truth will indeed forever have to remain something concealed, and for a reason just as simple as that for which I cannot know what sort of person is knocking at my door before it has been opened, or for which X remains an unknown for me because I have equated it with an unknown that is to remain such. But by whose fault? If it were not the representation of an Object out there that blocked philosophy's path, one would have to wonder that many have not advanced it further. But such an Object is itself, to begin with, only my representation of it — since otherwise I could know nothing whatsoever of it — and thus itself a Determinacy of my Consciousness. And of such a Determinacy now — whichever it may be — that I have in my Consciousness, the question is what Truth it has within itself, and first of all what content I thereby finds set within itself. Since I is therein alone with itself and relates only to itself, it will also in this relation of its Determinacy to itself as a Universal — which is also capable of other determinations — know how to indicate and determine something more precise concerning the content of this Determinacy and its conformity with its universal nature and its Knowledge in general. There is present: 1) something that holds as the True and is known as such; 2) the Knowledge — which however is nothing other than also the known, and equal to it in content; and besides 3) I as Universal. Now since I reflects upon the content of what holds as the True — yet this content, upon closer examination, does not prove itself to it, so that it can no longer regard and hold it as the True or the in-itself, perhaps because it has found a contradiction therein — it recalls and becomes conscious that this True was only its particular Knowledge and belonged only to a particular mode of its comportment and of taking the Object, of which it thereby simultaneously becomes inwardly aware. This is the same as what befalls each one already in his daily experience — that he becomes conscious of an error and then assigns the fault thereof only to the manner and mode in which he previously regarded the Object: only that in the philosophical progression of Consciousness the correction and the experience made are taken in their universality and concern total forms of the same. The fault, then, Consciousness has to assign to its own side — and indeed to its determinate or particular mode of comportment; yet if it lies therein, I will also have no better hope in any other mode of its comportment, and will find its satisfaction in no particular one in general, because no particular one is equal and adequate to its universal essence and Knowledge. Only when I completely finds itself again in its Knowledge and cognition, and is immediately at home within it, will it find itself satisfied and at rest; and conversely, when it has reached that which satisfies and equals it, it will also in its Knowledge and cognition of the Object have only itself, and be immediately at home within itself. And when it finds itself satisfied and exhausted, and is therefore no longer impelled from any side to go beyond its Knowledge and its Object, it will also have the Truth it sought — and sought without rest until it reached this goal — and will demand no other. How and where I arrives at this goal, the sequel of this consideration and development of Consciousness has to show. Should one wish a confirmation of what has been said already in advance somewhere in experience — although the entire path to be traversed is itself a chain of the purest, yet scientifically treated experience — one need only be referred to oneself and to what goes on within oneself when one arrives at a clarification or cognition regarding some Object with which one was previously not at settled terms, a clarification that one takes immediately for the correct and apt one and in which one finds oneself satisfied. After one was previously not in accord with the matter, and it appeared dark, confused, disintegrating, or alien to one — it not being forgotten that the determinations or moments which constitute this imperfect form of the matter for one are likewise only one's Knowledge of it — one now finds at once, through a confluence of the matter presented under a determinate thought or standpoint with one's thinking, not only this matter in unity with itself, but also oneself in immediate unity with the matter (so thought or apprehended), in which one now has one's Knowledge as in one's immediate possession. For this becoming-I of the Object — to call it so for the present — one finds in languages many apt expressions, whose underlying representation is partly that of a coming-together and meeting, partly that of a grasping and making-one's-own of the Object; many are also drawn from the image of light — that namely which dawns inwardly and was previously lacking. This unification with the Object in individually and contingently given cognitions can, to be sure, always be something merely formal — that is, belong in terms of form only to the immediate Certainty which accompanies Consciousness in each of its modes — so that therein neither I nor pure Knowledge nor the Object in the form of its pure Truth is apprehended or truly comprehended; just as in any case the connection with the whole of Knowledge, and especially the absolute necessity and the pure insight into the ground of Truth, is likewise lacking. And just as such a cognition, for all its immediate Certainty, falls to the lot of a lower level of Knowledge, so it is also found that individuals in general rest at length and come to rest at some comprehensive view and manner of thinking — even if it be not the highest view and level of Knowledge — and at certain Determinacies, principles, etc. that hold for them as something ultimate, finding therein unity and accord for themselves. They are not secure therein, however, that their supports will not in some manner break or be withdrawn from them, and the entire edifice — if not collapse anew — at least be shaken in its foundations. For that reason it is Science alone that considers the whole Consciousness in all its modes, relations, and Appearances according to its necessity, and leads beyond them to the absolutely ultimate ground of Truth.

§ 26. Yet just as the I, though it is universal and thereby at the same time, in its transcendence of any given Determinacy, a negative activity, is prior to its own determination still wholly empty and devoid of content, and receives its determination as well as its content only through the Being of an Object for it: so too does the Object as such appear for Consciousness initially as something given, something encountered, as something that it is not immediately itself, hence as something unequal to it, as something foreign and insuperable; it is thus the absolutely Other of the I, the Not-I (in a more determinate relation, the incomprehensible thing-in-itself, behind which the I believes it cannot penetrate). It has therein its limit in the I, and the I has its limit in it. Insofar, however, as the I is on the one hand determined by its Knowledge of the Object, and on the other limited by it as the unequal and Other, it is likewise — just as its Object is a finite and an Appearance — itself finite and appearing Consciousness.

Anmerkung. The I in its abstraction and emptiness of all content, in which it remains only as pure negative activity, but in this pure negative nature possesses at the same time the principle of all spiritual movement and of the absolute Concept itself, stands (to speak in the language of representation) upon a foreground, as it were, upon which it has before it a brilliant and seductive world of sense, and at its senses the key to its disclosure; in its background, however, it has the world of Truth, the realm of spirit and of reason, and the key to this in that which it is, in its pure activity immediately itself. The more purely it allows the content of spirit and reason — or of that which is in and for itself — to enter into Consciousness from this background (the falsely understood Platonic doctrine of learning as a recollection), or conversely, the more deeply it penetrates backward into their substance and makes it its own immediate property: the more does it advance in itself its pure and absolute formation toward Truth. The mixture, however, of both worlds — of that which derives from spirit and the matter of the world of sense — begets the imperfect Knowledge of opinion (δόξα, οἴησις), of reflection, and of mere or abstract understanding. To annihilate these untruthful modes in and of itself, the I has the power in its pure activity, and science shows the path of this annihilation in its necessary, gradual progress. Philosophy has hitherto been greatly hindered by the psychological view, according to which reason is regarded only as a faculty of mind and soul existing alongside many other faculties and powers — a faculty by means of which one operates toward something else — rather than as the substance itself to be known, which simultaneously knows and mediates itself through the pure I, even though God has indeed been called the absolute reason. — Fichte, who grasped the unknown thing-in-itself left by Kant over against the subject, in purer abstraction as Not-I, was notoriously unable, for all that, to be done with it; for all the mutual determination, exhaustion, and attenuation of I and Not-I, for all their extension into an infinite progress in which, as in a calculation with + and −, as much accrued to the one as was deducted from the other, yet I and Not-I themselves remained inseparably opposed to one another; the Not-I was overcome no more than an infinite arithmetic series ever arrives at a pure terminus. This Not-I, as the negatively determined abstraction from the Object of Consciousness in general, which Consciousness has within itself as a Being for it, is here and in what follows preferably called simply the Other (τὸ ἕτερον); overcome and sublated, however, it is when it is comprehended; for then it ceases to be an Other for the I.

§ 27. But since the I, in this mutual Determinacy and finitude and reciprocal separation, is nonetheless as Consciousness (§ 10) connected with the Object and related to it, this connection — which is at the same time a separation, this union which is at the same time a differentiation — may provisionally be regarded as a judgment, in which the I as subject is united through the relation (copula) to an Object with this Object as predicate, yet both are also distinguished from one another just as in a judgment subject and predicate are different, mutually other to one another. And just as in the judgment, which according to its Concept expresses: subject = predicate, the ought of equality is at least contained and the Sublation or removal of inequality is demanded: so too has the I, as the universal, active, self-moving (spontaneity), this ought within itself, and is thereby in itself the impulse (impulse of Knowledge, the desire to know and to inquire) to be done with its Object — that is, to become equal to it — and to overcome it as still unequal to itself. As still unequal to one another, however, both sides are dialectical in themselves, that is, precisely on account of their inequality and mutual limitation they exhibit in themselves their deficiency and their limit as the Negative of themselves, which leads each of the two beyond itself and presents it in its Determinacy and limitation as something one-sided and untruthful, hence as something to be negated or sublated.

Anmerkung. The comparison of the connection of subject and object contained in Consciousness with a judgment does indeed import into the content of the § something already borrowed from Logic, but is presumably intelligible in itself, and what is requisite for its comprehension is already known to the beginner from grammar. This comparison, which was not absolutely indispensable to the matter but may perhaps contribute something to the elucidation of the whole development, contains at the same time, as will easily be recognized, a reference to Kant's synthetic judgments. The important question posed by this philosopher as the task of philosophy, but resolved by him very imperfectly — How are synthetic judgments a priori possible? — would more simply and correctly have been put immediately with reference to the pure I and its connection with an Object, as Fichte also did, though he too came to terms with the matter only imperfectly. The judgment of this connection is, however, an analytic one insofar as Consciousness, already in accordance with its Concept, is absolutely the Knowledge of an Other, a Not-I, or an Object in general, as Fichte also took the matter in his absolute I which itself posits the Not-I; but it is a synthetic judgment insofar as the Object, taken in its Determinacy, is not the I itself. Or in other words: the subjective I of Consciousness is in and for itself determined to know of an Other, and insofar the I already holds this Other in its abstract universality within itself and in itself as contained; but for concrete Consciousness itself the Other is also still as Other. — Since moreover it may appear that the application here made of the form of the judgment is inappropriate because a judgment contains a relation of subject and predicate but not of subject and object, one must observe in reply that in the case here given the object may also be regarded as predicate. Since the Object as known is an own Determinacy of Consciousness, the judgment would run: I, as a knowing universal, am myself this determinate known (in the form of the judgment: the universal is particular or singular), which is as much as to say: this determinate Knowledge is one of my own Determinations. What the dialectical is and whence it arises is indicated in the § itself. (Zenonian or Eleatic, sophistical, the true Platonic Dialectic.) Dialectic in general is no special art, nor a sleight of hand determined only to produce externally a deceptive and false semblance of the true, but in its truth rather rational thought itself in its pure negative activity, insofar as it dissolves one-sided and self-contained Determinations and moves beyond itself toward the cognition of their untruth — or the same activity of the pure I which was indicated above (Anmerkung to the preceding §) as the principle of all spiritual movement. Without Dialectic there is no true science, since it alone is the soul of all scientific progress, generates its method, and brings into it coherence and necessity. The true and speculative Dialectic, however, does not proceed merely negatively — that is, does not rest content with the dissolution of the determinations shown in their untruth, nor with a result that would be only Nothing — but wins from the negated content, because its negation is itself a determinate one, a new positive content. Cf. the indications above in §§ 6 and 8, and Hegel's Encyclopaedia §§ 15 and 16.

§ 28. But since the I, through its Negativity (§§ 26 and 27) and the differentiation of itself from the Object as well as from itself — a differentiation of which the Object is incapable, whereas in the I, being distinguished from itself, it is equally referred back to itself, and insofar as in this self-reference the I is self-identical, the reference as such is not a finite but an infinite one — is itself the impulse of transcendence and the activity that undertakes this dialectical movement, and therein at the same time coincides in one with Determinacy and limit (boundary) itself, that is, is equally the determined and limited as the determining and limiting, and also that which goes beyond Determinacy and limit or sublates them: this movement and negation falls first upon the still immediately given Object, over which the I reaches — as over something partly self-determined and partly recognized as its own Determinacy — and presents itself against it, the unequal and inadequate, as the universal, and simultaneously becomes a concrete Consciousness, in that it gains for its initial simplicity and emptiness, or its pure abstraction from all content, a determinate content.

Anmerkung. Consciousness can be finite only through the fact that its Knowledge, or that which it knows, is a finite. The common representation takes this in the sense that it is prevented by its limited nature from going further (and thus from knowing the infinite). But if it is capable of grasping the Concept of the infinite, for which it has and employs the word: why should it not be able to know the infinite just as well as anything else is known through its Concept? Enough attempts are made in textbooks that treat of God and His attributes to determine the infinite: wherein it is admittedly strange that often the very same persons who there teach and determine what God is, at another time again declare Him to be unknowable and inscrutable; especially since they possess revelation. The error in the matter consists rather in the fact that it is precisely the Concept of the infinite that is lacking, and instead of it only an indeterminate representation, a merely intended infinite, hovers before the mind. Here, by way of provisional elucidation — since the more precise account in its scientific connection belongs to Logic, and Knowledge of the infinite occurs further below as well — only the following. The finite in general is that which has an Other, or more determinately an Other of itself, outside of itself; in this Other, which it is not itself, it has its limit, its cessation, as this Other has its limit in it. Consciousness is therefore finite by virtue of the fact that it is not itself the Other by which it is determined and at which, as at an Other, it has its limit, as this Other has its limit in it. The infinite, by contrast, is taken initially as the not-finite, and with this merely negative determination imagination certainly finds room for everything indeterminate and ranging into immeasurable breadth, when it lets the infinite come after the finite and cannot find an end to its progression, whether in time or in space. But just as representation cannot sustain itself in this, but suddenly sets a limit in between, then, recalling that the infinite has no limit, removes it again and proceeds further, and repeats the same thing in the same manner yet more often: so it also forgets that the infinite already has its limit at the finite as its Other outside of it, which it is not and is not supposed to be, and is consequently just as finite as that other. If this infinite is therefore itself a finite, and no other can be found, then indeed it must remain obscure what the infinite is; only it is to be remarked that most of those who take the infinite as an unknown have to do merely with this intended infinite, which has represented itself to itself as a finite. But if we take the not-finite in the sense of the conceptual determinations supplied by the finite, then the truly and genuinely infinite can necessarily only be such as 1) has no Other outside itself, and 2) even if such an Other presents itself at the outset — as, for example, the finite itself — is not thereby limited, which 3) is possible only through the fact that this Other is no Other for it, or that it, sublating the posited limit and going out beyond it to the Other, rather coincides with itself in the same and is equal to itself. Whether such an infinite exists or does not exist may be left to those who say that no Being can be extracted from the Concept alone, and who therefore hold to faith. For us this question has no significance, since what concerns us primarily is cognition and Knowledge and its content and Truth. In what was said above, only the reference of Consciousness in its Other to itself and the self-equality therein present have been called infinite (infinity of form); so long, however, as the content of the Other is for Consciousness also actually an Other, the I remains, for all its infinite nature, only finite Consciousness. Limit and barrier are here taken as nearly synonymous; their distinction Logic develops. Both are negations, but not merely of an Other or of an otherness, but also of the own Being of that to which they belong, as already the sensible or spatial limit shows, which is a cessation (or also a commencement), hence a non-Being of the limited, but at the same time also separates from it what is contiguous, of which it is equally the limit. The distinction, however, between the two consists in this: that the (logical or metaphysical) limit (ὅρος, ὁρισμός), which more precisely becomes the own Determinacy of what Something is and whereby it distinguishes itself from Other, lets this determinate Being subsist in its unmoved repose, whereas the barrier, which relates to the Being-in-itself of Something and to its determination or the ought, is a limit as much posited as negated or to be sublated — a limit to whose Concept it belongs that it be sublated, or that it be transcended. He who sees limits set upon himself does not acknowledge his limited Being as his true Being and his determination or as what he ought to be; but he who sublates the limits set upon him thereby first attains his true Being or that which he is in himself. For the relation indicated above — of Consciousness going beyond its Determinacy through an Other to Truth, a relation whose moments, which constitute it and coincide in one act, may well be duly grasped — the expression barrier is therefore more correct; only it is, as will subsequently emerge, a barrier which spirit has set for itself in its subordinate relation as Consciousness, and, as is already apparent here, both the positing of Determinacy or determination and its Sublation belong to the own activity of Consciousness (for only a knowing I can distinguish and determine).

§ 29. So long, however, as this content remains unequal to the I, the latter has, in its immediate connection with the Object and in the positive reference of the Object to itself — whereby it is its Knowledge — certainty (§ 11), but not yet Truth, on account of the differentiation and inequality that equally subsist, which, as merely external and negative unity and connection of mutually independent sides (Synthesis), becomes precisely the incitement to dialectical movement. For Truth is the agreement of Knowledge with the Object, or the unity and equality of both. The Object, however, or the Gegenstand, is nothing other than the content of the known distinguished from the I, or Knowledge itself, for which the respective Object is what it knows of it and how it knows it (§ 18). Consequently, when the Object is still unequal to its knowing Subject — and this is so long as the I still goes beyond it: §§ 25–28 — this is an inequality that falls within Consciousness itself, and is therein none other than the inequality still subsisting between the limited and thereby dialectical content of the known and the universality of the knowing Subject, on account of which the latter, not satisfying itself (§ 25), passes beyond that content and its Object to a different Knowledge thereof, and hence also to a different content of the known.

Anmerkung. This and the following § contain, as the result of the foregoing development, already something more specific concerning the condition for the Truth of cognition and concerning the sense in which the definition of Truth as the agreement of Knowledge with the Object is to be understood. But since herewith, in accordance with the nature of this investigation, nothing further is gained than a cognition of the formal constitution of the matter, and hence only still an outer Knowledge of it: it readily appears that what is rather at stake is the content (the substance) of Truth, with which the I, purified of its untruthful content, must fill itself in order to know it as itself, and to move in that element in which Knowledge (or thought) and Being are in unity and equality. The cognition of this substance of Truth is, however, precisely philosophy itself. The pure I as pure Knowledge would indeed be able to enter immediately into this element, for as pure Knowledge it is already the same; but it is first necessary to prove that the other modes of Consciousness are not; this proof, however, is conducted only through the fact that they present themselves, with the content conditioned by them, for cognition in their untruth, and that in them the I brings itself forth as pure Knowledge. What one might otherwise represent to oneself in connection with the given definition of Truth, even when one allows it to be correct, may be regarded as fairly disposed of by the foregoing development; it will be found that with respect to the Object one had to do only with a represented and intended one, and had not fixed one's eye sharply upon the sense of the definition in general. Were one to lay this definition itself immediately at the foundation, and to investigate its sense on all sides, its Dialectic could have had no other result than the one developed here. In the first place, if one does not wish first to investigate Knowledge, it will be a matter of what the Object is; for in order to be able to judge whether something agrees with something else, one must first get to know this other. Now, however, the Object is itself nothing other than our Knowledge, a known Being in Consciousness for Consciousness; if it is not supposed to be this, how is one to get behind it? Or rather, that Consciousness which makes it into something else is equally a Knowledge, and indeed a determinate mode of knowing the Object, and thus itself a Determinacy of Consciousness. In the three answers cited above (Anmerkung to § 9) to the question of what Truth is — or rather in the one of them to which the other two are also to be traced back, namely that it is the agreement of our representations with one another — the Object has been left untouched. And since among the answers that are to be traced back (i.e., not to be left standing but to be brought to a greater correctness and more adequate Truth) the very definition itself is also to be found which expresses the agreement of representations with their objects: one may assume that for Truth it is not so much the objects that matter as only the representing and its comportment toward itself — wherein one might easily wish to discover something idealistic, namely something of the vulgar idealism for which external objects are only representations. But even apart from the fact that this definition speaks only of representations, whereupon it may be translated thus: Truth is the agreement of untruthful Knowledge with itself — it is in general difficult to find any real content and thought in it. For as representation, one is equal to another; representing is representing; a distinction can lie only in the represented, that is, in the Object, and must enter through it. If we assume, however, that the author of the three answers had something better and deeper in mind — namely Knowledge and thought — whereupon the definition would read: Truth is the agreement of thought or of Knowledge with itself, then we must indeed assent to him herein, and also in the sense that in Truth Knowledge and thought have to do only with themselves; but the question is: which Knowledge and which thought, and of what? It therefore remains in any case only an empty identity that is expressed. A thought, however, and something speculative comes about only through the fact that the unity and equality of something first differentiated is exhibited.

§ 30. The dialectical movement therefore becomes a comparison of Knowledge with itself, for which Consciousness itself possesses the standard and has it within itself. And the aim of this movement of Consciousness is to elevate certainty to Truth — that is, to that self-certain Knowledge in which subject and object are not merely in the manner of certainty (§ 11 and § 29) but according to their content or their substance perfectly equal and one. It will be shown that this aim of Consciousness is attained through the Knowledge that is reason, in which the subjective determination is just as much objective determination. (Cf. § 9.)

Anmerkung. In the dialectical investigation of the definition of Truth mentioned in the preceding Anmerkung, it was furthermore necessary to inquire what agreement is and is to be called, since one may in no wise in philosophy let something pass as if it were already self-evident and surrender it to the opinion of Knowledge. Already in mere certainty, according to the definition given of it, there is an agreement (identity) of Knowledge with the Object; yet, before it has been elevated to Truth, the Object in it is just as untruthful as the Knowledge of it. Since in it, as in Consciousness in general, a connection with a content still distinguished from the I — in which consequently the I does not yet know itself — is present, this was called in the preceding a Synthesis. Synthesis, however, from Empedocles onward — whose friendship (φιλία) that resolves the plurality of elements into unity was also only a Synthesis — down to modern philosophy, belongs among the untruthful modes and views, whose uncritical retention rather bars the entry into philosophy. The unity to which a manifold or a diverse is to be united through Synthesis is no true unity, nor equality, nor identity, but merely a connection or composition in which the connected still subsist as much for themselves, and are still just as external to one another, as before in their separation; for the fact that they are now to be together in a One that comprehends them has not yet sublated their independent validity and subsistence, that is, their separation. Thinking — namely, a precise thinking — can, so long as diversity is present, only see diversity for all synthetic unity; an imprecise thinking, however, tarries now with this merely represented and intended unity and declares it to be the Truth that is in itself, now again passes over to diversity and plurality without inner necessity, and wants again to take the individual as the in-itself-subsisting; it is a merely external and inconsequent going back and forth, as with Empedocles. With such a mere composition and external connection, therefore, one cannot get on when one wants to comprehend something in philosophy, nor take a single step further. A true unity, however, can come about only through the fact that the one is recognized as the same as what the other is, and the differentiated are in this recognizing reduced to mere moments — that is, lose their independent subsistence and validity against one another. The sense in which the agreement of Knowledge with objects is ordinarily taken rests on the one hand upon the misguided view that the objects (one thinks first of all of the external ones) are already in and for themselves something true and reposing in their Truth. Of external objects the representing subject may indeed have in its representation faithful images — as well as a mirror, or even better — and thus an agreement with them: but is this Truth? Is representing Knowledge? Are the changeable, finite, and transitory things themselves something true and the Truth? They are indeed something existent and present, but is what is present therefore already something true? Lies too are something present, and of many a lie it might perhaps be shown that Truth in a higher sense is contained in it than in many a present thing. The things are also something determinate, but precisely their determinate Being — limited and conditioned from without — constitutes also their finitude and untruth, and drags them into their ruin. Great philosophers have therefore said that the Truth of things is only in God and can only be known in God; and even if we do not go so far here, nor yet wish to investigate what that means, the immediate Truth of things is their imperishable genus and their Concept, which, even if it is a determinate and not the highest Concept, yet carries within itself the infinite form of the Concept in general. Genus and Concept, however, are given and known only through Knowledge. It must therefore be said that the objects, as they immediately present themselves, are rather not something true, nor reposing in their Truth, but are moved to their Truth only through Knowledge. In the case of objects not given to the senses, however, which constitute by far the more important part — for even the known content of the sensible objects belongs to their number as soon as one has gone beyond what is sensuously given in them and beyond their immediacy and singularity — wherein does the agreement of Knowledge with the Object consist for these, or what constitutes their Truth? Since objects of this kind, according to Kant — as he says for example of right — cannot appear at all (when, namely, the word appearing is taken in as restricted a sense as by him), but belong to the understanding, in which their Concepts lie, and derive in general from spirit, and are originally at home on the ground of Knowledge: it will also be Knowledge alone that is acquainted with them and is master over the determination of their Truth. Yet whatever content-determinations Consciousness may first have employed to grasp such an Object — whose objective content here too can only be that as which it is known — here too applies the question already so often posed: what then does the I know when it knows this Object thus; and here too will the question after this What, in the continuing alteration and dialectical progression of Knowledge as of the Object, come to rest only when the I has arrived with its Object at the point where it perfectly satisfies and corresponds to it, and the I finds it necessary from no quarter to go any further beyond it and its known content; this, however, will occur only when the I has and knows itself, its determinations and its pure Concept in the Object and its content-determinations, or when it recognizes the content of the Object — which thereby becomes its pure and true content — no longer as an Other and something foreign but as itself and its own. There is therewith already contained in what has been said also what remained to be recalled, on the other part, in the discussion of the sense and content of the definition of Truth, concerning Knowledge. However one may otherwise take the definition, it already contains of itself that Knowledge is something altogether necessary for Truth, and that the latter, as the agreement of Knowledge (or of cognition) with the Object, cannot come about without Knowledge. And indeed Truth is to be sought only in Knowledge — be it divine or human — not outside of it.

§ 31. In this dialectical progression and development toward the aim of Truth, however, the side of the I is just as dialectical as the side of the Object. For although the I proves itself against the Object through its negative activity as the more universal, it is nonetheless 1) as this merely formal and relatively universal still without its own content (§ 26); 2) dependent upon and determined by its Object — which has not yet been produced by itself but is given and encountered, and which provides its content; 3) in this dependence and Determinacy through the Object, which is a finite and limited one, itself finite Consciousness (§ 26).

§ 32.
