In this initial bondage to its Object, however, the I is at the same time the power and activity which, through reflection upon the respective content and upon itself as the knower of it, in each of its determinate modes or shapes (§ 28), purifies itself step by step into pure and rational Knowledge, and elevates its Object along with itself to the point where the latter, as content of pure Knowledge, is no longer the Other and the insuperable of the I (§ 26), but rather the I knows it as itself.

Anmerkung. The pure — or more precisely, the empty, contentless — I is already an abstraction of thought, since every natural and given I is a concrete one filled with manifold content. To raise oneself to the thought of this pure I is not difficult; one need only think of oneself or say to oneself: I, and therein leave aside all content of a known. The pure I in the proper sense, however, would be designated as that which has already been purified from the untruthful content of Knowledge, which has through this purification freed itself from its concretion and immediate mode of Being, and has thereby produced itself as pure I and as the principle or the active form of pure Knowledge — that is, as that which has already left behind the untruthful modes of Consciousness and finds itself at the beginning or rather in the element of pure and actual science. That the side of the I on the path to this aim is just as dialectical as the side of the Object follows immediately from the fact that the determinate mode of the Object is the determinate content of the Knowledge of the I itself, and that therefore the dialectical movement represents only the one and the same progression of the whole of Consciousness. The side of the I comes forth more closely as such only when the I, in the contemplation of its Object and through that which the latter becomes for it in the process, finds itself of its own accord compelled to reflect upon itself and to attribute the Appearance of the Object solely to its own mode of contemplation and comportment — or when, as was designated above, it makes the experience that what was initially taken as the Being-in-itself of the Object was such only for Consciousness. A distinction might be drawn between the I determined by its content and that which, as the self-identical negative activity or active form, is the inner soul of progression still hidden from immediate and transient Consciousness itself, and the principle of dialectical unrest; yet the I as the knowing in general is also this other I itself. The pure I, however, in the proper sense mentioned above — which moves in the element of pure reason and pure science — is by no means an empty and abstract one; its content rather, which it simultaneously generates, cognizes, and acquires through the movement of pure thought and Knowledge, is the pure Being of reason itself, its pure essences and Concepts, which underlie all particular (concrete) Being and thought — the substantial Truth and the concrete universal thoughts. The concrete content of the pure universal thoughts and Concepts is, however, well to be distinguished from what is called concrete in the ordinary sense.

§ 33. Besides this Consciousness engaged with its Object in dialectical progression, however, there is also our Consciousness, for which that Consciousness in its movement is itself Object. It is indifferent in this regard whether to regard the Consciousness appearing in its movement as a foreign one or as one's own — though as distinguished from itself in its Determinacy and becoming objective to itself (§ 24) — since it is through its respective Object and the content of its Knowledge thereby present in it perfectly determined in itself, and that distinction, if it were made, would belong merely to arbitrary representation. Essential for us, the contemplators, however, is the objective bearing of that Consciousness which is contemplated in its Knowledge of an Object, and for which the latter — as the content filling it and constituting its Determinacy — has the significance of Truth or of something that is in itself.

§ 34. In having before us, therefore, this appearing Consciousness — whatever its content may be — and in contemplating therein both the Object it has and in that Object itself and how it has it, we have ourselves to comport ourselves merely in quiet and, as it were, in a watching manner, and to take care only that we apprehend it faithfully in its determinations — whose reception falls to us — and allow it to proceed in its own development. It will be shown that in general this Consciousness in its movement always itself arrives at the point at which we stand from the very outset — namely, that it becomes objective to itself, and thereby the Object first valid for it as Being-in-itself or as Truth sinks for it to something belonging only to its Knowledge and being so only for it: wherewith this Knowledge determines itself rather as the Truth of the Object, and the general expression of the Truth of appearing (i.e., itself still untruthful) Consciousness is: for the I in this shape of its Consciousness the Object is this, or is determined thus and so. With the movement of Consciousness that is for us the object of contemplation, however, we ourselves are also led to its aim — to pure rational Knowledge. Particular reflections that may in the course of this belong to our anticipatory activity and contemplation, and do not yet fall within the course of the contemplated Consciousness itself, will also be distinguished especially from the latter.

Anmerkung. The content of this and the preceding § still concerns our relation — that is, the relation of the subject in general in whom contemplation falls — to the Consciousness that is contemplated. This relation is a purely objective one, or our comportment is a merely passive or neutral one, in that we must abstain from any interference of our own in the course of the matter, and are, as it were, only the cunning that lurks in ambush, watching and observing what comes to pass. Thus the most important modes and shapes of Consciousness to be here considered step before us and unfold their character and their particular (i.e., constitutive) Truth. The activity of our thought therein consists only in our apprehending correctly and purely that which each Consciousness is with its Object, or its content-determinations. Since we ourselves likewise know as yet nothing more precise of this, and wish rather to arrive at the aim of pure Knowledge and Truth only through our comportment together with the Consciousness that is contemplated, we differ from it or are in advance of it only to the extent that, instructed as to the nature of Consciousness and the progress of the matter in general, we make appearing Consciousness itself the object of our contemplation and come to know its development in scientific context according to its objective necessity. Yet even what has already been said and recognized in general concerning this matter must still verify itself for us in concreto in the individual Consciousness determined by its particular Object and content. The separation of the contemplating and the contemplated Consciousness, however, can, according to the nature of the matter and the intended aim, subsist only until both coincide in one aim. That moreover — for all the apparent repose and passivity on our part — the objective necessity with which the development of the contemplated proceeds before our eyes is the own movement and absolute necessity of our I and rational thought, and that behind us, the contemplators themselves, there is furthermore the substantial Truth that is in and for itself which moves us toward itself — a higher result will more closely demonstrate in what follows. That which already is in itself is to become, on the path of cognition, also for us.

§ 35. Consciousness, which is in this manner contemplated in its Appearance and dialectical movement or in the experiences it undergoes within itself, constitutes as such (phenomenological Consciousness) the object of a special science, the Phenomenology of Spirit. In the following section, for the propaedeutic purpose, the most important stages of its development toward the Knowledge that is reason will be considered according to their principal moments.

Anmerkung. The name Phenomenology Kant employed in his natural philosophy solely with reference to the modality of the motion or rest of matter and of natural bodies, and placed this part of his doctrine thereof — following the schema of his categories — as the fourth alongside Phoronomy, Dynamics, and Mechanics. (See his Metaphysical First Principles of Natural Science, 2nd ed., Preface, p. XXI, and p. 138.) The Phenomenology of Spirit in the meaning indicated was first established as a fully elaborated science by Hegel, and indeed at once in such a degree of completion that for subsequent elaborators there may remain only the explication and interpretation and perhaps the further development in individual parts or presentations, together with the demonstration of what has been scientifically developed in external history. This most profound, most thorough, and greatest of all histories — a work upon which that nation has cause to be proud in which the spirit first grasped itself in such a depth and disclosed the inner realm of its organization for the entire domain of its Appearance in Consciousness — seems, nonetheless, still not to have been duly attended to and appreciated by many thinkers. Having been worked out in its great scope initially for the same purpose for which only the most important moments or stages of the whole — which also underlie the higher and more concrete shapes of spirit and therefore recur in the developments that fall later — are to be considered here, it was subsequently incorporated as an integral part of philosophical science itself, into the philosophy of spirit. The propaedeutic application, justified by its particular purpose and different in its direction, is not thereby excluded.

SECOND SECTION. THE APPEARING OR PHENOMENOLOGICAL CONSCIOUSNESS. Preliminary Overview of the Stages or Shapes of This Consciousness.

§ 36. Consciousness in general is consciousness precisely by virtue of being, as knowledge, related to an object -- initially an other than itself -- (and at least in form, or as its own determinacy, at the same time identical with it, cf. §§ 17 and 28), while also distinguishing this object from itself. But through being distinguished from it by the knowing I, the object also becomes the I itself, and thus something known. If now the object of consciousness is considered, for the time being even only externally (through reflection upon it), according to its possible diversity, then, depending on whether it is either that which stands over against the I simply as an other (object as such), or the I itself as subjective, or finally a determinacy that belongs as much to the I as it is objective, or is both in a unity (the thought), there also arise three distinctions of consciousness, namely: 1) consciousness of a sheer other, or external consciousness, consciousness as such or in the narrower sense; 2) self-consciousness; 3) that which unites the first two within itself, or is just as much their identity as it is neither the one nor the other alone (identity of thinking and being), i.e. reason.

Anmerkung. When, for the purpose of a division, the object is considered according to the mere possibility of its diversity as the principle of division (whereby, however, the diversity must already be no longer the indeterminate or merely empirical diversity admitting of a great manifold, but diversity in determinate relation or absolute difference): then one will hopefully allow as a third possibility that the object could equally well be the one as well as the other at once (unity or, if one prefers, synthesis of both), and equally well neither the one nor the other, which for a merely separating understanding would actually yield two further distinctions, but in substance falls into one unity. Logical thinking, one would at least expect, would through so many upheavals of its fixed concepts of the understanding in our time have finally arrived at a higher consciousness about itself, so that it would no longer wish to hold up the so-called laws of thought of reflection -- here above all the principium contradictionis and exclusi tertii -- like an insurmountable talisman against speculative matters, if indeed it has not renounced rational cognition altogether. These determinations of reflection, which everywhere have truth only insofar as a one-sided and itself untrue understanding fixes the opposed and the particular in its rigid opposition and particularity and holds them apart, are, far from being truly supreme and immediately certain laws of thought, rather subject in themselves to a higher dialectic, as logic has to show, and cannot maintain themselves as truth even in natural things, since their untruth rather comes into the hands of physicists and chemists under experimentation. In speculation, however, which itself first brings them to their purest form in their subordinate sphere and mediation, but here displays and dissolves them in their abstraction and one-sided truth, i.e. untruth, and thereby also leads them to their truth, they can be used even less as instruments of operation and ladders of progress, since everything speculative and all true philosophy consists in not allowing the separated, the distinguished, the opposed and the contradictory to persist in this form, but rather in recognizing it in its unity and identity. Just as individual examples or intimations of this have already occurred, so not merely the continuation in what follows, but all actual philosophy in general supplies examples everywhere that something is truly to be thought and known. Applied to the object of consciousness, which, however, precisely for this reason, in its coinciding with consciousness, ceases to be a mere object, the third possibility would be that which is equally well other as well as not-other of the knowing I, or is itself, and equally well also neither (mere) subject nor (mere) object, but the unity of both. This, however, equally well the true subject-object as what is neither (one-sided) subject nor (one-sided) object, is as a single determinacy the pure (and for that very reason also real) thought, but in its totality reason, which, and insofar as it becomes conscious of itself as all reality. Or, inasmuch as consciousness is equally well simple consciousness as self-consciousness, it thereby shows itself to be already in itself the unity of both; in its truth, however, this what it is in itself is reason. Reason, being the thinking and at the same time the substance of true reality for cognition, has therefore also been designated as the identity of thinking and being. In a few words one can here indicate what constitutes the actual character of philosophy and what is philosophical in philosophy. To find and recognize this identity, in whatever form the opposition might be grasped and expressed, was the endeavor of all genuine philosophy and speculation since the dualistic conception gained a foothold in general consciousness; to hold fast this dualism, on the contrary, not merely with the common conception but even to assert and pronounce the absolute separation and non-identity of thinking and being, spirit and nature, etc., as something absolute and as the true, is a renunciation of everything speculative and merely the character of modern and subjective unphilosophy. In the philosophy of the ancients (Greeks) this glaring opposition, as it has fixed itself in the representation of modern times, was not present in this form; rather, identity already lay of itself at the ground of their philosophizing, and this maintained itself, without separation and opposition of the subject, in the purely objective element of the matter as it is in and for itself; and already their oldest natural philosophy rested on the very important proposition that only the like can know the like, whereby the equality of knowing and of what is to be known, however they may have grasped and explained it, was presupposed. The oldest skepticism, however, was directed precisely against that which the unphilosophy and the self-styled skepticism of our time has elevated to its most important dogma and lets pass, as it is given to them through eyes and ears etc. immediately and without thinking -- namely, against the certainty and truth of sensuous experience. By contrast, among the ancients, already among the Eleatics, genuine knowledge or the knowledge of truth (zero) was very determinately distinguished from the knowledge of mere representation and opinion (5, 5), a distinction whose significance and recognition has, in our time, been lost precisely in that philosophizing which, instead of dealing with actual thoughts, deals only with formal thinking and its unities and with representations.

§ 37. However much, through this division, the three main stages of consciousness as appearing knowledge (if indeed one still wishes to call the knowledge of the third, of reason, a consciousness in the ordinary sense) are correctly brought forth, this division nonetheless has, like all divisions of the ordinary sort which, whether in the domain of the spiritual or of nature, are obtained through a merely external reflection upon their subject matter, the following faults and deficiencies in common with them: 1) the distortion and incorrectness of the appearance thereby occasioned, as if the members thus found through the division were also to stand alongside one another (as coordinate), and each had equal justification in truth and equal value with the other; 2) the lack of an inner, self-producing connection and transition from one member to the other, and of the organic vitality that mere abstraction and reflection can never confer upon the dead, externally separated and kept-apart parts of their objects; and therefore 3) the deficiency that through such a division, and even through the execution undertaken according to it, just as little insight into an inner connection and necessary progression is afforded. Against such a merely externally intellectual classification, the truth and the scientific rather consists in this, that each shape of consciousness determines itself through itself to its higher stage, and that they therefore come to stand not alongside but after one another, and in their development follow from and upon one another. Of these stages, however, through and beyond which the consciousness of appearance raises itself to its truth, the following is a preliminary general overview: I. Consciousness (in the narrower sense) a) as sensuous consciousness or immediate certainty, b) perceiving, c) understanding; II. Self-consciousness -- a) as drive and desire, b) relation of independent and dependent self-consciousness, c) universal self-consciousness; III. Unity of consciousness and self-consciousness, or reason, which is the concept of spirit, while spirit is its truth.

Anmerkung. 1) Of the inadequacy of ordinary dividing for the presentation of philosophical truth, mention has already been made in the introduction. As little as in the system of philosophy the arrangement of the parts can be undertaken at will and caprice, and rather everything specific and particular receives its place in the connection of its mediation most determinately assigned by the idea or the concept of the whole and its inwardly self-articulating development, and has its significance and truth only in this place: just as little can, precisely on account of this character of the matter, the essence of philosophy be reconciled with the ordinary procedure of division and classification, of which the example given is by no means one of the worst, insofar namely as some philosophical material is regarded as an already wholly given and independently existing thing, and in the deliberation as to what sort of an intelligible arrangement and (externally) expedient distribution might be applied to it, some determination of reflection, which, even if it belongs to the conceptual moments of the object itself, is yet only a single one of its determinacies, is to furnish the ground of division. If, according to such a division or disposition, the execution is actually undertaken, the transition from one member or piece to the next, as one so often hears in our practical lectures and reproaches the ordinary treatment of logic itself for, tends to consist in nothing other than: Now that we are done with the first part, we come to the next -- no better than when in natural history it is said: Now that we have spoken of the bear, we come to the dog or ape, etc. But if the execution is better, i.e. if it really delivers a particular that articulates itself according to the concept and essence of the object and determines itself further through itself: then it precisely thereby refutes its division and shows its insufficiency and inadequacy to the subject matter. Already in schools, the beautiful construction of the whole in the rhetorical masterworks of the ancients, and the inner, organically living connection of the parts, which presents the simple concept of the subject matter spread out in its total particularization and development, and which also closes together again to the whole the members mutually mediated through the idea of the whole as among themselves -- this should be especially highlighted and demonstrated to students, and their sense nourished early on by such presentation, before the prescriptions of a later coming ordinary logic or rhetoric, applicable only to dead and dismembered materials, teach them the opposite of what an ideal, artful presentation requires, one that expresses and exhausts the concept in its particularization and connected articulation. -- 2) Although, in contrast to self-consciousness, the consciousness of an other which is not I, insofar as this other is for consciousness also something existing outside it, can be called external consciousness, it is for knowledge not so much a matter of this external existence of objects as of what they are inwardly, and what they determine themselves to be in the knowledge of consciousness. Since, however, as the progression has to show, the whole of external consciousness very soon transforms itself into an inward one, moving only in its own determinations, it is appropriate not even in name to occasion, through a designation that would lend an apparent weight to something transient in the development of the itself transient whole consciousness of appearance, a distorted view and misinterpretation. Essential, on the other hand, would be to designate consciousness, in its character of being consciousness of an other than I (regardless of where representation places this other), insofar and as long as the other is for it also a genuinely other, as such from this side as well. But this lies, strictly speaking, already in the concept of consciousness in general, and consciousness which has not yet more closely determined itself to self-consciousness is therefore better called simply consciousness. The pure rational knowledge, for which no other as such any longer exists, is therefore also only improperly still called consciousness.

## I. DAS BEWUSSTSEIN. (In engerer Bedeutung, im Gegensatze des Selbstbewusstseins). — I. CONSCIOUSNESS. (In the Narrower Sense, in Contrast to Self-Consciousness).

§ 38. Consciousness, as it first gives and finds itself -- and as it gives itself it is also merely taken up by us in the scientific treatment of its experiences -- is the immediate consciousness, in which it is itself not aware of having done anything for its knowledge of an object, but merely has it immediately as it presents itself. It is only I and an other that is not I, but rather something external and alien to it, something given and found. And just as this given is for consciousness an immediate or a being, so consciousness itself is also immediately related to this its object, the simple unmediated certainty of it. It is thus with its object at first still in immediate, compact unity, inasmuch as it has it immediately or is entirely only in it, not yet even distinguishing itself from it -- merely the reflecting mirror that knows nothing of itself and its reflecting. For becoming conscious of this is already reflection of consciousness upon itself, or the distinction (cf. § 10) through which the other first stands over against the I as other, and the I over against it. Just as little does consciousness at first, in the immediate having of its object, distinguish that it has it through its seeing or hearing etc., or that the object arrives at it through the medium of the senses at all: which likewise occurs only through a further reflection, recollection, and distinction. Since, however, everything that thus immediately becomes an object of consciousness comes to it only through the senses, it thereby determines itself as sensuous consciousness, and its object as a whole as the sensuous world (world of the senses).

Anmerkung. What the simple expressions "immediate," "mediate," "mediated" mean to say requires, for understanding, no special elucidation at this point. It is to be noted, however, that the immediate, insofar as it presents itself as that which is mediated neither in itself nor through another, and as devoid of mediation, or as something equal and one, is also equivalent to being -- namely, to what is only a being or something that is, nothing further, and as a pure, mere object for thinking cannot be designated otherwise. In this sense of a being, however, immediacy holds not only of what for representation is outside it, but also of all thought unities, insofar as they are grasped and regarded by thinking as a resting and compact same into which no difference penetrates, or in which mediation is sublated. Likewise, in contrast to its mediating activities, consciousness, spirit, the mind presents itself as an immediate comportment and as a being. More speculative is the recognition that, just as without thinking and knowing and outside of it there is no mediation, so too there is no immediate, as the distinguished and opposed, without mediation and without something mediated, and therefore it too is possible and present only for a knowing and thinking.

### A. DAS SINNLICHE BEWUSSTSEIN ODER DIE UNMITTELBARE GEWISSHEIT. — A. SENSUOUS CONSCIOUSNESS OR IMMEDIATE CERTAINTY.

§ 39. Sensuous consciousness, as it gives itself as such and is the very first mode of a knowledge of something, appears as the immediate certainty of all objects of the external world; and since to it belongs the entire richness of this sensuous world, it also possesses therein a richness and an endless plenitude of immediately certain truths. Since, furthermore, nothing is knowingly either taken away from or added to the object, and the object is rather taken and left completely and unchanged as it presents itself, it seems to be not merely the richest but also the most complete, truest, and most thoroughly real consciousness. The object is therefore not merely an immediate one for this consciousness, but it also counts for it as being-in-itself, as essence and truth.

§ 40. It is now to be seen how sensuous consciousness, given this constitution of its object and of itself, comports itself further, what experiences it makes about this, and how the stated determinations of immediacy and immediate certainty, of completeness, truth, and reality are maintained and asserted for it. In the name of this consciousness, we may well grant to the testing investigation that, since it is a matter of the truth of knowledge and cognition: 1) truth and reality, or the being-in-itself, must be something abiding and unchangeable; 2) truth, the certainty of which consciousness has and acknowledges as its own certainty within itself, must also be statable in its content, since a truth cannot lose anything of its truth and validity merely by being stated; and 3) the immediate object of certainty, precisely because consciousness has kept it whole and complete and unchanged, insofar as it is distinct from other objects and is only this one that it just has and means, must also be statable as this determinate and particular one in the most determinate, exact, and complete way; but if this does not happen, then the knowledge of the certainty that cannot be determinately expressed is also no knowledge, but only a meaning (intending).

§ 41. If we ourselves provisionally reflect on this consciousness and its object, the following soon emerges. Just as the object, by which consciousness finds itself determined, is a determinate, particular, and for it also contingent one, so too is it itself only this particular I that happens to have this object, and therein a contingent and dependent one; another particular I, and it itself at another time, has another object, and therewith also another truth. Immediate certainty therefore limits itself only to this: that this particular I knows this particular object. But it knows of it only because it is for it, and it will know to say of it only this, that it is for it, and will not even verify this saying. If it wishes to avail itself of the expression of a universal representation, a genus name (tree, animal, house, etc.) to designate the object and what it knows of it, it would therein be no longer either first or sensuous consciousness, to which such universalities, precisely because it is only immediately sensuous, do not yet pertain, even though it is driven to this result through the course of its experience; nor would it thereby leave the object as it immediately received it and abstain from its own contribution and alteration: for universal representations and generic concepts are obtained only through comparison, abstraction, and negation (every abstraction is a negation). But if it avails itself of such a genus name, it immediately commits a double untruth: that instead of indicating an immediate, it utters a mediated, and instead of designating a particular distinguished from all others in the most determinate way (namely, this meant one), it names a universal which is also not-this, i.e. every other. "The object is, is being" -- this can be said even less and with even less truth for its determinate indication, for a being is everything. It thus already shows itself from this side that the immediate knowledge of sensuous consciousness of its determinate object, which would be only this particular one, is something unsayable, and that it does not get beyond the wanting to say, i.e. beyond mere meaning (intending).

§ 42. Yet this knowledge is not even an immediate one. If "the object is" is supposed to mean as much as: it is immediate, i.e. it is not yet a relation to an other, has not become through any other nor itself become an other -- then this is equally immediately untrue, for the I distinguishes itself from it; thereby it is an other, as the I is an other, or it is only for the I because the I knows of it, i.e. the one is conditioned and mediated through the other. Just as the certainty of this object is conditioned for the I by its being, so it is also conditioned for it by this knowing I; the conditioned, however, is not an immediate but a mediated. That in this way the immediacy of the relation is rather a mediation, insofar as this I has rather to regard itself as the condition of its certainty of this object -- this experience emerges more closely when sensuous consciousness reflects on the inherent inequality and diversity of its senses, and becomes aware of the conditionedness of the peculiar constitution of its object through the senses. For it is one object that is seen, another that is heard, tasted, etc. Granted that this difference also falls within the object itself, yet for the one only seeing, for the other only hearing etc. is the condition of its awareness and its certainty. But if the object is for me only insofar as I see or hear it etc., and without this is not for me, then it, which at first counted as an immediate, is rather a conditioned and mediated one; and the truth that at first, for sensuous consciousness, lay in the object, whose immediate and unconditioned being and whose being-in-itself in its being for consciousness it expressed, now falls into consciousness itself, into its seeing and hearing etc. -- After this preliminary reflection undertaken by us, this consciousness is now itself to be considered in its own experiences that it makes as sensuous, and it will be shown that it not only makes the same experiences, but also arrives at further determinations that contradict what it wanted and was supposed to be.

§ 43. The truth of immediate certainty can, as noted, be summed up in the proposition: I (i.e. this particular individual) have or know immediately this, this particular object. That this is so and constitutes its truth, sensuous consciousness will also acknowledge, or rather, if one wished to wrest this certainty from it, stubbornly maintain. But the question is what content it has therein, or as what particular and statable determinacy of its consciousness the immediate object, its truth, presents itself to it (cf. §§ 16 and 24); and since a real content of truth cannot only not be lost through being stated, but must also be presentable without the senses and for everyone, including the non-seer, non-hearer, etc., of the meant object, i.e. in and for itself: sensuous consciousness is to be called upon to display its truth, or rather to utter it. It will answer: This is, e.g., a book, a house, a tree, etc.; or: Here is a tree, house, etc.; or finally: Now it is, e.g., noon, 2 o'clock, day, night, etc., and when asked again about the truth of these indications, will confirm them as such. They are thus propositions of which this consciousness avails itself, in which This, Here, and Now are the subjects. That these subjects exhaustively cover the sphere of sensuous certainty -- for us (since this consciousness, for itself, from its lowest stage first rises to this universality in the course of its experience) the necessity of this lies in the fact that "This" signifies the external object in general, while "Here" and "Now" refer to space and time as the elements in which all external objects are situated. Anmerkung. That "This," "Here," and "Now" are called the subjects in the propositions with which sensuous consciousness expresses its certainties -- this will perhaps be granted only of "This" but not of "Here" and "Now," insofar as a proposition with "This" can at the same time be grasped as a judgment, whereas "Here" and "Now" yield mere propositions by which it is by no means meant that the Here and the Now are the object. But these propositions too can become judgments, when, e.g., the question arises whether the object is at this or another place, at this or another time; and judgments such as the following, when someone says in the matter of time, e.g.: The Now is midnight, or also: The Now is confusion, the Here is a vale of pilgrimage, or mortality -- will be found not merely valid, but perhaps also witty or sentimental. Sensuous certainty as such, however, has no judgments at all, precisely because it is only a meaning (intending), and "This is" means for it nothing more than: "There is." If now, e.g., it is said: Here is a tree, this means: At the place that I mean there is a tree; but in the sense in which immediate certainty means it, for it the Here (the here-being) is also the tree; it has mere presence. Precisely in this, however -- that it is brought to reflection on what it says and on the inadequacy of what it says to what it means, and experiences that the Here, the here-being, is also not a tree but everything in general -- i.e. precisely in this, that its mere proposition is raised to the honor of a judgment, consists the beginning of its rational dialectic.

§ 44. The further consideration is now the reflection of consciousness (in substance, it matters not whether its own or another's, or the latter prompting it to make it its own, since sensuous consciousness in general, and the matter in its universality, is to be grasped) upon what it has said, and upon the agreement of what was said with what it meant -- initially from the side of the meant object. Thus: This is, e.g., a tree. (On the use of such a genus name cf. § 41, and below the remark.) But which This? This is for me, for another, or also for the same subject that just said This of the tree, equally well also not a tree, but a house, an animal, etc., and conversely also not-This (namely, another than the meant immediate object) is a tree. "This" is everything in general and holds of everything, whereas it was supposed to serve only for the designation of the quite determinate, particular object distinguished from everything else. The word used is thus the exact opposite of what was meant, or This is rather a completely universal, which as such contains all particular Thises within itself, and, just as each of these particulars is the negation, a not-This, of the other, is the universal negation of all, which are in it only as negated or sublated.

Anmerkung. Pure or mere sensuous certainty can, strictly speaking, avail itself not only of no genus name but of no name at all for the designation of its objects, since every name is already a universal. Whence would it have obtained one? Or, in using one, it is already more than mere sensuous consciousness. (On naming and language in general, see the third, psychological, section.) Let us therefore take a consciousness that is first developing, for which an object upon which it stumbles is still nameless: it will, like the savage whom a new and striking object fills with wonder, only be able to exclaim: There is, there -- and get stuck at the word for what is meant, unless it already utters the quite universal something, and we concede to it the universal "is" and do not restrict it to the mere natural sound of its meaning (intending), such as "There."

§ 45. It is no different with the spatial Here. Already the fact that to the indication and assertion: Here is, e.g., a tree or a table, the question can immediately be asked: Where? i.e. in which Here? -- shows that the designation, which was supposed to be the most determinate for the meant truth, was rather a completely indeterminate and universal one; and if the opposition of another Here is perhaps corrected with the words: Not here but here, then in one utterance the complete contradiction is expressed. Only through pointing with the finger, i.e. sensuously for one who sees or touches, and hence not as something determined in and for itself, can the meant Here be shown; but every other point touched by the finger is likewise a Here that again has a multitude of other Heres around and beside it, each of which is a not-Here of the first, as each is of every other. "Here" is therefore rather "everywhere," every point in space, or the same universal that space itself is, which as universal contains all particular Heres sublated within itself, i.e. negated as determinate particulars.

§ 46. Just as already with This and Here the truths of immediate certainty vanish, and others equally vanishing take their place, in that the determination that was to express their particular truth immediately becomes an untruth and a universal that negates it, into which all return: so this is, for sensuous consciousness itself, even more striking in the case of the temporal Now. If through the Now the immediately present certainty and truth of time is to be indicated -- e.g., Now it is noon, or 2 o'clock -- then this truth has already become an untruth in a few minutes, indeed in the next second, and another Now or the Now of another content has taken its place; and the smaller the point of the present to be determined, i.e. the more determinately and precisely it is to be indicated, the sooner has the certainty meant by the Now turned into untruth. Now is therefore rather not-Now, something past, a then, or rather an always, i.e. an indeterminable quantity and series of particular Nows, each of which, as soon as it is uttered, is already negated and sublated by another. The truly abiding and ever-present, however, is the Now as universal, which has all particular Nows within it, just as time itself is a constant present, or the present is also an ever-present and never-vanishing, or one that, in vanishing and sublating itself (negating), of itself produces and restores itself again, i.e. sublates its being-sublated or negates its negation. Such a negation of negation, however, is precisely the universal.

Anmerkung. Just as the Here develops and expands into space in general, so the Now develops into time already in this experience of consciousness, together with the more precise determination corresponding to the concept of both -- namely, that Here and Now, as particular spatial and temporal determinacies or as discrete points and as vanishing and re-arising negations of universal space and universal time, present negation in general, which does not sublate time and space as such, but in which, as in their quantitative otherness, they rather, according to their infinite nature, restore their universality and continuity, or their self-equality. What time and space are in truth (i.e. in their concept) is, incidentally, not yet a concern of the experience of consciousness here; insofar as they are considered as forms of intuition, this is to be discussed in psychology; as real elements they belong to the philosophy of nature.

§ 47. If we reflect on the experience of sensuous consciousness thus made, as the result of its dialectic so far, it consists in this: 1) that the knowledge of its immediate certainty, in that it wants to utter it in the most determinate way but can in no way accomplish this, is something unsayable and, with the mere wanting to say, also remains only a meaning (intending); 2) that what it actually says of its meant certainty through This, Here, and Now is the opposite and indeed the contradiction of what it wanted to say -- instead of being the most determinate, it is rather the most universal of all; and 3) that when one asks about the truth of immediate certainty that remains for consciousness, all the alleged objects of this certainty have vanished along with their immediate knowledge, or have transformed into others equally vanishing, and, just as something vanishing and perishing has no reality, so too no truth of knowledge and for knowledge can be present where not a single determinacy, much less something determined in and for itself, can be indicated and shown, while the only abiding for consciousness is This, Here, and Now, but not as particulars and as meant, but rather as their negation, or as the universal. (Object in general as a being, space and time.) Anmerkung. That the immediate certainties not only themselves vanish for consciousness, but that no content of knowledge and truth remains in consciousness from them -- this might perhaps be disputed on the ground that the images of external objects are preserved and stored in the representation of the intelligence as the abiding element of intuition. This is correct; and although the images and individual representations too are something transient that passes back into the intelligence and is sublated in it, yet much that has already wholly perished in its external existence, especially everything that appeared only in time and vanished again, is still preserved in the representing intelligence, where it has a more enduring existence -- as proof that already at this lower stage spirit is more and superior to nature. But firstly, everything that one might wish to infer from this for a reality of sensuous certainty accrues not to a being-in-itself truth of its objects but to spirit and its activity as intelligence, which is the subject of psychology; secondly, however, the discussion is not about representations but about knowledge and its truth. For the question about a content of truth for knowledge, it is completely indifferent whether immediate certainty relates to external objects or to their images in representation; the dialectic would be the same, since here too it would first be said: Of the individual images that I have in my representation, this one is a house, this one a tree, etc.; and apart from the fact that representation as such, precisely because it belongs to spirit, already has for itself a character of universality that the individual external object of intuition does not have, no more content of truth would come from the particularities of representation for knowledge than before; but the result, as the universal, would here too be the same. When common reflection speaks of truth and reality of sensuous certainty, it confuses this with the mere factuality of an external presence; but this is as little doubted as the correctness that what is seen, e.g., is a tree and not something else. But it will also immediately be noticed that such correctness is not yet a truth of reason; of such truth, however, philosophy alone treats and inquires. Of the untruth and nullity of sensuous certainty, the whole sensuous world and sensuous pleasure, one finds, by contrast, in many individuals a very deep and developed consciousness, factually and better than in many a philosopher. The vividly realized flight of all earthly things, the insight into the emptiness and poverty of these finitudes that cannot be held fast for any knowledge and any enjoyment, the one and same experience in all experience that nothing true and nothing real remains for the subject from it -- in short, the deep conviction and certainty of the nothing of all these immediate certainties -- has therefore long since turned them away from such reality and directed the drive of their consciousness toward another truth of enduring satisfaction, which no sensuous experience confers. And what does the teaching and precept of religion contain, what does a religious consciousness, a devout heart, recognize and will, other than this?

§ 48. This experience seems at first to be only an experience about language, for which the presentation and indication of the content of immediate sensuous certainty is something thoroughly unattainable, since every word and every expression that is to serve for the designation of an immediately particular contains absolutely something universal that encompasses not merely this particular but many others or all of the same kind and genus. And indeed language is this absolutely universal, which is able to grasp the merely meant and for it unsayable particular only in its universality, and hence also in the form and element of truth, and is able to utter it only in this. It is this, however, because consciousness is the in-itself-universal, or because language is the first immediate existence and the most fully executed work of the subjective spirit itself, which through it gives birth to external existences anew, but in this second existence also confers upon them the character of spirit, the form of universality and truth. The experience made, however, concerns equally the determinate content of consciousness itself, of which it is the negation; just as it also objectively experiences the non-reality, changeability, transitoriness, and nullity of the objects asserted as true and being-in-itself, even though from this side it is primarily only time, according to its particular nature, that forces upon ordinary consciousness the non-reality of what is asserted as true and certain in a so to speak palpable manner. (Cf. the remark to the preceding §.) Considered as content of consciousness, however, of all immediate certainties as particulars there remains absolutely nothing that would be a content of knowledge; the meant and unsayable itself vanishes; what remains and maintains itself through all experience is that by which the meant was to be held fast but could not be held fast -- not as this meant and immediately particular, but as a universal and in-itself-mediated, mediated namely through the negation of all the particulars sublated in it, each of which was a This, Here, or Now.

Anmerkung. Since the word (language in general), the firstborn son of spirit, also in turn bears witness to its father, and therefore, just as also the individual representation, the image of an object, has already stripped off the particular and more contingent element of it and preserves only the more universal, so and still more the word and the name reproduce and express only the universal and abstract representation, so that every expression whatsoever by which a mere particularity of sensuous consciousness, something meant, is to be communicated and designated to the representation of another subject, is immediately unable to accomplish this and utters instead something universal: it would be a completely futile undertaking to try to display and describe, through language, a meant particular object as sensuous certainty has it before itself. For speaking itself has, as Hegel says, the divine nature of immediately inverting the meaning (intending), making it into something other, and so not letting it come to the word at all. The undertaker of such a description, and his object, would therefore sooner themselves no longer be present than even a part of it could be called successful. Everything that would be said of this and only this tree, e.g., would be determinations that hold equally well not merely of every other tree but also of many other objects. One would, however, also soon declare such a beginning to be something trivial, and the knowledge of such unsayable particularities and contingencies, whether they are constituted so or otherwise, to be something very indifferent. To this category also belong the painterly nature descriptions and similar depictions in which bad poets and writers often seek their strength, to which Lessing in the Laocoon already drew attention, even if not initially in the relation here considered. Sometimes the necessary fate of such descriptions is even expressed by their undertakers in a naive manner. For when they make arrangements to deliver a narrative or description of some event important to them that still vividly fills their representation with all its circumstances, but after the attempt made cannot bring it off, they dispatch the tense expectation of their readers or listeners briefly with the declaration that the matter is indescribable and that one must necessarily have seen it oneself: either because the meant is truly something indescribable, or because they have not yet succeeded in bringing what may be truly remarkable and uncommon about it into the shape of what can be said, i.e. to a determinately grasped representation and still more to a thought. It would therefore also be wrong to believe that the part that drops out in the description is the better part of the matter; and when educated people, who otherwise know very well how to capture their thoughts and representations in the determinately designating word, abstain from such talk and from all expressions and exclamations that refer only to being stuck in meaning (intending): they do this probably not merely because it is a futile and absurd endeavor to want to say the unsayable nonetheless, but because this unsayable part of the matter also contains the insignificant, the contingent, and the irrational. For what constitutes the rational and the intelligible in all things must also be determinately statable and sayable; the rest (ò 070) is worth neither mention nor preservation. If objects of nature are of such a constitution that absolutely no other knowledge of them is to be obtained than that which immediate sensuous consciousness has of them, and this cannot be summarized in any determinately expressible representation, then this would be precisely a proof that in this otherwise unknowable and unspeakable element, reason, which is after all presupposed even in nature, is not at home. And nature, besides the fact that it realizes the truly rational and the idea itself in an immediate and external manner, also has in its released element the creative power to bring into existence and drive into appearance, in infinite manifold and particularization, everything possible and also the merely possible -- productions to which by no means a higher value is to be attributed than to the likewise merely possible eruptions, notions, and opinions of the spiritual substance. The merely possible, however, brought to actuality, is the contingent, the merely particular and arbitrary, which, lacking universality and inner necessity, for that very reason also lacks reason. Unfortunately, however, many who wish to conduct the proof of God from nature tend to hold only to the external and sensuous aspect of it, or to a meant abstractum they call nature -- in general to that in which the concept of reason is not to be sought, or is at least not recognized by them. How then do they propose to recognize and prove, from that in which they cannot yet find and demonstrate a single rational thought from nature, the absolute epitome of all thoughts of reason? To what sort of external reflection this inference has fallen prey, to what poverty of individual abstractions it clings in general, cannot be more closely discussed here. The one true and most perfect proof of God from nature, however, would be the philosophy of nature itself in its completed presentation and development. On the chief content of this section and of this remark -- namely, on what is completely worthless and insignificant for knowledge and for scientific determination, and in itself transitory, in the content of sensuous certainty of particular objects as such, as well as on the universal nature of language -- there still deserves, all the more as many are inclined to regard Aristotle as merely a common empiricist clinging to the reality of such certainty, to be cited and read whole and in its wider context a pertinent excellent passage of his, Metaph. VII, c. 15, of which an excerpt follows here. Διὰ τοῦτο δὲ καὶ τῶν οὐσιῶν τῶν αἰσθητῶν τῶν καθ' ἕκαστα οὔθ᾽ ὁρισμὸς οὔτ᾽ ἀπόδειξίς ἐστιν, ὅτι ἔχουσιν ὕλην ἧς ἡ φύσις τοιαύτη ὥστ᾽ ἐνδέχεσθαι καὶ εἶναι καὶ μήν διὸ φθαρτὰ πάντα τὰ καθ᾿ ἕκαστα αὐτῶν. εἰ οὖν ἡ τ' ἀπόδειξις τῶν ἀναγκαίων καὶ ὁ ὁρισμὸς ἐπιστημονικός, καὶ οὐκ ἐνδέχεται, ὥσπερ οὐδ᾽ ἐπιστήμην ὁτὲ μὲν ἐπιστήμην ότὲ δὲ ἄγνοιαν εἶναι, ἀλλὰ δόξα τὸ τοιοῦτόν ἐστιν, οὕτως οὐδ᾽ ἀπόδειξις οὐδ᾽ ὁρισμὸς ἀλλὰ δόξα ἐστὶ τοῦ ἐνδεχομένου ἄλλως ἔχειν, δῆλον ὅτι οὐκ ἂν εἴη αὐτῶν οὔτε ὁρισμὸς οὔτε ἀπόδειξις. ἄδηλα τε γὰρ τὰ φθειρόμενα τοῖς ἔχουσι τὴν ἐπιστήμην, ὅταν ἐκ τῆς αἰσθήσεως ἀπέλθῃ κ. τ. λ. Αναγκαῖον δὲ ἐξ ὀνομάτων εἶναι τὸν λόγον όνομα δ' ου ποιήσει ὁ ὁριζόμενος· ἄγνωστον γὰρ ἔπται· τὰ δὲ κείμενα κοινὰ πᾶσιν. ἀνάγκη ἄρα ὑπάρχειν καὶ ἄλλῳ ταῦτα· οἷον εἴ τις σὲ ὁρίσαιτο, ζῷον ἐρεῖ ἰσχρὸν ἢ λευκὸν ἢ ἑτερόν τι ὁ καὶ ἄλλῳ ὑπάρξει.

§ 49. As such a universal, however, the particular object itself -- be it a spatial or temporal one -- that was first taken only according to its compact unity, also presents itself to consciousness upon closer inspection, insofar as it at its own self again has a multiplicity and manifold of particular determinacies (as extension in space, a quantity of specific Thises and distinguishable Heres; as a larger portion of time, a quantity of successively following Nows) and contains them within itself, of which the particular object is precisely the containing and encompassing universal, as their mediation. From this side, then, the object of immediate sensuous certainty passes more closely into the object of the next following stage of consciousness, that of perceiving. But first, sensuous consciousness is to be considered, secondly, from its subjective side, as reflected into itself, and its experience therein.

§ 50. Sensuous consciousness is first directed at the object, of which it has immediate certainty in the sense that the object is in and for itself even without its immediate intuition; and according to its presupposition, it therefore counts as the most complete and of its truth most certain, because the object itself is taken up immediately into consciousness, as it is, completely and unchanged. But -- to say nothing here of the many sensuous deceptions (illusions of the senses) and of the objectivity that presents itself differently according to the different constitution of the subject, which already compels the subject to the reflection that the supposed truth does not lie in the object alone and for itself, but that it itself is the other necessary condition of truth for the being of the object -- the reflection on the experience made and presented in the foregoing, in particular the reflection on the content present and remaining, or rather vanishing, in knowledge, the unsayability of what is meant, and the inversion of the particular into a universal, has already made consciousness attentive to itself in the contradiction of its meaning (intending) and speaking, and turned it inward.

Anmerkung. As regards the uncertainty and deceitfulness of all sensuous cognition and of the common facts of ordinary consciousness, the elaborate presentation and demonstration of the insecurity of all such cognition belongs here, which the ancient skepticism already furnished in its ten older tropes directed against the dogmatism of common human understanding in general, and grounding the skeptic's withholding (oh) from all holding-for-true and holding-for-certain (Sext. Emp. P. H. I, 14), of which the first four tropes in particular (which Sextus Emp. for that reason also reduced to one) demonstrate this insecurity on the side of the subject. (Cf. also Remark to § 12.)

§ 51. In its self-examination, however, consciousness finds at first the difference that was previously an external and objective one now present in itself -- namely, the difference of the senses, according to which it is one thing that sees, another that hears, etc., and it experiences and recognizes its own determinacy as the condition of its knowledge of the object. (Cf. above § 42.) Thereby, however, the truth previously placed in the object is now taken over by consciousness, and immediate certainty now becomes one conditioned and mediated through the particular determinacy of the I. For me, the object is "this" not in general, not unconditionally and in itself, but for me only as seeing, hearing, tasting, etc. (cf. above Remark to § 25); without this it is not for me. The certainty of the object is therefore for me conditioned and mediated in a twofold way: 1) through the medium of the senses, through which I receive it, 2) through me as sensuous consciousness in general. Only for me as this determinate consciousness is the object "this"; and hence universally in the negative: without sensuous consciousness, i.e. if there were no seeing, hearing, etc. at all, there is also no sensuous world. Thereby, however, what at first counted for consciousness as being-in-itself has sunk to a being of it only for consciousness in this its determinacy (only to a for-it-being of the being-in-itself), i.e. to mere appearance, and the knowledge of it has likewise become only an appearance of knowledge. (Cf. §§ 19 and 20.)

§ 52. Furthermore, however, the particular I, which was first fixed only as this and as an immediate unity, experiences that it itself is also a universal and in-itself-mediated, but thereby also an abiding and through all change self-maintaining. 1) As the knowing for which this and this particular etc. is, it recognizes that in the change and vanishing of all particular objects of sensuous certainty, the I itself does not vanish for consciousness, any more than This, Here, and Now as universal, but rather that it, in its essence untouched by every change and for itself indifferent to every determinacy and external affection, maintains and remains itself through all abstraction and negation of them as the universal into which all those determinacies fall, and therein as pure, simple I. 2) Against the difference of the senses, according to which the I finds a manifold and diverse at its own self, yet in each of which the I knows itself and knows itself in unity with each, it recognizes itself equally well as their universal unity, as it also still distinguishes itself from its senses, and in the negation of the senses and the abstraction from them maintains and recognizes itself for itself as the abiding and unchangeable. 3) Insofar as the I of immediate certainty has itself in mind as only this, i.e. as this determinate, particular I distinguished from all other I's, and as such wants to express and make itself known, it makes the experience that, just as little as it was able to display its meant sensuous object through language, just as little does it succeed in designating its meant I as only this; for "I, this one" is every I, and everything said of it is a universal that holds of all I's. This particular meant I, therefore, which as this was yet supposed and wanted to be a self-subsisting, specific, and distinguishable one, experiences that it does not distinguish itself from others, nor is able to distinguish itself, since every one is such a particular I, and the uttered meant I rather contains all I's: so that on this side too the same thing befalls meaning (intending) as before, and the most determinate of its wanting-to-say turns into the exact opposite -- the immediately particular into the universal.

§ 53. Since thus the dialectic of sensuous consciousness, being not a foreign one but that which it has at its own self, has shown it and us from all sides what the matter stands with its certainty and truth, the object of consciousness too has not remained the former but has become another, which now enters as the object of consideration in place of the former. Instead of the immediately particular from which sensuous certainty began and proceeded, a universal and in-itself-mediated has resulted for us from both the objective and the subjective side of it. This, Here, and Now, which were still distinguished from one another, have, as the same universal that each of them has equally shown itself to be, now themselves no longer any difference from one another. But insofar as they have shown themselves for the content of consciousness as the abiding and self-maintaining, they now, after their indifference itself is gathered into a unity, constitute the objective side of the new object as that universal, just as the I for its part has become a universal. The universal result that we have obtained from the foregoing, as the element of the new object and its consciousness, is therefore in general a universality mediated in and with itself through the negation of the particularities sublated in it, something reflected into itself. With this so altered object, however, consciousness too has altered itself, or conversely, with this so altered knowledge, the object too has become another (§ 18). The consciousness, however, for which the object is thus determined, is no longer the immediate sensuous consciousness, but perceiving.

§ 54. Herewith, at the same time, in the scientific consideration of consciousness, which has to furnish the proof against the untruth of the other non-philosophical modes of knowledge at their own selves, the first untrue mode of consciousness has sublated itself for us altogether and with insight into the ground of its untruth, and through its own negation has at the same time determined itself further of its own accord to the next higher stage. (Cf. §§ 8, 23, and 25.) This transition has arisen through reflection on the positive in the negative, and has thereby become one grounded in the matter itself and necessary. For itself and without insight into the necessity of its dissolution, ordinary consciousness remains abandoned to its untruth and deception and its meaning (intending); it does not know that instead of the richness of a manifold being that it meant, the mere abstraction of being, the very poorest, underlies all its certainties. It does indeed make various experiences, but from them falls back, without further apprehension and pursuit of what is contained therein, into its ordinary mode; or it also comports itself according to another mode of consciousness, without, however, knowing of the one or the other comportment itself, and still less being able to indicate a difference between them. For philosophical knowledge, however, immediate sensuous certainty can contain just as little, and even less, actual truth and reality, as nature itself, the idea of absolute spirit in the element of externality, allows its particular and finite productions to persist as absolute truths, or confers upon them any other than a transient, self-sublating existence, i.e. nothing more than mere appearance. A genuine content of knowledge, even if the name was used, is in general not yet to be found in sensuous consciousness as such in its merely immediate comportment; for what has resulted as the outcome of its dialectic -- the universal -- belongs precisely for that reason no longer to this consciousness but already to the following, whose foundation it constitutes. -- Anmerkung. With respect to natural things and external objects in general, it may further be noted that sensuous certainty, or rather its dialectic and its result, the universal, taken in an objective sense, would have the meaning that it is not in the particular things and individual beings as such -- which are rather the transient -- but only in their universal, in their essence and organism maintaining itself equally through all individuals, in the genera and species, that the true is contained. But the consciousness does not arrive at the knowledge of this universal of the objective side itself until later in its development.

### B. DAS WAHRNEHMEN. — B. PERCEIVING.

§ 55. Perceiving occurs for itself, or in natural consciousness, as an immediate, i.e. for this consciousness not first specifically arisen through mediation and derivation, mode of its comportment toward objectivity and toward the things of the external world, just as other modes of comportment also do; it is, however, the most ordinary mode, and indeed that one in which consciousness, as the etymology also shows, wishes to take the true [Wahr-nehmen] from the things found before it, or to take them as they are in truth. The object itself is thereby again, for this consciousness, the being-in-itself, a self-subsisting truth, where it is only a matter of not taking it otherwise than it is, or as it presents itself, since as it presents itself it will presumably also be constituted in itself. This object, however, is the things, or these grasped universally from the side of their common thinghood, the thing in general -- yet not as it was the object of immediate sensuous certainty (where the name thing is not yet in its proper place either), only as this immediately particular and as a compact simple, but the thing as a universal, insofar as this, though for itself also only one, at the same time presents a distinguishable manifold and plurality. This distinguishable, however, which falls with and at the thing into perception, is the properties of the thing, so that the object of perceiving determines itself as the thing of many properties in general, and everything present, the whole richness of the sensuous world, now falls under this determination. -- Anmerkung. "Everything is a thing." In this extension and universality, the objective essence in general is comprehended under things when one demands of philosophy the solution of the question what things are. The simple answer that, as will be shown, the dialectic and truth of perceiving gives to this is that things are contradictions. If one is not satisfied with this answer, then one must also ask differently and not attribute the character of mere thinghood to beings that are something more than mere things. One can, however, reply to this that one did not take the word so precisely and understood by things objects in general. Now, organic living beings, animals, and human beings are indeed not usually called things, but apart from the fact that they are otherwise often treated as such, the scientific treatment and consideration of them not infrequently lets no better lot befall them either. The human being, one says, consists of body and soul, just as a thing consists of these and those materials or also of other things, whereby already the separation instead of the unity of spirituality and corporeality is expressed. To a mere thing, however, the body indeed becomes, but only when the soul leaves it. Quite properly, however, the former metaphysics made the soul into a thing of many properties, and empirical and also a self-styled rational psychology still does so now, notwithstanding that Kant already has the merit of having put the I in place of the soul-thing. (See on this below in the psychological section.) Indeed, the former metaphysics and, following it, theology even bestowed upon God the honor of making him into a thing, i.e. into a mere collection of many or rather of all, to be sure absolute, properties (also under the name of realities or perfections), and in this unworthy manner, in which one sought to subject the divine essence to representation (for thought destroys it), did not perceive the contradiction in which the divine essence is thereby entangled from all sides, all the more so since precisely the absolutizing of the individual and opposed properties makes the epitome of all realities all the more the epitome of all negations and contradictions. Or, if the contradiction was recognized, the means applied to remedy it (such as the mutual tempering of the properties, which again sublates their absoluteness) only made the evil worse, since the basic representation remained.

§ 56. For us, on the other hand, this new mode of consciousness is not immediate, but has arisen from the perishing of the previous one, hence as one that has arisen through mediation and necessarily, and thereby also the object itself in its essential determinations. For through the dialectic of immediate sensuous certainty, a universal had resulted on both sides -- the objective as well as the I-side -- and indeed a universality mediated in and with itself through the negation of the immediate particularities sublated in it (§ 53). Such a universal, however -- a self-subsisting simple togetherness of many particular Thises, Heres, or Nows -- is precisely the stated object of perception, the thing in the multiplicity and manifold of its properties; and the property, which as a sensuous but determinate being participates in the universality of the thing, is likewise a universal. The universal is thereby the principle of perceiving in general, also in the sense that the object, in order to be taken precisely in its truth, may, after the sublation of sensuous certainty that has perished in its untruth, no longer be taken in its manner, i.e. no longer as an immediately this and particular of mere sensuous sensation.

§ 57. With this, however, the sensuous itself has by no means vanished, since on the contrary the whole of perceiving still has the sensuous as its foundation and proceeds from it. What has changed, however, is the character of the sensuous, or the way in which it is for consciousness. The sensuous is indeed negated or sublated as immediate and particular This, but as this negated and sublated it is at the same time still preserved, i.e. it is still present as a mediated and now valid only in its universality, as will emerge more closely at its object in the course of perceiving. But since, on the other hand, the universal -- as which the object of perceiving, the thing of many properties, presents itself -- is such only because therein consciousness has already gone beyond the immediately sensuous to an essence of it or to something no longer sensuously perceivable, or in other words the universal is already a product of abstraction and reflection: so, just as the object contains a sensuous and also a non-sensuous at once, perceiving also presents itself as a mixture of sensuous and non-sensuous comportment of consciousness. This non-sensuous, however, is initially the intellectual (abstraction and reflection). Precisely for this reason, however, a beginning of knowledge is already made by this consciousness, which was not yet present in immediate sensuous certainty.

Anmerkung. Since in philosophical language that which is designated by the words "sublate" [Aufheben] and "sublated" [aufgehoben] is of the greatest importance, and without it, grasped in its determinate meaning, no step in actual thinking and in true speculative cognition can be taken further, it is necessary to provide a special reminder or explanation about it here. Cf. moreover Hegel's Logik, Vol. I, pp. 45 ff. Already in the language of ordinary representation, the word Aufheben has the twofold sense of meaning as much as putting an end to, no longer letting subsist (tollere), and as much as preserving (servare, condere). Both meanings meet in the sense that they indicate a removal from the midst of external existence, from its connection and its influence -- only the former with the intention of no longer letting the sublated subsist at all, the latter, however, with the opposite intention of all the more securing its being-for-itself (outside of the connection with other). Yet even that which is sublated so as no longer to subsist, such as a law, an institution, a position, is often not entirely removed but steps forth again in another shape, in another mode of its subsistence, into existence; and what is sublated for preservation is thereby often not meant to be taken out of use and circulation forever, but is also to come forth again at another time, in order then perhaps to vanish entirely. Both meanings, whose convergence and kinship shows itself even in their opposed sense, now come together in the sense that the word Aufheben has in philosophy, into one -- yet in such a way that in this unity of both their double meaning can also be distinguished again on two sides, and therein itself an example is given of what is to be explained. What "sublates itself" for thinking, or what becomes something "sublated," is thereby just as little entirely removed or vanished as it is also only something preserved, which would remain as it stood. Rather, it is precisely the previous mode of its subsistence, its immediate validity, that is what is sublated in it, i.e. comes to an end and ceases; and precisely in this, that it does not remain what it was, it is also sublated, i.e. preserved. The sublated has ceased as This, but it is preserved as not-This, as negated, but not as something entirely annihilated through the negation, but only as something negated in this determinate content, wherein precisely for that reason This, the determinate content, is itself also still, only negated, preserved. Or something is valid immediately, as it first presents itself; in this immediate subsistence and being-valid-for-itself it is sublated, it as something immediate; thereby it does not become nothing (nothing is itself something immediate), but it becomes only something mediated, i.e. something that subsists and is valid not for itself but only through another and with another, or only in the relation to another. From its self-subsistence and self-validity it is thereby at the same time demoted to being only a moment and belonging to a higher unity and universality, in which it is sublated and still preserved as a mediating and mediated moment of it. Thus the sensuous This, the meant particular namely, is first an immediate; but through the fact that This is also not-This, an other, then everything in general, it sublates itself as immediate and becomes a mediated, something subsisting absolutely not for itself but only through another and with another, and in this mediation further a universal, in which, as it first was, it is something very indifferent. Likewise the particular Here and Now, which as these vanish in the universal Here and Now (space and time) to complete indifference. But the sensuous as such is not thereby extinguished and vanished; the universal obtained is initially itself still a sensuous universal. Opposites brought into relation or united contradict themselves, and the contradictory sublates itself; the result of the unification, one says, is zero. And therewith for ordinary thinking and representing the matter is settled. But what then has become of the opposites? Where have they gone? Are they now nothing at all, wholly become nothing? Yet the opposed and contradictory exists everywhere, both in external existence, in nature, in states, in moral and social life, and in thinking and representing, which has it for its determinations; and it subsists often long before the contradiction is recognized, and subsists also as contradictory. Since in general everything that presents itself is first a diverse, and the diverse then enters more closely into its determinate difference, but in this becomes an opposed: it can with full right be said: Everything is a contradiction. And it is therefore also contradiction, which one usually dreads and flees like something that really cannot be nor can take place, instead of thinking and recognizing it as what it is -- something so essential and substantial that without it nothing could exist, nor arise, nor subsist, since it is rather the source of all becoming, all life and existence, all stirring and self-movement in the whole spiritual and natural universe. If one wants more specific examples, one need only be reminded of a few simple concepts that everyone has on their lips, such as beginning, end, becoming, arising, perishing, ought, and many others, the analysis of which necessarily presents them as contradictions, since each of them contains being and non-being in a unity. If one seeks to escape the contradiction by declaring everything in which it is found not to be a true being but mere appearance, it is yet present in this, and consequently for the complete removal of it nothing at all has been gained. But let it be found wherever it may: the main question is what it is in and for itself, i.e. it is to be grasped and thought in its determinacy. Yet the contradictory, brought together in its determinate opposition, united or related to unity, does indeed sublate itself. Certainly. But what sublates itself, and what does not? What sublates itself is: 1) the opposed as such, or insofar as it is opposed, i.e. the determinate in which it is an opposed and in which it was supposed to hold itself for itself alone; as this it is negated, but the negation is thereby itself a determinate one, not nothing at all but only the nothing of This, a determinate nothing; but This still has for its content that whose nothing it is (cf.
