Remark to § 17 at the end); 2) the opposition itself sublates itself as the contrary of that in which the two opposites -- which now, both as negated, are the same nothing of their being-for-itself determinacy -- unite, and through their mutual sublation and mediation are now only moments; the result of unification is therefore only zero for them in their determinate validity and opposition, but not for them as moments, which is what they first become, nor for the universal of both, in which they are sublated or are only moments. What therefore 3) does not sublate itself is the unity to which they are related, and which one thoughtlessly is already accustomed to name without further reflecting on it; for this unity first comes about through the sublation of the opposites, and is the universal and higher of them, in which they are now no longer opposed, but also "only" moments. To adduce special examples of this here is superfluous, since the progression supplies them everywhere, as does the whole of philosophy. For ordinary consciousness too there are in daily life, as in all education, examples enough of higher unities and universalities in which former immediacies have perished and occur only as moments, or in which what is otherwise held to be contradictory and self-sublating is found again, but now only as something demoted to indifference; one simply does not reflect on it, any more than on the examples cited above, each of which in its concept contains being and non-being enclosed in a unity.

§ 58. Since, however, for the perceiving consciousness at first the whole truth again falls into its object (§ 55), so that this, which also is as it is without the perceiving and indifferent to it, in itself, appears as the first and essential, while the perceiving I, which has only to take care for a correct perceiving, appears as the unessential: we too have at first, together with this consciousness, to apprehend its object as it perceives it, and to consider its experiences thereby. It need not be further recalled that the consideration is not directed at any particular thing and its properties, but, since all things have thinghood and properties in common, only in this universality can the thing in general and likewise the relation of the properties to and at it be the object of consideration. (Cf. Remark to § 21.)

§ 59. At the thing of many properties as such, considered in its universality, the following presents itself for perception, which it finds and distinguishes at it as belonging to the thing in itself. 1) A togetherness of many properties, inasmuch as in and at the thing this property, also this one, and also this one, etc. is perceived -- each one different from the other, hence each a distinguished and determinate one. These properties subsist quietly alongside one another; none touches, disturbs, or displaces the other; they rather subsist indifferently toward one another, each relating only to itself, or standing in relation with the others only through the thing, by also being a property of this thing and belonging to its unity. The thing is thus the communal Also, i.e. the mere being-gathered-together of these properties, and as the quiet subsistence of them, in which others too might well find room, or as their medium, a quiet, indifferent, passive universality.

§ 60. 2) The thing, however, also presents itself further as simple and one, as such distinguishing itself from other things and excluding other in general. It is thus a simple Here, as sensuous certainty has it; but at the same time also a manifold at its own self, since in the simple Here the many, namely the properties, are together; it is, however, the communal Here of them, since no property has a different Here than any other (sugar, e.g., is not sweet somewhere other than where it is white or yellow, not somewhere other than where it is thus or so crystallinely shaped, where it is soluble, etc.). 3) The many properties themselves, still more particularly -- namely, a) partly in their connection and unity with the thing, and partly also b) individually for themselves as perceived.

§ 61. a) The perceived properties can be properties only through not being something that subsists individually for itself, not themselves being substances (sweetness, e.g., does not subsist for itself and outside the thing, likewise sourness, bitterness, as well as whiteness, color in general, etc. do not subsist for themselves separately), but rather having their subsistence only at the thing, which is their bearer and whose determinacies they constitute; otherwise they would not be properties. They are therefore also so intimately connected with the thing that, although as properties they are distinguished from the thing itself as among each other, they yet absolutely participate in the unity and universality of the thing, and thereby themselves have universality, i.e. extend as far as the thing itself and are everywhere with it at once. Since the properties thus all find themselves together unseparated in one and the same Here with the thing itself, and none has a different place than any other, they also interpenetrate one another at the thing without, however, in what each is as a particular property, touching, disturbing, sublating each other, or passing into one another and being one; rather they are distinguished from one another, and since each is something other than the other, or is not what the other is (negates it), they also mutually exclude one another. And as these determinate properties that they are, they exclude other and the properties opposed to them not merely from themselves but equally from the thing itself, which through them and their determinacy is precisely this determinate thing. For the thing is enclosed in its properties. If, apart from its properties, something else could be perceived in it that would not itself be its universal thinghood, this too would be something particular in it, hence itself again one of its determinacies and properties. Since, therefore, the thing is nothing other than the togetherness of its properties, and through their determinacy is itself this determinate thing and no other, it is also through its properties that simple and exclusive unity through which it itself excludes other from itself and distinguishes itself from other things.

§ 62. b) Finally, however, this too belongs to the perception of what is present. Since the properties themselves are these determinate and other-excluding ones, and in their determinacy and mutual distinctness, apart from each being also a property of this thing, otherwise stand in no contact and relation with one another but rather in what each is relate only to themselves, they are also perceived as such particulars for themselves separately. Here it is found, however -- and the perception would without this finding not be a complete one -- that the properties are indeed present and gathered together in the one perceived thing, but that they equally well also belong not to this thing alone but to other things as well, hence also extend beyond the particular thing, and are free from the connection with this thing as well as from the connection among each other, and as unbound have a particular subsistence for themselves. For inasmuch as one and the same property is also perceived at other things and at these also in other combinations or in combination with other properties, it is consequently bound to this thing no more than to that other; the same property in this thing, however, stands in a much closer relation to the same property in another thing than to every other different one in the same thing, or rather it is, although in different things, yet one and the same, and this unity and equality is its relation. (Sweetness, e.g., as such is the same in different things, or, given existing differences of sweetness, at least more closely akin than anything else; and from these differences too the undifferentiated one and pure sweetness, as e.g. sugar substance, can be found.) Thereby, however, the equal properties gathered from different things present themselves to perception as self-subsisting free matters or substances (stuffs) -- sweetness, bitterness, sourness, the electric, the magnetic, caloric, etc.

§ 63. Through the perception and distinction of all these successively enumerated moments, the perception of what is present is completed in general. If anything should be found not to be correct, i.e. should be found to be such that it seems it absolutely cannot be so, then according to the presupposition of the perceiving consciousness, for which the object itself is the being-in-itself truth that is only to be taken in its truth, the incorrectness cannot fall within the object; the error can lie only in the perceiving consciousness, which seems then not to have apprehended the object, which it wished to apprehend as it is in itself or in truth, in the way it is. But if something in the perceived strikes consciousness as incorrect, as something that necessarily cannot be so, it will not take offense at what has consistently presented itself as the one and the same in the course of perception; the incorrectness will rather be for consciousness that which becomes unequal to itself in perception, loses its agreement with itself, and contradicts itself; for the true cannot contradict itself. In this, therefore, consciousness will also have the standard guiding it for testing the correctness of its perception; this criterion of truth will be self-equality or freedom from contradiction.

§ 64. Since, then, for the perceiving consciousness the object itself is only the true, hence also the one, self-equal, contradiction-free, then if contradictions are found in the thing of perception, they cannot be present in it itself: they must rather fall outside the thing, i.e. into the perceiving I; the I has to take them upon itself and to alter and improve its incorrect procedure accordingly. Now the entire course of perception and the moments distinguished therein do in fact contain nothing but a series of contradictions, which a closer consideration immediately makes noticeable and highlights, and which the perceiving consciousness too, as soon as they are pointed out to it, will not deny. Contradictory, however, is called that which would be this and not-this, or its opposite, in a unity. If therefore questions such as the following were put to this consciousness: Whether something can be one and also not-one, unity and plurality in one; whether something excluding other and at the same time penetrated by this other, or the one together with its excluded other in one and the same place unseparated at once; or excluding other and at the same time in community with it; whether something can be a self-subsisting and also a not-self-subsisting, an independent and a dependent at once, etc.: then these questions would no doubt not merely be denied, but perhaps it would also be maintained that something so contradictory cannot exist at all. But precisely these contradictions are to be found in the perception that has been made, as the closer consideration shows.

Anmerkung. If everything in which a contradiction can be demonstrated were immediately either nothing at all, a non-being, or at least something untrue: then ancient skepticism, which was at pains to point out contradiction everywhere, and precisely for that reason abstained from every dogmatic assertion and renounced all holding-for-true and all cognition of truth, would long since have carried the victory over all philosophy of its time as of all the future, and would have sufficiently ensured that none could hold out against its weapons and attacks. This is something that those philosophers of our time who behave so fastidiously and delicately toward contradiction do not seem to consider -- as though it were really nothing at all, least of all something belonging to the essence of any object, and therefore also, wherever a contradiction arises, immediately stand at the end of their wisdom and science, or also fall into all sorts of hypotheses in the attempt to circumvent and avoid it. Everything that such philosophy, in particular all dogmatism of empirical and common consciousness, puts forward has already been refuted by ancient skepticism, and the former can produce nothing that would be able to maintain itself against the tropes of the latter, especially the five later ones. One must therefore concede the contradictions (especially the one opposed to all dogmatism: i. Abyą Abyav isov derintisini) to skepticism, but in conceding them must not again commit the new one-sidedness of placing them merely in the subject. The error of skepticism consisted rather in this, that it remained standing only at the contradiction and its negative result. It is therefore unable to do anything against (genuine) philosophy, which does not remain standing there, but rather knows how to recognize and hold fast the positive in the contradictory itself, and presses through the contradiction to unity and truth (cf. Remark to § 57): whereby alone actual philosophy and speculation becomes possible. Remarkable, however, how ancient skepticism, even in holding fast to the contradiction, according to which everything was only appearance for it, yet remained so faithful to its principle and character of being in no way dogmatic, that it treated the appearance of contradiction likewise only as appearance, and guarded itself against every dogmatic assertion of it. Sextus Empiricus (P. H. I, c. 29) therefore censures, e.g., Aenesidemus for saying that the skeptical formation or treatment (774; for so skepticism preferred to call itself rather than a proper philosophical school or sect, aiparis, cf. Sext. l. l. c. 8) is a path to Heraclitean philosophy, because the (for the skeptics valid) appearance of contradiction or, as this is here briefly and aptly designated, of the opposed in one (tò ravavría nepì, Tò aûrò quívesSai) precedes the (by the Heracliteans asserted) being of contradiction (tò ràvavría nepi Tò uûTò bπúpz); for the latter, says Sextus, is a dogma, while the appearance of the contradictory, to which the skeptics held, is a mere fact (pa) which presented itself not only to them but also to the other philosophers and to all human beings equally well; so that, with such a commonality, skeptical philosophy could no more than any other, and than common experience itself, be called a guide to Heraclitean philosophy. Such facts of consciousness, however, were for the skeptics by no means, as nowadays for many philosophers for whom actually contradictory things do not seem contradictory, an undeniable certainty and truth, but only facts of appearance. And since for the expression of the uncertain, that it is uncertain, they availed themselves of certain catchwords (ai Exentinai pavać), such as: One no more than the other, Nothing is to be determined, Everything is false (οὐδὲν μᾶλλον, οὐδὲν ὁριστέον or ὁρίζω, πάντα ἐστὶ prod), etc., but these were themselves thrown back at them by the dogmatists as a dogma: they deflected this by subjecting these determinations, with which they wished to determine nothing, likewise to mere appearance and to the same uncertainty as everything else, and by saying of them that they include themselves in their own sense and content. (Sext. l. l. c. 7.) -- Still finer, but for that reason also less significant, distinctions did skepticism seek between itself and academic philosophy, with which, especially the doctrine of Arcesilaus, as Sextus himself confesses (c. 33, 232), it agreed in substance, and against which it also did not contend. Cf. the treatise already cited above, Remark to § 12, in Schelling's and Hegel's Journ. d. Phil. Vol. I, Pt. 2, pp. 22 ff.

§ 65. First of all, this contradictory concerns the opposed perception, according to which on the one hand the multiplicity and distinctness of the properties constitutes the first and predominant element of the consideration: in which case the thing is only a togetherness or medium of the many, but not a pure, exclusive unity: on the other hand, however, the thing, insofar as it is this determinate one, distinguishing itself from others, is perceived as such a unity. For if it is really a self-subsisting and compact one and through its determinacy excludes other, as it first gives itself as one and distinguishes itself in the most determinate way from others: then it cannot at its own self be a diverging and distinguished plurality of properties; just as little can these then be a universality extending beyond the particularity of the thing (also belonging to other things), whereby the thing, instead of distinguishing itself from other things, would rather stand in community with them. But if the objective of perception is such a community, the determinate nature of the property contradicts this, since through it the property, distinguishing itself from others and thereby also negating the community (continuity) with other, excludes other and the opposite from itself and from the thing whose property it is. But if the object is accordingly really again an exclusive one, simple unity and simple Here: how can it then at its own self be also a manifold and separated of properties? Or if these properties perceived in it are in a unity with the thing and in one and the same Here, how can they distinguish themselves, how can the one exclude the other? But if each property is really a determinate one for itself and thereby distinguishing itself from the others, without however the being of other properties subsisting with and beside it in the same thing being touched by it, rather comporting itself indifferently toward it: how is it then possible that they mutually interpenetrate and that the one is entirely there where the other also is? But if one holds to the view that the properties, since they are not bound to this thing either, being equally present in other things and also distinguishable for themselves alone, are self-subsisting universalities: then the thing is also not an exclusive self-subsisting one, but only a communal togetherness, a universal medium of properties, at most a surface enclosing them, and the properties are then rather matters.

§ 66. Or, in this latter respect, the properties considered more closely both for themselves and in their relation to one another and to their thing: it was shown that each property, as it is perceived individually for itself, is also a particular for itself that stands in no relation either to the other properties or to other in general, and rather comports itself indifferently in its mere self-relation. This property, whichever it may be -- e.g., of the determinate taste, the determinate shape or color, weight, etc. -- is of no concern to the other properties; it is and subsists whether the others are or not; they could also be other or differently constituted, even the opposed, and toward this too it is indifferent for itself. But if it really were such an indifferent, self-relating being, then it would neither distinguish itself as a determinate from others, nor would it be a property at all any longer. For what distinguishes itself as something determinate has its distinction only through not being the other, negating it in itself and excluding it from itself, and thereby stands in a determinate (negative) relation to this other. But it is a property only through the fact that, while it does not subsist for itself but has its subsistence at the thing, it is yet as pure universality joined together with the thing as exclusive one, and through its determinacy, through which it excludes the property opposed to it from the thing itself, first makes the thing into the exclusive one, i.e. into the thing.

§ 67. What is perceived individually for itself must therefore rather be determined as a property; but if it is this, then through the medium of the thing to which it belongs it also stands in relation and contact with the other properties of the same thing, and is with these, as with the thing itself, contained in an inseparable unity. It is, however, a determinate property, i.e. it is neither its opposite, which it excludes from the thing as from itself, nor is it what the others are, which for their part are likewise determinate properties. But what it is not, what it negates and from which it distinguishes itself in order to be for itself the determinate property that it is, this it also excludes from itself and from the unity with itself in its one and in its Here; consequently, two or more different properties cannot be together and at once in one and the same one or Here either, but each is only for itself what it is. Then, however, the property is equally little in one unity and universality with the thing -- which is precisely what makes the thing a thing. The thing is rather as such dissolved; it is no longer a unity and a one, but a mere togetherness, an external collection of isolated sensuous matters; the property, however, is then equally no longer a property either, but rather a matter, a perceived sensuous being that is only "this."

Anmerkung. It has indeed happened, namely with such philosophy as deals merely with representing, that one found nothing contradictory in the thing and in its relation to the properties and of the properties among each other -- and indeed because it presented no difficulty at all to represent the thing as it is. If representing were already thinking, one would have to congratulate this philosophy on being able to think the contradictory. As it is, however, it has it in representation without knowing it, or without taking it for something contradictory. Such philosophy thinks that if it can only represent something to itself, it already has the truth, and everything is in order; truth, however, is for it what is present; if one can only be certain of really translating this, as it is, through intuition into representation, then the subject is also assured of the truth; if only representation is not expected to represent the thing, say, as white and black in a unity (where it could at least be gray) or as sweet and bitter at once (as, however, e.g. honey tastes), then, since the thing itself unites different properties in itself, representation has no difficulty with it at all. Herbart, who as a sharper thinker finds the true real neither in common reality, nor has anything to do with the common idealism which, in its transformation of things into mere appearances, leaves what is to be explained just as unexplained as before, and who as a thinker also otherwise commends himself in many a trenchant critique of other philosophy and unphilosophy and in the uncovering of their weaknesses and defects in a noteworthy manner, has, as in other subjects -- such as change, motion, the I itself -- so also in the thing found and acknowledged the contradiction, and developed it (see his Einleit. in die Philosophie, §§ 97 and 101) especially from the side of the plurality of marks or properties opposed to the unity of the thing. The thing itself is there still treated as the unknown possessor of the several properties, or the actual What of it as something unknowable. We miss here the observation or special highlighting of the contradiction contained in the unseparated togetherness and simultaneity of the different properties in one and the same place or Here, as it was called in the text, and which, as is well known, gave occasion for the invention of the in-itself bad and also unproven hypothesis of the porosity of bodies. Since Herbart indeed speaks of a compulsion to think the thing as a group or complexion of its properties, but denies the sensuous perception of a bond holding them together: the aforesaid local or spatial unity would have been suited to make the sensuous perceivability of such a bond evident to him as well -- unless he actually intends to let the difference of sensuous experience made with different senses at one and the same thing really diverge entirely, and accordingly still intends to dispute, as something already given through sensuous perception, the cognition that others would straightaway take as sensuous certainty or as a fact of consciousness -- namely, that, e.g., what is seen as white is not only one and the same but also in one and the same place as what is tasted as sweet, or felt as thus and so. Besides this unity of place and connection (urà tò szés), the unity of the thing also shows itself, even for sensuous perception, according to quantity (xarà rò mosov) and according to motion (uvrxes i. q. v, oũ xingais pía a aró, Arist. Metaph. V, 6); and the diversity and hence the ground of contradiction concerns, to continue in Aristotelian determinations (ibid. 4), only quality (ò oby). When now Herbart (Remark to § 101) further observes: "It seems that no one came closer to finding this somewhat hidden (?) contradiction than Aristotle," in that this namely "emerges fairly clearly from several obscure, probably very corrupt, or also carelessly written passages of this author, e.g. Metaph. VII, cc. 1-7 and 13": this will, firstly, hopefully not be understood as though no one else had discovered the contradiction in the thing. Secondly, however, it does not seem to us at all doubtful that Aristotle -- who made not merely this or that opposite, like earlier physicists, but opposition and contradiction in general into a principle of natural things and of all motion and change; who further, despite all distinction of matter or the substrate and form (ró sidos), and of form itself again within its various determinacies, yet held the thing fast as one substance; and who resolved still quite other contradictions and brought opposites to unity: and who shortly before (L. V. πepi tõv noddaɣõs deyouévov), as the various modes of unity, identity, etc., so also of opposition and contradiction, as everywhere, with a wealth of determinations, distinctions, and examples enumerated -- that Aristotle was therefore also in the case of this contradiction, standing on a rather low level, somewhat more than only very close, since he has the matter not merely in these chapters but also in the following chapters and books and everywhere. If in the indicated passages he is in many places so close to the supposed discovery of this contradiction that one need only add: But this is, or So the thing is, a contradiction; and yet leaves the pronouncing of this finding to others: this is probably because Aristotle here is already further beyond the contradiction (namely in terms of the higher matter), and inasmuch as he, alongside many excellent speculative and idealistic determinations (e.g., c. 7, on the unity underlying even the opposed: καὶ γὰρ τῶν ἐναντίων τρόπον τινὰ τὸ αὐτὸ εἶδος, and on the immaterial substance as essence in itself: λέγω δ᾽ οὐσίαν ἄνευ ὕλης τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι), entirely separates the unessential constitution (xarà supßeß7x65) from the essence of substance, but takes the essential into the unity of its essence and concept and draws it in -- he is rather occupied with, so to speak, not letting the contradiction break out at all, and has already, in Herbart's expression, corrected it through thinking. This occurs especially in the following chapters, rich in important content -- especially cc. 10 and 11 and others -- in which Aristotle endeavors to present the form or species (rò sidos) as the true substantial essentiality (oùsiz) and as the actual being-in-and-for-itself (τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι, αὐτὸ καθ᾽ αὑτό) of things, whereby the mere matter and the various, changeable, and perishable material constitution sinks greatly in value for him. It is not the place here to set forth the whole doctrine of Aristotle, for which a special treatise would be required, but only to show that he was quite well acquainted with the contradiction in question; since in any case the cited chapters, in which Aristotle is more concerned with the universal determinations falling within the concept of six and their unification and mediation (to which also belongs the investigation of the (only abstractly grasped by him) universal, rò xalov, begun in c. 13 but only concluded with c. 16, and the whole polemic against the Platonic Ideas, without Aristotle's own determinations thereby bearing less the character of ideality and pure speculation), are not even the actual source for what matters here. To be sure, there is found, after a brief touching upon in c. 11, right at the beginning of c. 12, where Aristotle says that he herewith supplements the doctrine of his Analytics concerning definition and division, already the whole matter; for the difficulty (anopia) there discussed -- how a being that is to be defined according to its concept, and in which different things can be distinguished, is one and not many (διὰ τί τοῦτο ἐν ἐστιν ἀλλ' οὐ πολλά;) -- is surely nothing other than the contradiction. And further below, where the question is repeated, it says the diverse many is not one just because it is found in the one (¡àρ бтɩ èvʊжάρxie), for so everything could become one. Since, however, Aristotle has here initially only the diversity of species in relation to the unity of the genus in view, and the resolution runs toward the correct subordination, division, and subdivision according to the doctrine of logic that through and after Aristotle has become the ordinary and general one: one can hold that what is meant is not yet contained therein; and indeed the unity of the genus and the unity of the individual itself, which has its genus and species in itself, is a much higher one than that of the mere thing. Likewise, in c. 14, in the investigation directed against the Ideas and against those who let the species consist of the genus and of the differences, about the universal, the contradiction between the unity and determinate kind and constitution simultaneously present in one (in the passage where it says: àðúvaróv τι συμβαίνει τἀναντία γὰρ ἅμα ὑπάρξει αὐτῷ, sc. τῷ ζῴῳ, ἑνὶ καὶ τῷδέ τινι ὄντι) is quite clearly expressed, and Aristotle rejects the explanation through composition, connection, or mixture as inadmissible. And many more such passages could be cited. What more closely concerns the contradiction of our subject, however, is already touched upon by Aristotle at the end of c. 13, where, in asserting that substance (usi), as self-subsistence namely, if it is one, cannot consist of other substances contained in it (and indeed in the sense of the Democritean proposition that out of two it is impossible for one, or out of one for two, to come to be), he also comes to speak of the analogous relation in number, insofar namely as every number is itself to be regarded as a unity of the arithmetical ones contained in it, as a s provάdov according to some; for either the two (ŉ duάs) is no unity, or the one (os) is not in it with true actuality (vтeλeɣsią); further, at the close of the seventh book, and then throughout the whole eighth book, in which Aristotle deals with the individual sensuously perceivable and material things and their apprehension and definition, and at once in the first two chapters with his customary precision and care supplies an enumeration of them according to all distinctions and categories, also according to being through mere composition or mixture, but also in several places gives not obscurely to recognize the impure, unequal, conditioned, and contradictory character of their perishable material nature, in comparison with the true, not through another outside them conditioned, their being and unity immediately (ús) having in themselves, self-subsisting essences, whose name some things perhaps do not deserve at all, or only as an analogue (c. 2, and c. 3, in the middle), especially still in the last chapter. Since now the Herbartian contradiction in the thing, whose discovery Aristotle is supposed to have come closest to, limits itself to the mere plurality of marks or properties against the unity of the thing -- an abstraction whose poverty for itself, however much it occurs in Aristotle's cited passages on almost every page, yet falls short against the inner richness and vitality of the conceptual determinations otherwise applied by him to things, of possibility and actuality or activity, substance, form and matter, etc. -- and since, moreover, each property contained in the thing counts only like a numerical one: this is absolutely nothing other than what Aristotle says about number in comparison with definition (µò; and 26705), a chief passage for which is to be found at VIII, c. 3 toward the end, where among other things it says: καὶ τὸν ἀριθμὸν δεῖ εἶναί τι ᾧ εἷς, ὁ νῦν οὐκ ἔχουσι λέγειν τίνι εἷς, εἴπερ ἐστὶν εἰς· ἡ γὰρ οὐκ ἔστιν ἀλλ᾽ οἷον σωρός, ἢ εἴπερ ἐστί, λεκτέον τί τὸ ποιοῦν ἐν ἐκ πολλῶν. καὶ ὁ ὁρισμὸς εἰς ἐστίν. τοῦ αὐτοῦ γὰρ λόγου, καὶ ἡ οὐσία ἂν οὕτως, ἀλλ' οὐχ ὡς λέγουσι τινες οἷον μονάς τις οὔτα ἢ στιγμή, ἀλλ' ἐντελέχεια καὶ φύσις τις Exάsty. x. T. 2. Precisely the same occurs again in c. 6, where the difficulty mentioned at the outset regarding the contradiction and the unity nevertheless required for the matter as for the definition is once more set forth, and against others who do not know how to resolve it is settled in genuinely Aristotelian fashion through the distinction of matter and form (poppň), possibility and actuality (ivépysız), so that -- οὐκέτι ἀπορία δόξειεν ἂν εἶναι τὸ ζητούμενον. For even if the determinations falling together into one form a new plurality, they are yet such as get along very well together and in substance constitute a unity in the concept, in the byos EvomoLÓS (c. 6 at the end). Likewise, in the cited passages and in several others (such as VII, 13 at the end: ἀσύνθετον ἂν εἴη ουσία πᾶτα, similarly VII, 17: ἐπεὶ δὲ τὸ ἔκ τινος σύνθετον οὕτως ὥστε ἓν εἶναι τὸ πᾶν, ἀλλὰ μὴώς τωρὸς ἀλλ' ὡς ἡ συλλαβή, κ. τ. λ.), the determinations in our dialectical development of perceiving opposed to each other as the two extremes of abstraction -- the thing as one and the mere togetherness, which Aristotle calls a heap, copis -- are quite clearly to be found expressed. The example chosen by Aristotle (VII, 17) for comparison, however -- the syllable -- supplies, just as Herbart answers the question "What is the thing?" with the sum of the properties through a, b, c, likewise the laying-out of the syllable into its components, the elements or letters, yet with the remark that the syllable itself is not the letters, nor is b and a the same (consequently different), just as little as flesh is fire and earth, if these primal substances are taken as its components. The binding and something-as-one-presenting unity, however, in which the components do not remain what they are outside it, and which we later call a negative unity, is in Aristotle activity or actuality, vépy, and in a higher sense entelechy. The question about the unknown What of the thing, in distinction from its external, perceivable constitution, is also not left untouched by Aristotle. For when he comes to speak (VIII, 3, in the middle) of the still uncertain matter whether the substances of perishable things are also something self-subsisting (xwpistai, a thing in itself), and observes that one would probably wish to accept nature itself alone as the true substance in perishable natural things, he adds that such uneducated people as the Antisthenes school are, however, helped here somewhat in the difficulty they create for themselves, when they say that the What of the thing cannot be determined (though only for the reason that determining is a verbose affair, yos paxpbs), but only its "constitution" can actually be demonstrated. After all this, one may well still doubt, and must doubt, whether in Aristotle there already existed as developed a consciousness about opposition and contradiction and about the whole of logic and metaphysics as is to be found, e.g., in the Aristotle of our time, i.e. in Hegel. Aristotle wished just as little as Herbart and other philosophers in general to let the recognized contradictions stand as absolute truths. But that in his "ontological attempts, which absolutely should not be despised as useless subtleties," as Herbart further says (Does Herbart seriously believe he has gotten further than Aristotle, or even as far?), "he was of course little disposed to recognize the contradictions hidden therein," as the "naive question" to the Eleatics is already supposed to show: διὰ τί ἀκίνητον, εἰ ἔν;... ἀλλοίωσις διὰ τί οὐκ ἂν εἴη; (Phys. I 4, perhaps 3) -- this we cannot help regarding as an assertion that is refuted not only by the foregoing, but on almost every page of Aristotle's philosophical writings and in every speculative investigation of his, and which should least of all be advanced against this philosopher without precise and complete knowledge of his philosophy and philosophical method -- a philosopher who had precisely his chief strength in grasping every subject for itself in the most circumspect way from all sides, in analyzing the concrete particular and comprehending it in its determinacy, and who lived in and after a time in which, since the Eleatics, the occupation with the everywhere-tracked-down contradiction was at home not only in the schools of the philosophers but had, through the Sophists and Eristics, even passed into general education. Aristotle, who therefore rather here too, as everywhere, proceeded -- i.e. considered and scientifically treated opposition and contradiction in and for themselves, distinguishing four forms or categories of the opposed (cf. further M. X. 3) -- saw himself in his deeper speculations everywhere, and beyond the abstract theory of his own logic, compelled to express of one and the same the opposed determinations with equal value. How else could he have made, in his Physics and Metaphysics and elsewhere, the deep insights that few have replicated, or even understood? Since, moreover, what Aristotle says about motion and change (Phys. III-VIII) belongs to the profoundest things ever said on the subject, the question to the Eleatics, initially to the somewhat crude (poprixos) doctrine of Melissus, is probably not to be called naive -- such as Aristotle probably never posed at all. After he has already, at the end of Phys. I. 2, censured the misunderstanding arising from the monadic conception of the one, as though one could not also be many, he adds to his questions, even in passing, trenchant reasons. "The One -- why as such immovable?" Motion within does not sublate the unity of the whole. "Why unchangeable?" But through the very fact that the one is determined according to a kind or form (side) (and the Eleatics had a series of predicates that they asserted of their unmoved and abstract one), it is already an other, an opposite (this already as mere one, which does not exist without the many), has therefore the principle of change already at its own self, and as one is also many. For this is probably Aristotle's meaning, who reproaches the Eleatics for not seeing, in their obstinate and immovable adherence to their rigid abstraction of the one, the distinctions they themselves had made, and for not knowing how to bring together the thoughts they already had (συμπεραίνειν). "But this," Aristotle adds at such a distinction that he demonstrates to Parmenides (whose doctrine he also investigates deeply and thoroughly in what follows), "Parmenides of course had not yet seen." Who then was the greater -- the one who obstinately holds fast the rigid and unmoved one, which is for that very reason already the hardest contradiction, or the one who makes this rigid thing fluid through thinking and thereby frees it from contradiction? In believing ourselves to owe Aristotle this, albeit for a remark somewhat lengthy, vindication and clarification, there follows from it of itself his importance for philosophy, as well as the wish, which we share with Herbart, that his works may find the deserved effort and an industrious working-through -- if not precisely for the reason of the somewhat biased view of providing a counterweight, however necessary, to the influence of Plato and Spinoza in today's philosophy, but rather so that a thorough study of philosophy may be more generally awakened and promoted again. But if the efforts of the philologists are first to make the text of Aristotle readable for us through purification and elucidation, we might sooner expect a new dozen from those writers of antiquity whose editions already exist by the dozens, than a complete working-through of Aristotle. For without philosophy, philology alone will help little here, and the latter can conversely expect elucidation rather from the philosophers. But there is also not so extraordinarily much that is unreadable that a philosopher could not even now fathom Aristotle.

§ 68. In this circle of contradictions, in which the dialectic of perceiving moves, the opposite has been repeatedly predicated of the thing as well as of the property. With the result, however, at which it finally arrived -- the sensuous being that is only "this" -- perceiving would at the same time have come out of the true as well as out of perceiving, and would have fallen back to its previous stage, mere sensuous certainty; consciousness would therefore have to begin the whole circuit anew. But before we consider it further in its behavior in this and in its further experience, it is appropriate here to reflect for ourselves first on the perceiving consciousness and its object. As a result of the dialectic of sensuous consciousness, a universal emerged; this universal is the principle of perceiving in general, and the essential for us. As such, as a sensuous universal, the object too presented itself, which constitutes the essential for the perceiving consciousness, while it regards itself as the unessential in the matter, since it only wants to take the object in its truth. But the perceiving I is the very same universal, as emerged above (§ 52), and perceiving as consciousness and the object of perceiving are therefore not different in essence. The difference between the two consists only in this, that the former is the same universal as movement of displaying and laying-out, the latter the same as a simple; the former the successive exposition of it in the unfolding and distinguishing of its moments, the latter the aggregate of the same moments gathered into a unity. Perceiving consciousness accordingly experiences only what it itself is.

§ 69. We find consciousness first determined through its object. But conversely, its object is that as which it itself is determined; only as perceiving does it have the object as it has been considered, so that here too only both taken together -- consciousness and object -- constitute the true whole object for us (§ 23). If we now reflect on the content of knowledge present in consciousness, which it has of its thus-determined object, or if we ask what it knows when it knows its object thus, then this content, grasped in its determinacy, is nothing other than the stated universal, which, however, from this side and in its determinate appearance for this consciousness, is still to be considered more closely.

§ 70. Perceiving has to do with something that it calls a thing. But "thing" is something completely universal, which as such has no (external) existence at all. For everything is a thing, and in that something is a thing, none is different from any other. What has existence is only this and that, a particular and determinate. Even if "a" thing, i.e. something one calls that, is pointed out, "the" thing can in no way be pointed out. The thing, therefore, of which perceiving speaks, is present only as the consciousness's own product, as abstraction, as universal representation, or as something thought, a concept of the understanding, and only in consciousness. What perceiving initially (i.e. in sensuous experience) has to do with is merely a something, a being, which presents itself in different ways for different senses or lets different determinacies be perceived at it -- for sight, e.g., color and shape; for hearing, tone or sound; for taste, sourness, bitterness, etc. That these determinacies are called properties is, just like the thing itself, again something completely universal, not something present as such. Neither "the" property, which is a pure universality, nor even the particular properties can truly be pointed out. The truly or immediately present is only the completely particular sensuously-felt, "only this," something meant, unsayable, yet designated by the name of a universal. If perceiving therefore says, e.g.: salt is bitter or sour, foliage is green, or also: a property of salt is bitterness, of foliage the green color, then it immediately commits an untruth for sensuous certainty, because not bitterness, greenness in general, but only this determinate salt-bitterness, leaf-greenness is present, or still more determinately only that which is just sensuously felt. And even this is for the subject present only in its particular sensuous determinacy -- the bitter not for the seeing, the green not for the tasting, as all this has already emerged in the treatment of sensuous certainty. But if under the stated property the perceiving consciousness understands more than what is immediately sensuously present -- e.g., bitterness in general -- then it has thereby also taken up the particular under a universal, which as such is neither found nor felt nor sensuously perceived (though taken in its truth). Inasmuch, then, as perceiving speaks of thing and properties, these are pure universalities that also fall purely into consciousness itself, and that consciousness, instead of finding them outside itself, rather brings along to perceiving, and brings them along because as perceiving it is itself already determined to be a universal.

§ 71. Since the perceiving consciousness, then, instead of dealing with a sensuous material, actually deals only on the occasion of this sensuous material with its own determinacies, which it merely perceives out of its object in objective form -- but in doing so makes the experience that the thing of perception turns for it into a thing of deception: for us, too, partly that content of knowledge will be recognized in its determinate import (cf. § 30), partly the given of the object will be compared with it, and partly the reason will thereby also be found why the thing deceives its perception. The universality mediated with itself through negation, which constitutes the determinacy of consciousness and its actual content, has already been called above (§ 53) something reflected into itself. The universal, considered in its simplicity and apart from the difference that it has in itself as something diverse and manifold, is the simple self-equal; this constitutes its abstract identity. In its inner difference, however, in the particularities and diversities sublated in it (the particular Thises, Heres, Nows, the special determinacies of the I), it is reflected into these, an other to itself. The universal is here in a determinacy, a specific, a particular; this determinacy is the negation of the universal, but the particular, the This that this determinacy is, sublates itself as such, or has rather already sublated itself, and has itself become a universal. The negation that it is, is therefore its own negation, through which it returns into the universal, or the universal rather returns into itself, and restores its self-equality through the sublation of its otherness (negation of negation). The universal is thus through its negation self-equal, mediated with itself through its negation, or receives itself back from its negation, from its otherness. This universality is, like its identity, the concrete and true one.

§ 72. Or, if one begins from the individual determinacies, which at the same time outwardly, as a being for an other (for external influence, for a perceiving consciousness), are an external manifold: then their being and subsistence, insofar as they as such determinacies have no subsistence of their own for themselves but are only moments in the whole, is reflected inward from its external side, and taken back into the also in them self-equal unity and universality. These determinacies can indeed be grasped as such for themselves; but since they are only self-negating moments, they have their significance and truth only in the whole, in the universality in which they are rooted and in which they participate. As such a concrete universality as the one set forth, a property -- which, however, is then no longer a property but a self-subsisting matter or free existence in general -- can indeed be considered, insofar as through all the differences that fall within it or rather proceed from its determinate essence, it maintains itself as the same universal, as e.g. color remains color through all color-differences. Not in the same way, however, is the "thing" such a universality. In relation to its determinacies, the properties, it is not genus, not even "ground" of them, and on this distinction also rests the experience of perceiving, which as such has the thing for its object, while the consideration of that higher universality also belongs to a higher consciousness.

Anmerkung. If one looks first at experience, then many properties of things can indeed be treated as free existences, if and when they are freed from their bondage in the thing and presented more for themselves. There belong here a quantity of substances, juices, extracts, essences, etc. But even so they are not completely free, but only brought into another combination, and are therefore also again things of several properties -- only that what was essentially at stake in their artificial preparation and processing, in a determinate respect, also constitutes the predominant element in them and is regarded as the essential, while the remainder, what is still there besides, or what has the predominant property or substance as its bearer, as its mere matter (7) -- such as the viscous and the fluid -- is regarded in the same respect as the unessential. On the other hand, our physics also has to do with many substances -- among which, besides the so-called substances or matters, e.g. the electric, also several so-called fluids, aether, and the like belong -- which as such are mere hypotheses. When in the explanation of many phenomena one cannot separate oneself from the representation of something stuff-like and wishes to avoid having thought-things in the good sense, one comes to having in the assumed matters the exact opposite, namely mere thought-things in the bad sense of the word, i.e. only hypotheses. Nor is it to be overlooked that so many components of things separated out by chemistry, which through the separation first become what they are -- namely, parts of something that subsisted -- are only the dead, by no means any longer what they were in the living and natural connection in the thing, in its vipya, and so also nowhere exist independently. Aristotle, who for the general designation of that property which indicates the component or the "out of what" (rò ) of the thing, after the form-os, divos, etc., formed the word ixsivos (Metaph. VII 7), which cannot easily be rendered in German in an equivalently designating way (perhaps "such-ern" or "such-en," as golden, a "such-ern" or "such-en" garment, e.g., as one sometimes hears from children; the jenerlei given in the lexicon, which does not stand on the same line with wooden, stone, etc., does not express it), observes thereby that the thing is no longer what its component is (ox ixtio); rather, the latter is in the thing something other than outside it. And he distinguishes throughout in the most precise way what the thing is in its determinate actuality (èvspysią švre)syciąg) from what it is made of or can be reduced to. The latter is in contrast to the former only the being according to possibility (ovvάpet), i.e. the being-for-itself, and the This of something is as such "sublated" in the unity and actuality of the thing; the monad or one is in the dyad or two no longer a monad according to entelechy (see Remark to § 67), the property is one with the universality of the thing. It would, however, be a great misunderstanding of Aristotle if one wished to represent the matter according to his doctrine, as seems to have happened, as though the thing or being in general were mixed out of possibility and actuality, composed as it were out of a piece of possibility and another piece of actuality. This possible is for him itself something that is. What Herbart (l. c. § 117, Remark) wishes to note about this in two words: "that Aristotle, in inventing (?) the nоxtipevov, the bearer of the several marks, which as such bears the name substance (?) -- set over against this the whole constitution under the designation ados or poppǹ (so abruptly does only Herbart put it), and now thought every actual thing as consisting of both" (?! Where then is the unity, where the orix itself?): further, that the roxipevov now contained merely (?) the possibility (in the actuality of the thing it ceases for this thing), while the popph added (?) the actuality of the thing (? It brought it along from outside, presumably from somewhere?), and so (so indeed, but only not in Aristotle) a separation and composition of possibility and actuality arose, which has haunted philosophy in the most absurd way up to the time of the Wolffian philosophy and has only been driven out by Kant and Jacobi, both of whom almost simultaneously restored to the concept (?) of being, which immediately designates the absolute position of the object (consequently something not-conceptualized?), its true meaning (as everyone can grasp it with their hands)": finally, that one recognizes quite clearly that false (i.e. falsified) thought of Aristotle in his definition of the soul (De an. II 1), or rather of the vital force, with which he confuses (?) the soul" -- this thoroughgoing misunderstanding of all the main matters on which everything in Aristotle depends is too great to be corrected here in two words. But should something genuinely Aristotelian really have been driven out by Kant and Jacobi, we intend to retrieve it, especially the entelechy, against the being of mere opinion.

§ 73. To pass over at once "the thing in general," which is a mere abstract universality, and as thing-in-itself also the untrue abstraction from all properties and from all determination (cf. Remark to § 20): the determinate thing too has its determinacy only through the properties that have their subsistence in it, and their quantity, number, and constitution. A plurality and the togetherness of different properties constitute the character of thinghood in general. But with the thing there is not a being already determined in and for itself present, which would posit its properties through itself, or from which they would flow through inner particularization of the universal essence. The thing is rather, as thing, indifferent toward its properties, if only it has properties at all; what properties they are is for it something contingent. As a determinate thing, however, it is not itself the first, but rather the determinate properties that have happened to come together just so, in such a number and quantity, and thereby first make the thing into the determinate thing. The thing as such is therefore, although a concrete, yet no true concrete universality, no positive self-equality and unity; it is only a negative unity of different ones that stand in no relation, connection, or mediation to one another arising from themselves -- a mere one that gathers together its various determinacies.

§ 74. The properties, for their part, which as the peculiar determinacies belonging to this determinate one that binds and unites them are so named, are indeed taken up into and reflected in the negative unity of their thing, and therefore in unity not only with it but also among each other, each with the other in one and the same Here: but they are equally also for themselves, only contingently related to one another through the unity of the thing, hence indifferent toward each other and external to one another. Since none relates itself through itself to the other, none posits or conditions the other, each distinguishes itself from the other only as from "an" other, not as from "its" other: they also themselves stand only in an external and indifferent negative relation to one another, and therefore also pass through their own self into their own self-subsistence. From this side, it is just as essential for the character of the thing to be a mere external togetherness of different self-subsisting matters as it is essential for it to be the binding one of its properties. But if this opposite is equally essential to it, then the thing is in fact itself nothing other than a subsisting contradiction. And this it also is; but precisely for that reason the thing is also only appearance, like its knowledge that takes it for an in-itself; the contradiction therefore rather does not subsist, for the thing suffers external influence that it cannot bear; it disintegrates, dissolves, is altered, and its properties leave as self-subsisting existences this powerless connection that was unable to hold them together.

Anmerkung. Since in phenomenology it is initially a matter only of the experience and dialectic of consciousness, what is here said about the thing itself serves only for elucidation and support. The scientific treatment of it, which, however, equally presents the dialectic of the thing, belongs in the doctrine of logic or metaphysics, on essence and on appearance and existence. Cf. Hegel's Log., Pt. 2, p. 143 ff. -- Here mention may also be made of the porosity of bodies assumed by the atomists from the side of the thought underlying it and its necessary dialectic, insofar as the possibility of the interpenetration of the different properties, given the presupposed atomistic separation and impenetrability of each for the other, is thereby to be explained, and through the interstices in which each property receives the other, the unseparated unity of them is to be set aside and, instead of the actual interpenetration, rather a juxtaposition and externality of them is to be maintained. According to this representation, each property takes place only in the pores or empty interstices of the other property, so that this other is indeed penetrated by it, but not itself affected by it, but only there, where it is not itself, i.e. only in its empty spaces, is filled in by the others. But since each property stands in this relation to the others, it is not only penetrated in its pores by all the others, but it equally penetrates all the others in their pores, and not only this, but, being itself penetrated and others by it, it in turn also penetrates its own being-penetrated, and is consequently in the space empty of it, in which according to the assumption it was not supposed to be, itself again. I.e., the assumption by which the contradiction was to be avoided rather posits the contradiction -- being and non-being at once -- and contradicts itself, and what was supposed to separate and keep the properties apart rather first completely encloses them in one another, so that each is entirely there where the others also are. Or, the matter grasped in its pure thought-determinations: a being of each property is posited and a non-being, its negation -- the latter namely in its emptiness; but this its non-being, its emptiness, it itself sublates again: it is itself the non-being of its non-being, the emptiness of its emptiness, the negation of its own negation (hence the affirmation), i.e. it restores itself, and through its non-being and its emptiness is its being and its fulfillment. -- For the justification of a hypothesis, it is not enough that a phenomenon be explained by it and that it hold for all cases and conditions, or at least for most (but it cannot fail that even a well-calculated hypothesis, if it does not truly hit the matter as it is, somewhere falls short): rather, it depends above all on whether the thought underlying it also holds up and withstands scrutiny considered in and for itself. The natural sciences have been filled with a quantity of such hypotheses that in no dialectical movement of their content would be able to maintain themselves as what they were originally supposed to be and what they were invented for. Other sciences are not free of this either, notably the self-styled rationalism in theology, with its flower of all modern enlightenment, the "mediate" revelation. As one has rather to lament in this wisdom the blindness toward the divine and the loss of the rational (which is indeed a mediation, but different from what that completely externally taking enlightenment means): so the deity would also have to have had and revealed very bad thoughts in nature, if the hypotheses of the physicists were true. Had Aristotle already had the experiences of our physics and chemistry at his side: how this thinker would have dealt with such material quite differently! And even now there would be much to learn from him for a thinking observation and treatment of nature.

§ 75. The error into which the perceiving consciousness in its presupposition therefore falls -- that the thing that deceives its perception is being-in-itself essence and truth -- has its ground, firstly, in the fact that it does not suspect contradiction and considers it impossible in the thing; but secondly, in the fact that the universal that this consciousness has gained is not yet the true one but only a sensuously conditioned and at the same time abstract one, and that abstraction in general and the isolated holding fast of the individual and opposed determinacies grasped for themselves in their one-sided validity constitutes what dominates the perceiving consciousness, as the understanding in general, and prevents it from reaching truth, instead of being mastered by it and recognized for what it is -- namely, for a completely untrue mode. As little as the thing yet expresses at itself the character of that higher unity and universality, its negative unity yet already has this power in it, that its properties as such are sublated in this unity, and therein themselves in unseparated unity -- each where the other also is -- and thereby as particulars at the same time share the whole universality of the thing. The ordinary perceiving consciousness, on the other hand, which, as little as it penetrates into the contradiction and rather flees than thinkingly grasps it, just as little arrives at the true cognition of this unity, because it can only ever represent diverse sensuous things alongside and outside one another, gets hold now only of the one, now only of the other of the opposed moments for itself alone, has to do now only with the abstract universality, now only with the equally abstract and isolated particularity, and is thus thrown back and forth by and between its own fixated extremes, instead of recognizing that it has itself first called forth through this fixation that which sets it in contradiction and sends it around from one error to the next. The above dialectic, which with the help of precisely this abstraction and fixation points out the contradiction everywhere

the pure concept or the concept as such (not of something else or particular, but the concept of the concept, or the simple absolute form of all conceiving) already is in itself, the inner has also developed itself objectively for consciousness so that it now also presents the form of pure thought, and is one and the same with it. Indeed, it was already this simple infinite, or the necessity of the concept, that guided all the dialectic of consciousness thus far as the soul of its movement, and that already sought to break forth in particular in the final result of perception, the unity of the moments of being-for-itself and being-for-another passing over into one another and sublating themselves, then in the play of forces and beyond; but it is only in the movement of the non-explaining explaining that this simple infinite, undifferentiated in differentiating, has stepped forth in free form as inner necessity and the understanding's own principle, and has then also become, for consciousness, in its object, that which it in truth is — namely, an other which is no longer an other, and as which consciousness knows itself (self-consciousness). —

[p. 182]

§ 113. Since the concept of this infinity has emerged for us out of the whole movement of consciousness thus far, and has presented itself as its truth, it is also only this that is, for us, the true and essential in the whole of objectivity — that which alone, wherever something true is to be cognized, lets us see this only in its pure shape. If it withdraws itself from and conceals itself from ordinary consciousness — which pursues quite different things in the opinion of their truth — beneath the sensuous husk and beneath the manifold determinatenesses, become fixed and hardened for it, of its own comportment toward the world, and is therefore misjudged or not recognized by it: then the other views which this ordinary consciousness may happen to have fall within the path that we have traversed; taking them up at the determinate standpoint to which they belong, their [p. 183] own inner dialectic would necessarily lead them, first, beyond their fixed determinateness, and then, along the same path and to the same goal, further onward. But there is, in general, for consciousness neither rest nor repose nor any earlier dwelling-place until it has gone back into this simple, but in itself absolutely universal, concept, and hereby also into its own absolute form, in which it has the immediate self-certainty of the truth (cf. § 30). For infinity, insofar as it was still grasped and held by us in a merely objective shape, becomes concept as such more closely through this: that consciousness reflects upon the content of its knowing, which it has in and on it, or knows itself in what it knows, in that it thus knows its object. But the content of this knowing is already nothing other than the consciousness of the difference as such, which is just as much no longer a difference, or is sublated. In this, however, the consciousness of the in-itself-being truth of the object is also perfectly for itself, or it is itself that which it knows, and the content of its knowing is for it no longer anything foreign or other; that is, content and form of knowing have now become perfectly one and the same. For I is in itself the differentiating of the undifferentiated; I differentiates itself from itself, but in that it differentiates itself, it has therein the immediate self-certainty that this differentiated is not differentiated. I is itself something homonymous [selfsame], which repels itself from itself, and for which that which is repelled, differentiated, and become unequal is immediately no unequal and differentiated thing. Only when brought back to this pure, absolute form of I is the object completely penetrated by its knower, and only thus is I a conceiving consciousness.

Remark. Here the concept is spoken of at first only from its subjective side, as the form of actual cognition in thinking, or insofar as consciousness becomes conceiving consciousness. But as concerns its completed objective shape, or the concept that is in-and-for-itself in the totality of its moments, the further scientific exposition of this belongs to pure science itself, namely to logic, where its moments, as those of the true and rational that is [p. 184] in-and-for-itself, determine themselves more closely as universality, particularity, and individuality — whose living mediation the concept is itself in its totality and infinity, and each of which, in its mediation, is not something abstract and dead, but has the other moments in it, or has the concept itself in it: so that this mediated being-concluded-together of the moments is inwardly already the rational syllogism, just as this is only the concept that has stepped apart into its moments. Its natural and immediate presentation, however, is life and the living, which is treated in §§ 119 ff. Of the poverty of what is ordinarily called a concept — where thinking, applied to a manifold matter snatched up and left in its whole empirical crudity, in the face of which consciousness has not yet gotten beyond the comportment of sensuous perception, yields only the activity of abstracting and deadening and a merely formal unity, and penetrates nothing and conceives nothing — mention has already been made earlier (cf. Remark 1 to § 100).

§ 114. Just as, in the concept of infinity, consciousness, according to content and form — which are no longer separated from one another therein — is only the pure knowing of itself, so conversely it itself, as pure knowing and thinking I, has now become, according to both — according to its content just as much as according to its form, and in the unity of both — what it earlier was merely according to its form (cf. § 28): infinite self-equality, infinite relation of itself to itself in its difference or otherness, in itself infinite knowing and thinking; or the pure self-movement of infinity is the pure movement of thinking consciousness itself. If we consider once more, in this connection, the movement of the concept that has come to be for us, which is just as much pure I as infinity, then, whether one passes from the pure or inner difference, which as such sublates itself, to the self-equal unity, or from this to that, the movement will always present itself just as much as the self-equal as the self-dividing-in-itself or differentiating, or as the self-division of the self-equal and the becoming-self-equal of the divided or unequal. The movement is itself nothing other than this, and the passing-over is not an external one, effected or borne by a thinking that is somehow foreign to the matter, but the inner movement of the matter itself, which is in itself nothing else, and the pure thinking [p. 185] I in the movement of its own thinking.

§ 115. Already in uttering "the self-equal," and grasping what is thereby said and contained in it inwardly, that is, from itself, or from its own reflection-into-itself, the inner difference or division, and just as much its own sublation, is contained and expressed therein. For what is equal to itself can be this only in relation to its difference, or through reflection into its inequality with itself; that is, as self-equal it is already in itself also an unequal and divided thing, and the movement from the one to the other is the movement from itself to itself — and indeed such that the self-equal is already far more the self-unequal and divided, and the divided is already far more the self-equal, and both are one and the same. The self-equal infinite essence is only pure relation of itself to itself; if, then, it relates itself to something, the relating and that-which-is-related-to are hereby already differentiated as an other, and the division has occurred; but if it relates itself to itself, then it returns into itself and sublates the division. The related, which is an other than the relating and only became this other through self-relation, is for it, the self-relating, far rather no other, for it is itself; and therefore it returns out of its otherness into itself as the self-equal. Or, if one expresses it thus: infinite relation of itself, in its otherness, to itself — then another being, but as the being of the very same, is already differentiated or presupposed; but this other is, precisely because it is the other of the very same, or its other, recognized just as much as no other, and the equal coincides in it with itself and is in it, as its otherness, only pure relation to itself. Or if we grasp the infinite in its inequality with itself, it is thereby already expressed that it is its inequality, and indeed with itself; the unequal is consequently just as much itself, and therefore, preserving itself therein, it is, in its inequality, [p. 186] equal to itself. But if we wish to hold fast, for itself, the inequality that has come to be, or the division already occurred: then, first, thinking has passed over from the unity of the unequal with them to their division, and the divided are, precisely because they are divided, in themselves still related to their unity, out of which they come; but second, each of them is so much, in and for itself, a divided thing and the opposite of the other, that each, related absolutely to its other, is neither for itself nor at all without it, and, being only as the other of an other, cannot be uttered at all without at the same time uttering the other — that is, it already has the other itself in its concept, on it, and is itself the other.

§ 116. In that we have, in and with the concept of infinity, returned from all our otherwise knowing, which was not able to maintain itself as truth and as in-itself, to the absolute form of the pure I, there is present, then, for consciousness as content simply nothing other than the unity or identity and the inner difference, together with their movement, passing over into one another, in themselves; and it is in this All and One that we here move with our I alone. Since, however, a merely external thinking overlooks, in the concept of the infinite or the absolute, the inner self-movement of the self-equal and of its differentiating within itself — even though this is, as the pure activity of the I in consciousness itself, likewise and indeed immediately present for the I — and, after it has, through its own abstraction, first made the self-equal simple into a rigid, unmoved unity, and likewise the difference into a division falling apart, and has set the two beside one another, is accustomed then to raise the question how the difference, then, is to come out of the unity, or how this could divide itself: so the knowing of this abstraction, too, must still be briefly considered in itself, whereby it will emerge that it already has and has already accomplished, [p. 187] without becoming conscious of it, that which it finds incomprehensible and asks about. For since, for the consciousness of the infinite and absolute object — in relation to which that question is supposed to have its irrefutable force and meaning — there is nothing else present at all than unity and difference, and all other knowing has gone back to this pure opposition (just as in logic, ordinarily, to identity and contradiction, as the supreme principles of thinking that comprehend and govern all other forms of thought under themselves): the consciousness which sets the unity beside the difference, or opposite it, already has, in this abstracted unity itself, something divided and opposed, and need not first ask whence the division of the unity comes and how it is possible. For the unity, which is supposed to be an irrevocably other than the difference and is merely opposed to the division, is one of two, and in this division into which it has fallen with the difference, is in itself something opposed. This thinking has thus far rather lost its unity, and it would now, conversely, have to be asked how it means to set about (itself) in order to attain it again.

§ 117. This abstraction, which excludes the difference from the self-equal and sets it beside it, and yet wishes to hold fast both, for itself, in their significance, is thus in itself a perversion of what it means, and its dialectic, which it is unable to withstand, is the movement toward the opposite of its assumption. In that it asserts, between the self-equal and the difference or otherness itself, an insurmountable difference, the former, instead of really being the self-equal absolute essence, is rather itself already, as has been shown, one of the divided, or this abstraction of the simple is only, as yet, the one moment of the opposition and division that is in general present here — hence no longer the unity, but an unequal thing, which has in itself its opposition to an other. The consciousness which resists the inner passing-over, proper to the self-equal [p. 188] itself, into division, has thereby already itself made this passing-over — but externally, and without knowing it. If, now, this — which is supposed to be the self-equal, but is already a divided thing — is first to pass over into division, it could only divide itself with itself, that is, become the opposite of what it is, and would consequently have to sublate again its being-divided or its otherness, which it already is, as something opposed, in itself. But if the abstract unity itself is already far more the division or the difference, then its other or opposite is no longer, say, the difference that previously stood opposite it and that may perhaps still have been retained in representation — for, with its being one of the divided, or in general something opposed, the determination of division and difference in general is already posited along with it; rather its true opposite can only be the self-equal unity, that is, that which it was supposed to be, but, in the abstraction, was not. But what, conversely, is the sublation of the difference, or the coming-to-be of self-equality, becomes, for this same abstraction, likewise rather a dividing; for what becomes equal to itself thereby steps opposite the difference or the division precisely by this; in that it thereby steps to the one side, it itself becomes, rather, a divided thing. We thus see, in this abstraction itself, the pure alternation come about which, for us, resulted in the inner self-movement of the infinite as the pure passing-over of the only moments still present, and which is the pure movement of consciousness itself.

Remark. The matter here at issue — the transition from an undifferentiated, and therefore one and self-equal, to a differentiated thing, thereby broken within itself and unequal, and conversely — is something so everyday, and occurring in all experience, in ordinary life, in nature, in all the sciences, as in common consciousness itself, that one must be astonished at a philosophy which refuses to recognize this transition where it is to be grasped in its pure thought and in its inwardness, and sets itself against it intractably. If something is first differentiated, or differentiates itself, then before its differentiation it is still an undifferentiated thing, or a one; but even differentiated, it does not cease, as the same matter, to be, even in its difference, still equal to itself [p. 189] — and indeed the very same relation in which it is differentiated also expresses its unity, because otherwise, too, no differentiation would be possible. And just as one passes over from the unity to the difference, so, conversely, the differentiated must also be able to arrive again at its unity.
