Abstraction does accomplish this too; but it fails in that it leaves aside and sets to the side the differentiated thing from which it abstracts, as though — just as consciousness lets it fall or sets it aside — it had thereby simply vanished and were no longer present at all; and therefore, since it thus only ever has the one beside the other, now the unity, now the difference, for itself, and passes from the one to the other only externally, it never brings the whole into the presence of thinking and consciousness, nor penetrates the inner and living [aspect] of the matter. Its unity therefore remains only an abstract and formal one, which precisely for that reason is no true unity but a division, and its difference likewise only an abstract one, fixed for itself. If, to cite a phenomenon occurring in everyday consciousness, someone says he is at odds with himself, or divided, he is thereby expressing not merely a difference but indeed an opposition or a contradiction as something present in his one and selfsame I; for he will surely not want to say that he has, in this being-at-odds, in this division of the one I, two I's — and if he took it so, he would then still have a third, which, in the two others and divided, would be conscious of the equal and the same, and, in both, also of its self-equality. This one lets pass for the subjective I; but when, in absolute thinking and in the thinking of the Absolute, the very same thing is to be cognized in its pure essential shape and inwardness, then external reflection steps back and asks how such a thing is possible. If this reflection, in its question and demand — merely to be shown how the duality comes out of the unity — did not perhaps expect that a visible and palpable trick should be performed before it, in which it first has a unity before its eyes, but then just as evidently perceives how, out of this unity, a duality either gradually moves forth or springs out all at once, fully formed: then it is hard to form any conception of how, where the purest and innermost cognition is at issue, all thinking could have so lost itself in externality as no longer to have even an intimation of the matter. For that the matter was actually present in the thinking and consciousness of all true and actual philosophy, and indeed already of the oldest, can be demonstrated throughout — although modern subjective and reflective thinking, which has made itself incapable of understanding a Plato or an Aristotle any longer, say, because it is unable to follow the thinking of these philosophers, and therefore grasps their so-called philosophemes only in an external way, and brings to their assessment a wretched Kantian-Tennemannian or similar yardstick with which such giants of thought cannot be measured, would nevertheless sometimes like to give itself the appearance that the correct understanding of that ancient philosophy is on its side, and that others, who really understand it, are only reading their own [notions] into it. The subjective philosophy and all its kin have otherwise always been very much concerned with proofs for the so-called objective existence of God — which was, to be sure, very natural once one had lost, even in faith, the concept of God and the substantial knowledge of him from consciousness, and yet wished still to connect a philosophically colored representation with the name that remained, and to fortify this by the further, externally added representation that God, too, has an external existence, like some thing whose external presence or non-presence can still be disputed. Since one became well aware that, with this, God first properly became something completely subjective, indeed a representation, one has not concealed the subjectivity, and has thereby acknowledged one's incapacity for the cognition of truth and of the divine — but, to make up for the deficiency, has let a new philosophical or aesthetic faith arise, brought forth through the mediation of reflection, to which, however, on its subjective standpoint of mere feeling, there is lacking, besides the immediacy of the old faith, which had God immediately present in consciousness, also the really objective and substantial content. This new faith, therefore, can only be called a faith of subjective opinion and individual feeling. That this element, in which only vague feeling still makes something of itself to do with God without objective content, is a much worse element than that of the old faith, which still hung together with the cognition of God, is, to be sure, not recognized by modern worldly wisdom; still less can one expect of this subjectivity the cognition that what is cognized through absolute cognition is the in-and-for-itself-being and alone truly actual, and that the concept of God, too, includes its own proof, just as the absolute content includes its form in itself, and that the philosopher, therefore, for his objective certainty of God, does not need some worse element in which God, without his absolute concept, and hence only in opinion and representation, is to come into external contact with consciousness; for through the elevation to this cognition, that supposed philosophy would cease to be a subjective one. But alongside those earlier efforts concerning the objective existence of God — in which, instead of proving straightaway into the blue, or rather the murk, of representation, and, in order to bring the matter out of this murk of opinion onto the path of thought and concept, one ought first to have made clear to oneself the sense of the whole undertaking, and asked what each word in it — namely, "prove," "objective," "existence" — actually means, indeed what God himself, even apart from his existence, must, according to his concept, necessarily be — one was, at the same time, also very much concerned with asserting the personality of God, which one believed, wrongly, to be endangered by the so-called philosophy of identity; but it was strange that precisely that on which personality alone rests was thereby overlooked, or at least treated only superficially and in the manner of representation, but by no means grasped in its absolute depth. For one did indeed represent God, in human fashion, as a subject active and acting in various directions and relations; but that one, entering into the essence and concept of a subject or of personality, that is, into the concept of I-hood, should have grasped God as the I of absolute reason and absolute spirit — or, since the knowing had proceeded from spirit, at least as the I of infinite and eternal thinking and acting, and as self-consciousness — and, from this point, the source of absolute liveliness and activity, should have sought further to comprehend the divine essence in its inner necessity already following from this, and its possibility of being all in all, in its I-hood and the self-conscious self-equality thereof — whereby something rational might easily also have been found for the two other [persons] as well — of this, to my knowledge, no one from that side has thought. Thus the representation of God's activities, too, had to remain a mere representation, that is, the mere meaning of a living thing, something faint and shadow-like, not much better or more essential for consciousness than the perceived movement and activity of a figure in a Chinese shadow-play. If, moreover, as was remarked at the opening of this Remark and earlier, the matter here at issue, or the infinity of form, can — just as it is itself the absolute form of I and the principle of its pure activity — likewise be demonstrated everywhere: then one must, on the other hand, also admit that a thinking still only struggling toward the absolute concept and the cognition of the infinite, straining for its own breakthrough — especially where it is a matter of grasping this concept, in its deepest meaning, in a concrete content and in a manifold but scattered wealth of its rational and spiritual substance, and of bringing the opposed together in thought, or where consciousness still has masses of representation to overcome — has often expressed itself thereon in difficult and dark words, and has driven forth those phenomena which one is accustomed to designate by the name of mysticism. The shallowness which believes itself already done with something once it has branded it with the said name here throws everything away, form and content alike, and, in the conceit of its worldly wisdom, even makes merry over it — because it in fact understands nothing of it either, and for it everything deep, even in clear words, is mysticism; the philosophy which does indeed also reject the mystical, because it lacks the essential form, the method of science, nevertheless knows how to find the good kernel beneath the coarse and rough shell, and takes an interest in such phenomena insofar as it has significance for it to watch and follow a deeper consciousness of spirit even under its veiling and in its wrestling with the mighty object — whereby one is often surprised by deep glimpses and great connections. But that much of what gives itself a mystical and at the same time pietistic tinge can be an empty puffing-up, a hollow, marrowless pious affectation, a solemnly acting semblance and zeal about nothing, or even hypocrisy, one will not deny; for what, in the realm of the contingent, is not possible? Such things belong to the outer appearance of life and of consciousness, as others also do, but hold no further interest for philosophy; still less may one confuse or conflate them with philosophy and self-conscious science.

[p. 191]

§ 118. If we now look back at the inner world into which the understanding, having risen above perception, gazed as into a world that had become for it, through this elevation, itself suprasensible and hovering above or beyond appearance: it has [p. 192] now become clear what this inner world in truth is. It was already the understanding's own inner self, or pure thought and the concept itself, that it still beheld and contemplated before itself in objective shape; it was itself what it had experienced in the development of its object into a self-equal infinity. After the two extremes of the inner and of consciousness, which were separated by appearance as their middle term (§ 96), had first — the inner beyond, for us — mediated itself again with appearance (§§ 98 and 109), so now the other, this-sided extreme as well, which was the understanding's own consciousness, has come, together with its objective extreme, and this latter with it, to absolute mediation and reconciliation — or rather, both have collapsed into one, into a middle term which is no longer appearance, for this has already been fully taken up (§§ 109 and 111) into the suprasensible inner, but is the pure concept of infinity itself. In that, within this pure and absolute concept, the objective inner has moved toward the inner of consciousness and unveiled its truth, otherness has vanished, and there is present only the pure gazing of the inner into the inner, for which and in which, in the undifferentiatedness of both, only the self-equal or the undifferentiated selfsame is to be beheld — which repels itself from itself and thereby divides itself into an inner difference, but just as much restores again the undifferentiatedness of what is differentiated, or its equality with itself. As the knowing of this undifferentiated inner, from whose pure essence, in form and content, consciousness itself is no longer distinguished, having therein only its own essence and knowing as object, it is infinite knowing, coinciding with itself alone even in its other — the concept as such, comprehending consciousness or knowledge in truth; and likewise, as I, the certainty of itself that is self-equal in its inner distinguishing, self-consciousness. What was, as object, an other for the I, is in the concept so completely permeated by it that it is no longer an other and something different, in general nothing merely objective, alien, and unknown (§ 26) for and against the I, but rather the I knows itself in it, and the determinations of the other as its own. Or the difference that formerly obtained between an in-itself — which then degraded itself to a being of that in-itself merely for consciousness, or to appearance — and this latter itself, has now likewise collapsed; for what is now the in-itself, consciousness knows as itself, and it is for it nothing other than precisely this; and as it knows its object, so too is that object in itself and for itself. Through this certainty, no longer distinct from its truth (§ 30), the goal of the movement and development of consciousness thus far is hereby also attained, insofar as the element in which pure science, immediately certain of itself, moves is only this pure consciousness and self-consciousness, which is herein already in itself reason, and rational knowing and thinking.

[p. 193]

Remark. What consciousness, at its earlier stages, beheld in and on objects from its very first going-out beyond immediate sensuous certainty (which is not yet any knowing at all), and how it beheld them — all the views it had of things, the abstraction of the thing itself and all other abstractions, reflections, and determinations — was indeed likewise only itself; consciousness has, in each of its determinatenesses and modes of comportment, only beheld itself out of the things (cf. 68 ff.): but these determinate modes of consciousness could, no more than the objectivities so conceived and known, still maintain and assert themselves before consciousness, owing to their inner deficiency, and have all irresistibly receded back into the concept of infinity, in which alone consciousness, as pure self-conscious I, has both its absolute movement and its absolute rest and satisfaction. The old saying of Egyptian wisdom on the statue of Isis in the temple of Minerva at Sais (after Plutarch, De Iside et Osiride, ch. 10), which has become well enough known through Schiller's reworking and is very congenial to our modern culture, is to this extent entirely correct: that no mortal, i.e. finite, thinking suffices to lift the veil that conceals the truth; an immortal, i.e. infinite, thinking is required to step behind the curtain and see that there is nothing behind it but what is in front of it — for therein lies the secret, that it cannot become manifest even to consciousness right from the start, but only after a series of other truths and supposed certainties have gone under. Schiller could therefore also indicate more precisely, as the [p. 13] guilt by which the beholding of truth is barred, the opinion that places an unknown behind the curtain, so that there be an unknown behind it. After all that has so far been said about infinity and its concept, it is moreover self-evident that there was no question here of any quantitative infinity, nor of an infinity of mere representation.

[p. 194]

§ 119. But just as consciousness, for its part, reflects itself into itself in its object, whereby it is, in its other, the self-equal certainty of itself, and, undistinguished from that, the truth: so likewise the object, for its part, becomes out of what it has become this same reflection-into-self, and thus emerges as a new object for consciousness — which, however, if it has not arrived at itself or at the concept in its previous object, recognizes itself in this new object, the result of the previous one, which presents the infinite concept itself in its totality and total particularization in an immediate and natural manner, still far less, or finds nothing but incomprehensibilities and unresolved contradictions. The selfsame out of which the concept of infinity was developed contained, as it were, only the first appearance or intimation of the concept as such — even though it stepped forth in it, in the undifferentiatedness of the opposed determinations of the understanding, the positive and the negative, at once in a very decisive but for that very reason also abrupt manner — but it did not yet contain its complete presentation and unfolding, nor likewise the mentioned reflection of the infinite on and out of itself into itself, whereby, in the self-relation of its unfolded and separated-out differences to itself as unity, it becomes a self or subject, and thereby living actuality. But this is life in general, and, in its determinateness, the living thing, of which the presentation, according to its principal moments, is still to be given.

§ 120. Universal life, which develops out of the dark and hidden life of the earth (the geological) more particularly toward vegetable life, and, in the completed presentation of its concept and of the concept as such, toward and within [p. 195] animal life — wherein nature, at the same time in its own external element, attains the highest stage of its completion and its return to spirit — makes itself known as the infinite universality whose essence has been recognized by us as the self-equality that differentiates and divides itself within itself, but that preserves itself in its otherness and, out of that otherness, relates itself to itself and mediates itself with itself — or as an infinitely universal fluidity, self-equal throughout all the differences of external, self-subsistent existence, in which, and undivided from which, life itself is the soul, or the omnipresent simple of a manifold externality. Living and developing within itself, it is only its own end; in its productions it attains only itself again, and has itself as its own presupposition. Thus, as the universal essence or the self-equal substance of life — which, being in itself the pure movement of infinite differentiating and mediating, as also of the dissolving and leading-back of differences into their unity, unfolds and particularizes itself into an endless manifoldness and externality of self-subsistent shapes, formations, and members — it goes on and forms itself within itself even to the point of opposition and to its own contradiction; but, in all its productions and all the moments thereof, never abandoning itself, and permeated in every particularization by the totality of its concept, it is everywhere, again, itself the fluid omnipresence and simple soul which, still holding sway and reaching over even in what is opposed and self-opposing, overcomes and sublates the proper and selfish drive of self-subsistent particularization, produces itself in the production of that drive, and, positing it only as means and moment, determines its leading-back into unity through the drive itself, and restores and brings forth itself as the universal. But this universal life, which is simply the subsistence of what subsists and its immanent substance, has its actualization in the living individual, which is the universal as a singular and as this negative unity, relating itself to itself in its universality — being-for-itself life, actual livingness, or subject and self.

[p. 196]

Remark. "In life," says Hegel, Subjective Logic, p. 280, "in this unity of its concept in the externality of objectivity, in the absolute multiplicity of atomistic matter, all thoughts simply desert the thinking that holds fast to the determinations of the relations of reflection and of the formal concept; the omnipresence of the simple in manifold externality is, for reflection, an absolute contradiction, and insofar as it must at the same time apprehend that omnipresence out of the perception of life, and thereby concede the actuality of this idea, an incomprehensible mystery — because it does not grasp the concept, and does not grasp the concept as the substance of life." Such a book with seven seals as life is, then, of course, also the whole of Hegel's Logic itself, to this kind of thinking, as is all actual science of philosophy; it looks into the one as externally as into the other, and sees, out of both, nothing but its own emptiness and conceptlessness — without acknowledging and accusing itself, for what strikes it as strange in the matter, as the guilty party. But in life, in fact, all the ordinary determinations of reflection with which this thinking has made itself familiar as its absolute truths are put to such shame that, under whichever of them it wishes to grasp life, the living thing slips away under the very grasping, or only something unliving remains behind. How, indeed, should any abstract form of reflection still be able to hold fast, where already with the favorite category of causality — which immediately goes under entirely in the concept of the inner purposiveness of life — absolutely nothing more can be done?

§ 121. The living individual, which, as determinateness (negation) of universal life, negates this life in its being-for-itself, or has and is the universal simply in the negative, self-determining relation of that life to itself within it, presents itself in this relation — just as universal life as such, in accordance with the concept (cf. Remark to § 113), itself divides itself within itself into particularity and opposition, and determines itself to singularity, or actualizes itself as this singularity — on the one hand as a singular thing so mediated, united, and closed together into a unity with universal life or its substance (a unity which is singularity itself, and, as being-for-itself, self and soul), that it has the universal and the particular of its substance partly inhering in it, or as predicate, in it, while, as subject of that substance, it carries out and presents its universal concept in itself through particularization and singularization; but partly, too, it relates itself to universal life — [p. 197] or now, rather, to life as divided into particularity — as to an objectivity opposed to it, existing outside it and alien to it, which it has to overcome in itself or to posit as its own, and comports itself negatively against it. On the one side, the living thing forms itself into a purposive externality and objectivity in itself, and into an organic corporeality that, in accordance with the inner difference of the concept, articulates and shapes itself both outwardly and inwardly, and moves and preserves itself within itself; on the other side, directed and strained against its presupposed outer world and objectivity, it enters into conflict with it, and is the drive to sublate its separation from it, or its otherness, and, through overcoming, appropriating, and transforming that otherness into its individuality, to restore again its in-itself identity with it and with its universal substance. The whole of individual life accordingly runs its course — to which is further added the re-sublation of its singularization, or the re-production of itself as a universal — under the principle of its subjectivity or negative unity, according to which it is itself the determining power and all-permeating soul, through the following three processes: a) the process of individual formation and inner organic activity (animal organism); b) the particular or properly so-called life-process; c) the genus-process.

Remark. What universal life, or the substance of life, is, may here too, provisionally, already be called the Idea of life — only, by Idea, must be understood neither the merely subjective concept of rational thinking, still less a merely subjective representation, into which the French language and, among us, ordinary representational and manner of speech has made it. The Idea, in its true meaning, is rather, roughly, the universal — held only somewhat more ideally by Plato, who called it Idea — united in one unity with what Aristotle calls entelechy: the absolute unity of the ideal and the real, of the subjectivity and the objectivity of the concept; hence itself absolute actuality, which is permeated by the concept and is its reality, equal to it, carrying it out and presenting it. Thus the Idea of life, the Idea of God, of nature, of spirit, of the state, and so on, is life, God, nature, spirit, the state itself — but as Idea; and precisely because it has the absolute concept in it, and is itself that concept, [p. 198] it too is present only in absolute knowing and cognizing, or, what amounts to the same, the Idea can, in its truth, only be conceived, not intuited, represented, and the like. But in the organism of life and of the living thing, the absolute concept is brought, by nature itself, in a natural and immediate manner, to intuition as well, for the one who conceives it. The same has happened with spirit in the organization of ethical spirit, which is the state; but as a rule, nothing is as yet conceived less than what the state — this spiritual actuality common to all, as is physical life — in truth is. But what distinguishes mere substance, in Spinoza's sense, from the Idea — indeed constitutes the most important and profound difference, like that between necessity and freedom — is precisely the absolute concept itself, whose universal ground, in objective in-and-for-itself necessity, is indeed substance, but not yet itself, or that necessity is not yet, for itself, also as subject, as a free, self-positing, self-grounding, and self-determining self. Because Spinoza's substance, however much it is one indivisible totality, lacks this absolute form, not only does the individual self-consciousness dissolved in it have no true self, no freedom, but substance itself has this deficiency within it, in that personality and the principle of freedom, together with subjectivity, are lacking to it; and since, therefore, thinking in it is lost in extension (being), and, in the determinateness which is indeed negation but not self-negating negation, not "negativity," has no return into itself, it stands, in relation to a reflecting thinking outside it, in an external relation — no better than what, in other metaphysics and ordinary philosophy, stands in such a relation as a particular object, be it God or something else. Universal life determines itself, through itself, through the relation of what it is to itself, through its becoming-for-itself, to subjectivity, and becomes actual only as individuality, as a single living thing. However much the necessary progression of the concept's inner self-activity and negativity is to be recognized in this, and however much the living subject, itself again as whole concept according to all its moments, has and develops the totality of life in and on itself, it is nevertheless, as a separated and detached individuality, and as negative unity over against its universal substance, still not adequate to its own absolute concept — and is, both according to its finitized determinateness in which it is concept for itself, and in its separation and particularization over against other individuals, a negative within itself which, however much it is not merely self-subsistent but itself posits itself as self-subsistent, nevertheless sublates itself again through itself. It is not itself the whole. In this may, for the time being, be grasped the difference between finite and mortal individuality, and that subjectivity which, infinite and immortal in itself, is simply equal to its absolute essence or its objectivity, and therein perfectly attains itself. (Cf. §§ 12–129.)

§ 122. Of this, the most important points are, more precisely, the following. a) Formation and organism. The living subject has an external and objective being as presupposition, immediately from nature or from the universal substance of life; but it is, as negative unity, itself therein the self-relating, and the [p. 199] positing and determining, of its own externality — or it is, as subject, the principle beginning from itself, self-moving, the soul that subjugates and governs its objectivity, so that this objectivity be its — that is, the corporeality alone suited to it, realizing it itself and its end, inwardly and outwardly. The purposiveness in which it carries out its reality is therefore no external, but inner purposiveness — that is, one in which the living thing closes itself together with itself through its externality (the shaping, formation, and articulation of which is its means and instrument), but through this same middle term likewise at the same time mediates and unites itself with its external objectivity. It is the selfsame in a higher and more fully carried-out reality, which repels itself from itself and posits differences, but holds these differences, in their external subsistence and selfish drive, simply within unity, and leads them back to it. The means, further, is indeed means for the subject, but since this subject has its own reality therein, it is also already the carried-out end itself, or a means through which it again only continues and produces itself within itself — so that in this process of the living thing with itself, nothing other than just this its own reality, and the unity of its concept with that reality, is to be attained, and only this circuit is present, in which, beginning and setting out from itself, it returns to itself, and the effect is also its cause.

Remark to § 122. Although Kant, in the Critique of Teleological Judgment, reawakened the concept of inner purposiveness (for it was already present, in the most definite form, in Aristotle), and thereby opened the way to the Idea of life and to a rational — that is, one finding and recognizing reason in nature — contemplation of nature: yet the merely subjective bearing again given to the matter — as though the inner rational accordance in nature were present only for an externally reflecting judging, which, in its considerations, in opposition to a mechanical causality, could also, for variety's sake, make use of the principle of final causes as an optional maxim — prevented the matter, from this side, from actually attaining to its objective rationality as well. In the relation of purpose in general, the determinations of an external existence, of objectivity, are [p. 200] considered not merely in their formal relation, as e.g. in mere mechanism, where the otherwise content of what is determined is something indifferent, and its particular determinateness something external to it, but also, according to their content, as essential determinations, as externalities determined by the concept of purpose itself. They are accordingly means for the purpose, for the attainment of something which is a third term in the circular movement of purposive activity, and, to begin with, also something other than the means taken for itself. But this third, the external extreme as against the inner, the objective goal (τὸ τέλος) of the subjective purpose, is again also the first, the purpose itself — that which sets it in movement and activity, or that in the attainment of which it closes together with itself (for here the concept becomes, in objectivity itself, a syllogism, in which two extremes relate themselves to one another and close together through a middle term), or that wherein its subjectivity coincides with its objectivity. If one wishes to draw in here determinations of reflection — which, however, at once betray their inadequacy to the matter — it would be a force that solicits itself to its own utterance, an effect that is its own cause. The purpose therefore already contains in itself, as subjective concept, the whole enclosed within it, and is, through itself, through its own negativity, in which it negates itself as merely subjective, related to objectivity and to its own realization — which, as its carrying-out, is the external self-positing of itself, or, through its own self-activity, the objective positing of that which it already is in itself or inwardly, in order to become this also for itself in externality. Before this its realization, the subjective purpose, as mere concept, which as yet holds its externality and objective truth enclosed only inwardly within itself, and, as this, negates itself, has this its negation on it as a deficiency and contradiction — hence as a drive toward its objectification, or as an ought and a striving toward its objectivity, whereby it, as the self-moving and self-determining, repels itself from itself, or divides and separates itself, in order first truly to posit and establish itself, in actuality, as the true self-equal. This positing determination now posits itself, to begin with, as means — which, itself already object, and indeed an object determined through the concept, is at the same time the first objective negation of the subjective purpose, or, for it, its becoming-other. But since the means, as such, is not yet the attained or realized whole purpose, and therefore itself again negates itself, insofar as it is only means, and thus has this negativity of its own sublation on itself — whereby it, as first negation in the whole, passes over also into a second, or becomes negation of the negation and thereby return — the means is, for the subject of the purpose, also already the beginning of its return into itself, as also of the objective attainment of itself, in that in the third term the purpose has rather already actually attained itself. Once the means to something are actually present, one is also accustomed to take the attainment of the purpose as already assured. But in relation to this third, the objective extreme, to whose attainment or realization the means makes the transition, it is in general only the middle term which, in external existence, can also subsist for itself as a persisting universal possibility (as e.g. in every tool) for the realization of the purpose, and can be esteemed higher than this, even in its singularity. Conversely, however, the means, according to its external existence, can also (just as the middle, the medius terminus, in the formal syllogism) be something indifferent; several means can lead to the same purpose, and one can therefore be put in place of another. But in its reflection according to the inner side of the purposive relation, the means is necessarily determined through the concept of purpose itself, and is essentially what it is; and hence too, as means, according to the concept, is, with all its external subsistence, a moment that sublates itself and passes back into the totality of the purposive relation. The realized purpose, however, which in external existence likewise can appear only as a singly determined object, or as a singularity in general, is by no means only this, but is, in general, the first term, now in truth attaining and fulfilling itself as third, wherein the whole movement has gone back into itself, and the subject of the purpose has positively closed together with its object. From this side it is the objectivity, wholly determined in accordance with the subjective concept, posited and permeated by it itself, wherein at the same time the self-negating subjectivity of the purpose — insofar as it were only this — has just as much sublated and vanished, as, on the other hand, objectivity too, insofar as it lets itself be regarded, prior to its determination by purpose, as an already presupposed, subsisting and particularly self-subsistent thing — i.e. still alien to the purpose — has now likewise sublated this its separated being-for-itself, and gone back into unity with the subjective concept of purpose, self-equal within it: a negation and transformation of the objectivity that was previously for itself, through which it itself, in every higher and true purposiveness, attains, over against what it previously was only in itself, or to which it was determined, now its true being-in-and-for-itself. Now insofar as the ordering and execution of a purposiveness perceived somewhere in external existence is ascribed to an understanding situated outside it, as the subject of the concept of purpose — as, for example, the purposive accordance in the world and in nature is ascribed to an extramundane understanding (νοῦς) in the well-known teleological syllogism — this is external purposiveness, in which what chiefly matters is the content of what one takes to be the intended purpose: whether this content, too, is for itself something fully valid and worthy, corresponding to the expenditure of means and adequate to the understanding presupposed therein; whether the alleged purpose, if it was indeed a purpose, could not also have been attained in a more direct, less roundabout or more immediate way, and so on. When, especially, there is talk of an absolute understanding of the world, ordinary understanding's consciousness, too, demands its satisfaction just where something determinate and singular in external existence is held up to it as the purpose to be attained — since it knows the judging of such purposivenesses of finite things, and of their adequate or inadequate execution, to belong to its own sphere as well, and credits itself with being competent to judge in the matter. But it is precisely from this side that the perceived purposiveness, and this whole external teleological mode of consideration, shows its nakedness, everywhere exposed to dialectic, its narrowness and poverty, on whose account it has become something stale and fallen into disrepute. If, for example, the wisdom of the creator was praised because he did not create so large an animal, requiring so much nourishment, as the elephant, to be carnivorous: then the determination of carnivorousness is thereby negated, and assumed, in the subject of the purpose, as something that, according to his intention, should not occur in his world and creation; but since all experience directly contradicts this, and so much meat is consumed daily by men and animals that the portion of it that would fall to the elephant appears utterly insignificant by comparison, the opposite, rather — the consuming of meat — must be assumed as the creator's intention; and then, precisely because the elephant could fulfil this purpose in all the greater portions, the question must also be asked why he in particular should have come away empty-handed in this respect. When Socrates, following Anaxagoras's lead (cf. Xenophon, Memorabilia I.4, and there Weiske's emendation), recognizes in the arrangement and formation of the animal organism in man a wise and wonderful accordance, and then demonstrates the purposiveness in detail, finding, for example, in the creation of the eyebrows the purpose of protecting the delicately built eye against sweat flowing down from the forehead, by deflecting it to the side: one does not see why so important an organ as the eye — assuming that seeing could not be aided in some other way — was not rather built more strongly, or why so dangerous a superfluity as sweat was not simply spared altogether. In the further explanations in the same place, where, for example, the sparing of sight and smell is given as the purpose of the placement, kept away from the senses, of the ducts of what is disgusting, the whole purposiveness loses itself in a wisdom of ill odor, which leaves unexplained why, given a distance that in any case affords very little security, the disgusting thing was not rather created sweet-smelling, or why the need for eating and drinking was not rather left altogether uncreated, whereby a great deal of want in the world would at once have been remedied. These attempts at explanation according to external purposiveness therefore provide the consciousness of the understanding, which indulges and exhausts itself in them, very little that is satisfying, and show the object of the alleged purpose to be something so singular, petty, and finite that neither does a favorable light fall back thereby upon the universal understanding of the world that is supposed to have had these purposes, nor does the expenditure of means appear justified, if the matter could be achieved more briefly and simply, nor, finally, does the attainment of the presupposed purpose itself, in many cases, actually come about. But even where this last is granted, what is attained is of such a nature that the question arises anew, to what end this, in turn, is now present. If, therefore, in external purposiveness, the infinity of form underlying the relation of purpose in general can indeed also be recognized — which consists in this, that the subject of the purpose attains itself in its object, and coincides with itself in its other — it shows itself, on the other hand, as the deficiency and fundamental defect of this external teleology, that it has absolutely nothing absolute in its content, and can therefore bring things to no pure conclusion, but rather that the externalities posited by it as attained purposes are themselves, in turn, only again means, which proceed into the endless and multiply themselves. A purposive accordance is indeed found and recognized in objectivity in general; but the purpose itself, the final cause in this purposive arrangement, remains unrecognized, and must remain an eternal riddle precisely because it is a finite and singular content that is sought therein and presupposed in the extramundane subject of the purpose. The matter stands otherwise in the consideration of its true relation according to inner purposiveness, as this can be recognized, for example, in the living thing, as the Idea of life, as objectification and actualization of the absolute concept itself. Life is, in itself, the subject of its concept, and likewise its own purpose. For its execution there is here no question of any external and finite deliberation or reflection upon what is purposive; the concept of life carries itself out, as living thing, itself, and this carrying-out is also its purposiveness, the accordance and equality of itself as object with itself as subject. What appears, in this connection, in the inner or outer life-process, as means, does indeed also have the meaning of a means, insofar as something else is thereby mediated; but this other, as a singular thing, is for its part likewise, again, only means, and as such each is interwoven and reflected into the totality of the organism, and held and borne up by the soul of the whole. On the other hand, the means is just as much also already the carrying-out of the purpose itself; for it is a member and part of the objectivity that the concept of life gives itself. But objectivity, in general, is present only as the reality of the concept, and shows its living activity and moments in a continuity of formations, members, and functions, which only together and in their unity constitute the totality of what is to be realized, of which each, taken for itself, is likewise, only in the unity and mediation of the whole, just as much means as, for its part, also an attained, self-subsisting purpose — but each also works, simply as a negative and fluid moment, at the return of the organic movement into itself and at the closing-together of the organic subject with its objectivity. But it is precisely for the sake of this total unity of the subjective and objective that life is, moreover, no longer mere purposiveness, but something higher — it is Idea. What, finally, still concerns the particular purposivenesses that are perceived in the particular species and genera of living individuals, according, at the same time, to the difference of their specific character: here too the same inwardly purposive carrying-out of the concept is present, only with the difference that the universal concept of the living individual realizes itself here in a particular determinateness, and that accordingly the particular subject also finds itself placed in a particular relation to the universal element of life and to its surrounding and conditioning (inorganic) nature — in general, to its presupposed external objectivity — according to which the particular purposiveness in the execution and formation of its organic corporeality also determines itself.

[p. 203]

§ 123. More closely, however, both in its tripartite form (insectum) and in the inner life of its organism, the living subject in its living objectivity and reality presents the determinate differences of its concept — of which each itself has the totality of all [the others] within it — in such a way that it distinguishes itself into three different systems (head, chest, and abdomen or digestive system), which, according to the difference of their formal determinateness, as sensibility, [p. 204] irritability, and reproduction, correspond to the moments of the concept in general — universality, particularity, and individuality — but it is only in the last of these, as the unity of the two preceding and as the concrete totality of the whole, that the subject first completes its actual being-for-itself as an individual. 2) The universal being-within-itself of the subject in its externality, on the side of sensibility — according to which it, as infinitely determinable receptivity, is reflected into itself absolutely in every actual and individual determinateness of its externality, and, as the self-equal universality preserving itself in its particularity, is simple self-feeling — passes over 3) on the side of irritability, or excitability and susceptibility to stimulation from without, into the particular behavior of the self-determining subject, which, in the posited determinateness of the difference, reacts outwardly; whereby it partly determines itself, in a formal and indifferent particularization, into a particular species alongside other species of the living, whose formal reflection into itself is the formal genus (the ordinary logical one), but in general and essentially relates itself to its objectivity presupposed to it from without, and, as living power of resistance and self-determining drive, enters with the same into the relation of reciprocal action. γ) But returning from this behavior toward its presupposed externality — wherein the organism has at the same time posited itself in externality against itself — and, through the sublation and turning-back of this division, relating itself infinitely only to itself, the living individual reestablishes its total and universal unity with itself, and, through the relation of this its concrete totality to itself, now first posits itself as a subject that is for itself and as actual individuality in reproduction — in which, now, the two preceding moments of self-feeling and power of resistance, taken together, first find their truth and their real subsistence, and the individual, as this subjective totality and unity with itself, passes over, out of the completion of its inner life-process, into its outer or objective [process].

§ 124. b) The particular or proper life-process. Insofar as what has just been considered shows the [p. 205] living individual only in its life-process, which completes itself within itself, or in the immediate execution of its concept upon itself: this second process, by contrast, presents the same [individual] in its actual conflict with its outer world, and is to that extent only the movement of the moments already considered, in this outer relation, and the fulfilled return of the subject out of the same into itself. And just as, for that very reason, the whole first process, since it contains only the abstract-universal relation of the living being to itself — a relation not yet emerged out of itself — itself stands under the moment of universality; so likewise the second, which moves within the actually disclosed difference and the relation of the diremptive sides, stands under the moment of particularity, and therefore expresses the character of irritability. On the first side the individual has universal life within itself and consumes out of itself; on the second, outside itself, and consumes from it and preserves itself at its expense. Apart from that mediation with external objectivity into which the subject, relating itself differently to different sides of the same (the elements), enters, through the very differentiation and exclusion belonging to its universal feeling, into a manifold of senses (the theoretical process), and apart from certain particular sides of the real process, in which it likewise again mediates itself with particular external sides (the cosmic, the telluric, the air, etc.), what comes into consideration here chiefly, in the real life-process just named, is that mediation and conjunction of the subject with its object which is rather an annihilation and sublation of the latter, and which coincides in one with the process of reproduction.

§ 125. Through the fact that the living subject has subjected the universal life within it as its objectivity, and has constituted itself, in its subjective reality, as that which is in and for itself and as the essential self-subsistence over against its external and presupposed objectivity, this [p. 206] [objectivity] has become for it the non-self-subsistent and negated, which, as appearance and inessential actuality, is thereby determined to have no power of resistance against the subject. The subject, in itself identical with its substance, and in the certainty of the in-itself nullity of the otherness standing over against it, is therefore, in itself, strained against the same, and, just as there is an excitation from without, so there is in it itself the drive to raise its certainty, for itself, to truth. But because this is first supposed to happen, or because the nullity in itself has not yet become an actual nullity for the still unsatisfied self of the individual, the latter feels itself negated. This feeling of negation — which, within the universal self-feeling, is itself likewise a negation, as a determinateness of the same — is need or lack (hunger, thirst, etc.). Just as universal life has divided itself into the two sides of subjective individuality and objective particularity, so too the subject — which, as the self-subsistent concept of its universal substance and its infinite relation to itself, is the whole itself — has this division within it, which, as absolute inequality of itself with itself, is the absolute contradiction. The feeling of this division, however, or of this contradiction, in which the subject, in its unity, is to itself an opposed thing, or posits itself as negated, is, like lack and need, also pain, which only a living being can feel, and pain is thus nothing other than a living and actual existence of the contradiction. But just as much, in itself, as it is the identity of its otherness, and as the subject's own drive to satisfy the need, the subject is also that which overreaches, which seizes upon its lack in the object, overcomes it, and transforms the sublated and appropriated (assimilated) object into its own individuality, and thereby becomes, and brings about, its identity with otherness also for itself.

§ 126. The inner physiological process, or the process properly so called of [p. 207] assimilation and reproduction, through which the transition of the object — at first only seized upon and appropriated more in a general way — into subjective individuality is completed, likewise shows, within the interior of the organism, first an excitation of the same against the object, as an externality already enclosed by the subject but not yet overcome. This excitation of its particular relation to something external is then, however, likewise once again an inherent negation and division of the organism, in which it enters into externality against itself; and the return of the same, according to its concept (§ 123 γ), already considered in the immediate organism, out of division into its being-within-itself — or the self-determining relation of the subject back to itself, in and out of its self-externalization and negation (reproduction) — is therefore, in the activity of the real life-process, just as much as it is the complete consumption and transformation of the object into the individual organism (in succum et sanguinem), also an overcoming of its own excited moment that has become external, a removal and exclusion from the organism, since its organic activity, in its return into itself, likewise completes itself in a twofold way, through a disjunction inward and outward. If this producing reproduction — in which everything that the living being, in its immediate process with itself, in its formation and its inner organism, has already determined and posited itself as, now repeats and comes about in a real manner — were, in its turning outward, a mere producing: then it would, in this loss of itself outward, also remain lost, and would pass over into an otherness; but as it is, it does not lose itself in its externality, but rather has in it its infinite return into itself, and its production is its own reproduction, through which it, mediated and conjoined with its external objectivity within itself — or in the sublation of its otherness — constitutes itself as the truly and actually for-itself-existing identity of itself with its outer world, and as the self-preserving individuality. Since, however, the living individual satisfies its lack only as a single [being], i.e., always only in a singular manner, and its real life-process is therefore a continually repeating one: the individual subject, though it thereby attains — as it is determined in itself, according to its concept, to do, and has the drive within it to do — also for itself, to overcome its objective world in the particular instance, and thereby to give to its certainty of its [world's] nullity also the truth (i.e., objectivity itself): nevertheless, just as this [world], as external to the subject, for its part, reflected as the universal substance of life into its universality, also preserves itself against the subject, so this [subject] likewise shows, even from this side, for all the infinity of its concept, also its inadequacy as a single [being] to its universal concept, and this constitutes the side of its finitude, which develops still more closely in the following process.

[p. 208]

§ 127. c) The genus-process. The living individual, having emerged from the diremption of universal life into individuality and an objectivity standing over against it, was at first only a presupposition, in which what it is in and for itself was not yet also for itself; rather, it had first to become this through itself, to prove itself as that which is in and for itself, and thus also to fulfill its concept for itself. This the individual has accomplished in its real life-process, in which, through its overreaching of otherness and the sublation of its objectivity (i.e., its negation), it sublated its mere state of being-presupposed, in that it, just as before only out of the concept of universal life, now brought itself forth out of actuality itself, and thereby constituted itself also for itself as actual living individuality. Just as much as this reproduction, as production of itself, shows itself, on its natural side, afflicted with the finitude indicated (§ 126, end) to which [p. 209] individuality as such in general is subject, so, on the other hand, the individual, through its conjunction and unity with its objective world — likewise presupposed — has also again sublated the particularity into which universal life had divided itself, or, what is the same, has raised its subjective totality itself to the whole, and, in accordance with the idea of life, has restored in itself the identity of the same with itself in its individuality. Herewith is posited the third life-process, itself standing under the conceptual moment of individuality as completed individuality, through which the living being concludes and completes its individual life as such, in that it sublates itself into its universality, i.e., returns into its genus.

§ 128. In the genus-process the living individual relates itself to itself as to another living being, to an objectivity outside itself which is not the previous indifferent and sublated one, but is itself — namely, a living individual. The completed individuality, which through the previous process has inwardly conjoined itself with universal life and its idea, and in this identity is "in itself" genus, shows itself, in this relation to what it is in itself, through a new presupposition — which the individual has to sublate through the fulfillment and actualization of its concept (i.e., through its own positing and becoming-for-itself of that which is in and for itself) — divided within itself, in a doubled, singular shape (sexual difference). Only in this difference and its unity has individuality attained its totality. But the individuals, which "in themselves" are this unity and inwardly have the certainty of it, relate themselves to one another, for that very reason, as to an external objectivity, in such a way that reciprocally — not in the sublating of this their externality (for each is the same subjective totality), but in its subsisting — each has the certainty of itself in the other, and, through their union, strives to raise this certainty to truth. But since that which is, in itself and inwardly, identical with the other [p. 210] has this other — as its negation, which is likewise an inner division of the individual with itself — outside itself, here too, as in the previous process, the drive and the feeling of lack, with pain, enter in, which is again an existing contradiction. The sublation of this contradiction in union has the two sides: that on the one hand it gives to the individuals, for themselves, the truth of their certainty, the feeling of their unity, and the enjoyment of their generic universality; but just as much also leads individuality beyond itself, [individuality] which therein dissolves and dies away; on the other hand, however, it first constitutes it again, according to its concept, which is to objectify itself anew, or, as the subjective totality which it contains simply enclosed within itself in its whole determinateness, is to constitute itself again for itself — namely, generates anew, as the germ of a living individual. Just as, in the genus-process (copulation) — the highest, but at the same time self-dissolving, activity of the living individual in the sphere of its natural (animal) life — the individuality of the single [being] as such has its downfall and death, so likewise it preserves itself therein in its universality as genus, and in the propagation of the living sexes, which, as a progress into the (common) infinite, constitutes, over against the inner infinity of life in its idea, the side of immediate and finite existence, in which universal life preserves itself.

Remark. Concerning the contradiction that receives its resolution through the genus-process, here too the result of the union is not merely null (cf. remark to § 57); rather, quite in accordance with the logical [pattern], it has a twofold side, a negative and a positive one. According to the former, for the sides bearing the contradiction, the relation to unity is the downfall of individuality into generic universality; according to the latter, it is the emergence of this universality itself as the higher third, which, though for itself again also only an individual, nevertheless, according to the concept, as the preservation of the genus, also has the significance of this universality. The connection of the genus-process with the death of the individual comes forward so strikingly in some of the weaker animal organizations that they, after their completion in it, forthwith also [p. 211] conclude their life. Aristotle, in his Problems (Sect. 4, in.), sets up, in a question, a comparison which — should it not already have been taken into account in physiology and the circumstance examined more closely — may be cited here: Διὰ τί ὁ ἀφροδισιάζων καὶ ἀποθνήσκων ἀναβάλλει τὰ ὄμματα, καθεύδων δέ καταβάλλει; His own explanation, through the warmth that draws upward in both these cases, to which accordingly the eyes also direct themselves, is indeed insignificant, or at least, even if the circumstance is correctly observed, remains, as against the deeper sense of the matter, only at the level of external observation and explanation.

§ 129. Let us now, for ourselves (still, for the moment, from the side of mere consciousness), summarize what has presented itself to us in the concept of life in its complete development, and what has been brought to our intuition in this object — already itself pervaded by the concept in its total execution: nothing other than the infinity already previously recognized has proven itself to us herein, though in that developed shape, carried out to the point of the absolute opposition of individuality to universality, which the absolute concept itself, and (as the objectivity and actuality pervaded by it, identical with it) the idea, is. Universal life, as it differentiated itself within itself and, in the course of its development, presented itself as a unity of three processes, showed itself to be the self-equal essence and infinite subsistence of everything living, which separates itself off from it, sets itself in opposition to it, develops for itself, and attains its own subsistence — but in its self-subsistence, in which, in absolute perversion, it preserves itself at the expense of its universal essence, it at the same time bears the concept and drive of the latter formed within itself, whereby the self-subsistent has, just as it has its firmness and determinateness, so too its dissolution again. The differences that establish themselves are therefore likewise of a fluid nature — moments that carry their negativity within them, i.e., which, since as differences and determinatenesses they are already negations, again negate this their negation itself, and, as negation of negation, have in themselves their turning-back and return into the universal. There is no greater difference, no stronger and more determinate opposition, than that of self-ish individuality to universality; resting only on its self and reflecting itself into its individuality alone, it would remain absolute inequality and would be evil; but the universal, the [p. 212] absolute power of what subsists, determines itself, through the relation of its essence to itself, for its part likewise to individuality, or to being, also as subject and for itself, that which it is in itself, and is herein infinitely self-equal essence. The separated and detached individuality, however — which for that very reason is also a finite one — has nonetheless the totality of its concept within it, and therein its own support and its power; and, conjoined in itself with universality, and being for itself its totality, it is of itself already related to its universality and reflected into it. But this negative reflection and return into universality is, for the immediate and finite individuality of life, its dissolution and its death — not of individuality in general, which rather, as absolute moment of the concept, again generates and forever preserves itself in the process of life, but of the single living individual as such. The second universality, however, into which completed individuality reflects itself, and which emerges out of the life-process itself, is the genus. The genus, however, however much it is the universality that is in and for itself, has nonetheless no immediate existence — which only the individual has — and therefore points onward to consciousness.

§ 130. Consciousness, however, which in its object and its determinations grasps and cognizes only itself, its own pure essence, its pure concept — or for which, since in the knowing of its other, the object, it only comes together with itself, this other, precisely as other, has thereby sublated itself — is, through this reflection and return into itself, self-consciousness, self-equal I (§ 118), genus for itself. Enriched, through the consciousness of life, still further by the knowledge of itself as a concrete individual, it enters into its new sphere, in order, also as self-consciousness, through the becoming-for-itself of what it is in itself, to complete itself, and to raise its in-itself-existing certainty to truth. After all the earlier shapes in which the objective [p. 213] essence appeared have sublated themselves for consciousness in their untruth and nullity, at last, in its return to itself, or in its becoming as self-consciousness, a new object — life and the living being — has set itself over against it, which, just as it itself, has reflection-into-self, and, as self-relation of itself to itself, is universal subject, and therefore asserts and offers resistance not only in general, but also against the individual subject, as a universal reflected into itself. Yet this universal — which likewise preserves itself and has truth only as universal, and dissolves all subsisting and for-itself-existing individuality again into its universal element, this infinite wealth of life and its inner self-movement, to the knowledge of which consciousness has now expanded itself — is, as such, no immediate existence; wherein it obtains an existence, in individuality, that in turn also sublates the universal again, and thereby makes it itself into a mere appearance. Consciousness is therefore here too not only that which overreaches, but its object — or that as which it knows the same, and what it knows in it as its truth — is the universal and absolute concept itself, in the knowing of which it comes together with itself, or has only itself for object: self-consciousness, to whose particular treatment we now pass over.

## II. DAS SELBSTBEWUSSTSEIN. — II. SELF-CONSCIOUSNESS.

§ 131. Consciousness as such, the knowing of an other as I is, or of an object which for it, at first,

[Consciousness, which] appears in the significance of a being-in-itself or of truth, has through the proper dialectic of its experiences returned to itself. This dialectic, which transformed for it the supposed in-itself of the object into a being-thus of the same only for consciousness in a determinate mode of its comportment, and thereby reduced both — object and knowledge — to mere appearance, to a negated that has no truth for itself, and which, although it allowed consciousness to arrive at a manifold content and its determinations, yet at such as it, on account of their inner deficiency and untruth, itself went beyond once more, and therefore could not find its self-satisfaction in these untenable and self-sublating determinacies of its knowledge, because it did not yet recognize in them its self-equal truth — this dialectic, in whose negative progression consciousness nonetheless, like its object, grew from stage to stage in depth and inwardness, led it at last to the indistinguishedness of its object from itself, to that cognition in which it became manifest to consciousness how and wherein truth is the agreement of knowledge with the object, and the correct sense of this definition yielded itself of its own accord. In this certainty of truth, in which the object has become for consciousness and consciousness for itself in the object the infinitely self-equal and its pure movement, the proper truth of consciousness that has returned into itself is now to be self-consciousness.

§ 132. Self-consciousness, in which truth in general has the realm of its existence and its native soil, is the truth of consciousness also in the sense that consciousness of an other is straightforwardly also self-consciousness, and only for a self-consciousness is consciousness of an other also possible. This other, the hitherto immediate object and its determinations, the entire hitherto contemplated wealth of external existence and its supersensible reflection, therefore remains for self-consciousness, insofar as it is also consciousness, still preserved, yet only in the relation to the unity of self-consciousness with itself, which now has the essence and being-in-itself in itself, not outside itself, and knows it as itself, or only as that which it had already become for consciousness — as something sublated and nugatory, only as appearance whose subsistence has no being and no truth in itself. The sublation of consciousness in self-consciousness has here too the sense and character that the former is only reduced to a moment, but is therein still preserved and retained. The movement and actualization of self-consciousness, which, just as it has for us here emerged as mediated from the foregoing and arisen in accordance with its concept, so likewise appears again as an immediate shape of consciousness in general (immediate and natural self-consciousness) and is to be contemplated in its course, therefore aims at becoming for itself what it already is in itself, or at giving itself the truth of its immediate and thus at first merely inner certainty through the sublation of the in-itself-nugatory other in its independent subsistence, or of the other standing opposed to it as such, and thereby producing and actualizing itself as essence and truth.

§ 133. At first, namely, or according to its simple concept, self-consciousness is only as yet the abstract, nothing further than the tautological I = I, in which it indeed, according to its pure inner distinction in which it repels itself from itself, retains itself as its own object, but equally, as unity and sublation of what is rather not-distinguished, has no object that would be an other, and has as yet no fulfillment and reality. In this immediacy of its concept, however, it is itself a being and something sensuously concrete (a living being), for which at first the entire object of the previous stage, of consciousness, still has an immediate existence as an other and as its negation. But since this objective existence is already inwardly determined for self-consciousness as what is in-itself-nugatory, or has only the significance of appearance and of what is inessential, this negation of the same is self-consciousness's own negative moment, according to which it itself becomes the drive of its realization through the sublation of itself as well as of the opposing negation, and as this drive, self-determining and overreaching, is precisely directed toward being no longer consciousness but self-consciousness, or toward knowing the object as what is in truth not distinguished from it but one with it. This fulfillment and reality in accordance with its concept, which the initially still abstract and immediate self-consciousness obtains against its in-itself-already-overcome or identical other, comes about through its equally sublating its mere subjectivity against the objectivity confronting it as consciousness, and positing its inwardness as externality, as conversely it sublates that objectivity as foreign and given and posits it as equal to itself — or, in other words, gives itself in everything the consciousness of itself. Both are one and the same movement and activity. This goal, however — the equality of its objective and subjective side, of its existence and its concept — self-consciousness achieves in its development through the stages already indicated above (§ 37, p. 63), inasmuch as it a) is first, as singular self-consciousness, drive and desire, then b) enters, as self-consciousness against self-consciousness, into the struggle for recognition, through which it c) becomes universal self-consciousness, but as such completes itself into the unity of consciousness and self-consciousness, or into reason.

Anmerkung. Self-consciousness in its movement and realization as singular, or as drive and desire, is in substance — only in the significance of the same for self-consciousness, or as what comes to consciousness for the self-conscious I — the same as what, in the sphere of immediate life, was the proper or objective life-process, in which the living individual carries out its concept against the objectivity presupposed for it. Likewise, the second process of self-consciousness against a self-consciousness, or the struggle in which the one wrests its recognition from the other, can be compared with the third process of the living individual, the genus-process, since, as in this the individual dissolves into its genus-universality, so in the former the individual self-consciousness dissolves itself into the element of universal self-consciousness. What the merely living individual, inadequate to its infinite concept, cannot achieve as such and in the sphere of its immediate being, is achieved by the self-conscious I, which, going beyond its singularity, elevates itself into the universality of its concept, which it itself is, but therein at the same time also preserves itself as self-consciousness, through the infinity of its knowledge; for it is the life of spirit that begins herein.

### A. DAS SELBSTBEWUSSTSEIN ALS EINZELNES ODER ALS TRIEB UND BEGIERDE. — A. SELF-CONSCIOUSNESS AS SINGULAR, OR AS DRIVE AND DESIRE.

§ 134. Self-consciousness, which has the being-in-itself essence in itself, not outside itself, is thereby already in itself lord of its world and objectivity. The movement of its realization therefore consists in also becoming this for itself through the sublation of the object as its still subsisting negation, and thus elevating its inner certainty to truth. At first still as immediate and thereby sensuously concrete and singular essence, it has for its relation a likewise immediate, sensuous, and indeed to it, the self-conscious, still unequal object — an external existence that is indeed determined for it in itself as a nugatory and therefore resistanceless existence, but in this determination is not yet the actualized negation, but rather still subsists externally and presents itself as independent. Hereby the object becomes for self-consciousness, which is directed and tensed against it as against its own negation to be sublated, the object of drive and desire. Only in itself, not yet for itself, the unity with its object, self-consciousness in its desire and the drive for its satisfaction is conscious of itself as something negated, and it is itself this negation, which it has at once in itself and outside itself. Divided within itself through its negation, repelled from itself and driven outward, it is need and what feels lack, and through the fact that in the ought of its being-in-itself it is at once being and non-being, unity of itself and its negation, it is in itself a contradiction (a contradiction and a unity of which only the living is capable: cf. §§ 124–126), and through this contradiction precisely it is drive.

§ 135. The satisfaction of the drive, however, which presents itself in the movement of the self-conscious subject against its object and is accomplished through the seizure, appropriation, and sublation of the same, is equally the sublation of that contradiction, or as negation of the negation the restoration of the self-equality of self-consciousness — but as one that is no longer abstract but now fulfilled identity, through which it gives itself its reality, and the self-conscious I, which in its movement proceeding from itself to the object that it sublates rather arrives at itself and in the fulfillment with its other closes together with itself, now also becomes for itself what before it was only in itself. In its determinacy as desire, lost in its other, it returns through this other and in the sublation of the same to itself, and, inasmuch as in the other it at the same time becomes to itself an other and objective, obtains therein its self-feeling and its self-satisfaction. Yet as much as the self-conscious I herein elevates its certainty of itself in the other to truth and actuality, it nonetheless makes in its satisfaction the experience that this is not yet a universal overcoming and sublation of the object, and therein still shows itself as an imperfect and deficient satisfaction — that equally, just as the object is sublated only as a singular, the I too gives itself reality and existence only as a singular and natural being, and therefore the universal object of desire remains just as much as desire reproduces itself anew. Or there presents itself for self-consciousness in its experience as desire a twofold side of the object: an unindependent side in its singularity, and an independent side in its universality; only the former is sublated by it in enjoyment, the latter preserves itself. This realization of self-consciousness as the singular is therefore itself still an untrue one, not corresponding to its concept, and belongs to the side of its finitude.

Anmerkung. Those who ask after the truth and reality of things, or believe in them, can also already be referred to eating and drinking, for in this (Eleusinian) mystery it becomes manifest of what constitution this reality is. Yet just as untrue — however much the concept of the matter is for itself true and infinite — remains also the singular self-consciousness itself in its singular satisfaction. On the side of its desire, the self-conscious subject comports itself as an unfree being dependent on its unconscious nature, and is therefore driven, like the animal also; its freedom and mastery over its nature it obtains in its natural enjoyment only through the cognition of its natural necessity in its truth and through its self-determination according to rational and universal ends, or in general through its sublating its drives as such, and, by giving their satisfaction the form of universality and rationality, now positing them as its own, as determined by itself. The ethical also begins only where self-consciousness ceases to satisfy itself merely as singular and to have itself as its end.

§ 136. The singular self-consciousness, which as sensuous and natural desire only selfishly destroys its object and sublates it in its entire existence, therefore attains, still within the limits of its singularity, on another side (which, however, receives its proper significance only at the following stage) a more enduring reality and self-certainty in its object than immediate and singular enjoyment affords it. This more universal and therefore also more truthful realization comes to it, namely, through its sublating only the immediacy and externality of the object that the object still has against self-consciousness, and thereby at the same time its own inwardness against the object, in that and inasmuch as it does not leave the object as it immediately presents itself, but through subjection and appropriation, working and shaping, makes it its own, an existence for itself that serves it and bears its form, and in which self-consciousness in a universal and enduring manner gives itself an objective existence in such a way that it therein has the knowledge of itself, or its pure being-for-itself, also as a being, present to intuition. In the object, this whereby it is at least sublated and overcome in its subsisting immediate form meets its still remaining independent side, at which, on account of the resistance that the object therefore still offers, self-consciousness as inhibited and driven back into itself becomes a negative comportment, becomes labor and cunning, and herein precisely realizes the nullity of the object that is for itself and the certainty of its mastery over the same by giving to the substance of the same the form as its own, or by shaping it.

Anmerkung.
