Under this shape of self-consciousness, which overcomes and sublates its presupposed objectivity in its found form and immediacy, and by treating it as in and for itself powerless and subjected and positing it as its own, attains in it its objective and realized knowledge of itself, belongs the human being as lord of the earth, which he subjugates and works and through his inventions and tools, with everything upon it, appropriates for himself and his service. The tool that the human being invents for the subjugation of what is in itself resistanceless is, as means to the end (cf. Anm. to § 122), itself already an overcome object that, in the purposive form given to it, constitutes the middle term of the syllogistic relation through which the self-conscious subject closes together with its targeted object and makes it its own. In this means, which bears and executes the purposive concept of the subject, the human being has brought his self-certainty of the impotence and resistancelessness of his object to objective intuition in an enduring and universal manner. This self-certainty therefore also gives the invented means and tools their value and significance according to the magnitude and importance of what is possible to overcome and accomplish through them, and constitutes the honor of inventors as well as the pride of peoples who can ascribe great inventions to themselves. Cunning, which is related to this, likewise has the certainty of the unindependence and serviceability of the other, or of the object, as its ground, but consists more specifically in the application and arrangement of a means calculated upon the object's own nature and of such a constitution that the other is driven through itself, through its natural kind and constitution, into what is intended with it, and thus moves itself to its own seizure and overcoming, while the subject of cunning, in the self-certainty of the infallibility of its means, watches the outcome withdrawn into itself and lying in wait.

§ 137. According to both contemplated sides, according to which self-consciousness arrives at its reality or at the actuality of its self-certainty in the in-itself-nugatory other, the object has presented itself as one still unequal to the subject and as something selfless, which precisely for that reason, incapable of any resistance, lets its presupposed negation become the actual sublation of its immediate existence, and herein fulfills its own determination to be nothing for itself but to belong to and serve the self-conscious power, to be a moment of its reality or to be entirely sublated by it. But the self-conscious subject, which as abstract and tautological I = I has as yet no reality but only expresses its empty concept, has through its realization and fulfillment with its opposing other — both in the self-feeling and self-satisfaction as desire, and in its knowledge of itself in its other, more universal realization — for its part become to itself an other against its first abstraction, an objectified I in which it is equal to itself. The self-conscious I, which at first distinguished itself only purely from itself and was only a theoretical I = I, has in this manner really doubled itself. This constitutes the transition to the higher and more truthful reality of its concept, which it achieves through the fact that the other I, as one actually divided and different from it, yet — since it is likewise an actual I — equally an I in itself equal to it, i.e., as another and free self, becomes and confronts it as its object. This becoming of I for I, of self-consciousness for self-consciousness, each of which is to and will have its self-certainty and self-equality in the other, yields initially the process of recognition.

### B. DAS SELBSTBEWUSSTSEIN GEGEN EIN ANDERES, ODER DER ANERKENNUNG UND DAS VERHÄLTNIS DES SELBUND UNSELBSTÄNDIGEN PROCESS DER STÄNDIGEN SELBSTBEWUSSTSEINS. — B. SELF-CONSCIOUSNESS AGAINST AN OTHER, OR THE PROCESS OF RECOGNITION AND THE RELATION OF THE INDEPENDENT AND DEPENDENT SELF-CONSCIOUSNESS.

§ 138. The concept of self-consciousness, to be I = I, has through the self-objectification of the same developed into the actual division and doubling of the self-conscious I, and now presents itself as a comportment of one self-consciousness to another self-consciousness and as their reciprocal movement, in order to restore through the development of this relation its unity and self-equality in a higher reality. There are accordingly two self-conscious I's that immediately and externally become objects for one another, and both at first — since each is what the other is — as absolutely equal, free, and independent I's, appear in a reciprocally equal relation to one another; of which each, however, in accordance with its concept to be realized, is also directed toward knowing itself in the other and recognizing its own self in it. Since as I in general, or as abstract I, each is equal to the other, to that extent each already intuits itself in the other as the equal, and this equality that they have in advance is their mutual attraction. Yet each is also an immediate external existence, and has as a living individual its self sunk in this existence just as much as it has its proper or being-for-itself I as a singular still withdrawn and hidden behind the same. According to this external side, each is therefore rather the unequal and still unknown of the other, an other for an other in general, and so as I itself and in its being-for-itself an absolutely independent other object. The immediate existence, in which both the self's own existence and its being for the other self or for other in general is contained, shows itself accordingly not only as the middle term between both I's, but also as the objective negation of self-consciousness, or as that which hinders it from knowing and recognizing itself as the equal I and self in the other, so long as it has not overcome and sublated the immediacy of existence as this negation or as other. But since this negation is equally the subject's own negation of its subjective concept, or of what it is in itself and therefore also ought to be for itself, herewith there is again a contradiction present, and through this the drive of the subject to go outside itself and through the sublation of the contradiction in the object to realize itself.

§ 139. This contradiction, which calls forth the process or struggle of recognizing for its sublation, and the relation of the two self-consciousnesses herein present, rests more specifically on the following moments. Self-consciousness as such is without distinction in both the same, or the universal unity and equality passing through both. According to this side, in itself each I already has in the other the certainty of itself, but precisely therefore only as yet as universal and abstract, not yet as actualized — or this certainty is not yet for itself. For each, the other I in its immediate, consequently still unmediated, existence and subsistence as a singular is indeed a being for the other, but precisely therein still an absolutely other that shows and asserts itself as free and independent. This independence and freedom, however, has the pure being-for-itself of each as its ground, and this being-for-itself is consequently itself what makes each an other for the other. But since self-consciousness, as what knows itself as the essence, or according to its concept that it has to realize and according to which the other as such is already in itself sublated and a nothing for it, relates itself through itself to the same and moves against it in order to become it for itself or actually to sublate it — consequently here is directed toward sublating the pure being-for-itself of the other self-consciousness itself as such and making it its being-for-itself — it can arrive at this its reality, or its own self-certainty of itself in the other, since this other is itself consciousness, only through bringing its own pure being-for-itself to intuition and certainty in the other, or through being recognized by it. Before it accomplishes this, it is, as before as desire, itself again being and non-being at once, and through this contradiction precisely driven to the movement of struggle that goes outside itself. This movement, however, as reciprocally equal, is equally also the doing of the other self, of which each has the equal intuition in the other.

§ 140. Furthermore, however, the immediate existence of each is present as the middle term between the two being-for-itself extremes. Through this, therefore, the mediation or the proper process of recognizing has to proceed, since only in this as the being for other and through the same can the one self-consciousness in the other arrive at its self-certainty, or at the recognition that its pure being-for-itself is also the valid one for the other. But precisely for this, as has already been noted, it is just the immediate and external aspect of this existence that is again the hindering and opposing, or the objective negation of the subject, whose negation and sublation is required for the realization of its self — and indeed for the one individual as well as for the other. Each, namely, can assert its freedom and independence only by showing that it does not have its pure being-for-itself in its external existence. When it rather withdraws its being-for-itself from this its being for the other, at which and through which alone it can be seized and coerced, and shows that nothing depends for it on this existence, on its life itself, that it does not have its essence therein: only through this self-negation and renunciation is it pure being-for-itself, free and unconquerable.

§ 141. But since in its immediate and external existence each self-conscious I at the same time also has its self-feeling, its sign, and its tool — in general the existence and subsistence of its being-for-itself and self-consciousness, without which it would not be there at all, and consequently could not find its recognition either — there follows from this the opposite: that the one is as necessary and essential for it as the other, immediate existence or life, namely, just as much as the negation of the same, which grounds the freedom of self-consciousness. It is accordingly this intertwined and double contradiction that, in the movement of the two self-consciousnesses and their direction turned against each other — which has the realization of their self-certainty of the one in the other, and indeed through the negative middle term and mediation of their external existence, as its end — ignites the struggle for recognition, and indeed a struggle of life and death, in which each puts itself in danger in order to preserve its independence and to win the recognition of its self in the other. The resolution of this contradiction and struggle is a twofold one: either the untrue one through the death of one individual, or the more truthful one through the subjection of the one to the other, from which the relation of independent and dependent self-consciousness, or of lordship and bondage, emerges.

Anmerkung. The relation into which here one self-conscious individual enters with another, and the struggle that they fight for their recognition, has been regarded, with respect to the social life of human beings, as a war of all against all, and this as the transition from the state of nature to the state of right. The nearest consequence of the struggle, however, is not yet the state of right, nor the contract, but the subjection to a lord, hence a condition of violence, out of which, however, through universal and reciprocally mediated self-consciousness, universal recognition develops, which is the foundation and element of the state of right and contains the possibility of the contract, which presupposes as its element the already accomplished mutual recognition of the person and of his possession, which through recognition becomes property. In the further development and organization of the state of right, however, into its rational shape as the state, the contract according to its limited nature, which is merely something abstractly of the understanding, remains something very subordinate, and is by no means to be regarded as the highest form under which one has to comprehend the social life of human beings in peoples and states, since it is here rather the concept of ethical spirit that unfolds and founds the realm of its rational actuality.

§ 142. The danger of death into which each of the two self-conscious I's enters also has only the significance of a danger, since the preservation of life is no less essential and necessary for the reality and recognition of self-consciousness than the freedom of pure being-for-itself, which first makes itself true freedom through the negation and disregard of immediate existence. Death is therefore indeed a sublation of the immediacy of the self, but not in the manner of mediation; rather, the sublating of immediate existence itself happens in an immediate (natural) and therefore crude manner. Through the death of one individual, therefore, the contradiction underlying the process of recognition receives its resolution so little that this outcome, against the end of self-consciousness — which through the struggle wants to arrive at its actualization in the other and at the recognition and validity of its self — is rather the unresolved and greater contradiction. The dead, precisely through the death that he did not shun, or through the renunciation of his life and immediate existence, which was not the essence for him, has proved and asserted his pure being-for-itself; he has passed away without the recognition of the other; to an existence and subsistence of this recognition, which was the end of the movement, it has accordingly not come.

Anmerkung. Between this struggle of individuals in the state of nature, i.e., the condition of self-consciousness still sunk in the crudeness of natural drive and desire, and the duel in the civic relation, there exists this distinction: the latter arises from the offense and injury to a self living in the element of universal, reciprocal, and secured recognition, hence an already recognized and thereby valid self, upon which personality and personal honor rest, and has as its end the restoration of the injured self and of the recognition that was individually sublated only by another individual. But because such an injury is at the same time also an injury and sublation of the already existing universal right and universal will, through which each individual has his recognition and the security of his personality, it is precisely for that reason that this duel is itself already a violation of right, namely of the prevailing universal right, according to which the restoration of injured personality no longer belongs to the revenge of the individual but is a matter of universality. Moreover, this duel too, when it ends with the death of one individual, misses its end in the same manner as that former one, or this end is the same contradiction against the purpose of restoring the sublated recognition, since the dying offender, who fought for the maintenance of his violation of the other, rather persistently refused recognition and in death no longer gives it.

§ 143. Against this untrue manner of resolution, which is the death of one individual, and which precisely for that reason affords the drive and end of self-consciousness realizing itself no objective self-satisfaction — because through the total sublation of immediate existence as the element wherein it was to come about, the self-preserving I arrives at the actualization of its self as little as the one sublated in its immediate existence, and accordingly the truth itself, which was supposed to emerge for self-consciousness therefrom, is destroyed — in this initially still reciprocally equal relation of the two self-conscious I's, the next true sublation of immediacy as the opposing negation consists only in such a sublation as rather preserves and retains what is sublated within itself (cf. Anm. to § 57); or the process and struggle for recognition receives its resolution in such a reciprocal mediation of the previously immediate and precisely therefore negative, according to which the two being-for-itself extremes of self-consciousness at first become unequal to one another and maintain and comport themselves toward one another in an unequal manner. This unequal relation results from the fact that one of the two subjects, which in the struggle proves and asserts its pure being-for-itself as the essence both for itself and for the other, thereby realizes itself as the independent and recognized self-consciousness, or makes itself lord; but the other, which has and knows the essence of its reality rather in its immediate existence, and therefore prefers life, subjects itself to the first and initially renounces its own recognition, whereby it preserves itself only as abstract, unrealized self-consciousness, and presents itself for the first as the dependent and recognizing one, or makes itself bondsman.

§ 144. This relation, into which the two subjects so determined against one another enter, is now to be more closely contemplated in its content and in the further determinations and experiences that emerge from it for self-consciousness. The independent self-consciousness, first of all, which as the recognized one has arrived at the certainty of its actualization in the other — which it has in the other's subjection and service as objective intuition — and from this serving of the other receives back its own self and being-for-itself as realized, is through this dependent self-consciousness at the same time also related to immediate existence, which is the object of desire and labor (§§ 134–136), and conversely through this, insofar as it is its own need, also to the former. But since the working over and overcoming of the object for the satisfaction of the common need is accomplished by the dependent and serving I, the lord has the enjoyment of the object of desire without feeling its resistance, which it still opposes to the bondsman in labor. According to this side, the two unequal I's, although each is self-consciousness, comport themselves toward one another like self-consciousness and consciousness, since the one that has become selfless and servile, which showed itself fettered to immediate existence, has this also as consciousness initially for its object and as bondsman is itself reduced to thinghood. Yet precisely for that reason, also the experience that independent self-consciousness makes of the dependent one as its self-actualization, or that which it receives back from it as its own reality for intuition, is the opposite of its true and proper self. For it has indeed realized itself as singular being-for-itself and as such asserted its particularity; but since it was also supposed to realize itself as free and independent, it rather experiences that the selflessness of the servile consciousness constitutes its reality and objective truth. Since accordingly it does not receive itself back according to its truth from its objectivity, self-consciousness has from this side not yet in truth realized itself.

§ 145. But since also for the independent and ruling I the immediate existence and the entire presupposed external objectivity — to which it is at first related only immediately through the dependent and serving I — constitutes the side of its actual subsistence and self-preservation, of which it can no more do without than the other, which from the start held to this side as its essence: here the commonality of needs and the care for their satisfaction enters as a middle term common to both subjects of self-consciousness in relation to immediate existence, wherein they unite with one another and become identical to each other. Only in this sphere of commonality does the subjection, working over, and shaping of the object of desire — the labor and cunning that has fallen to the dependent subject — also receive its true place and significance (cf. § 136), through the character of universality that it here assumes, inasmuch as equally, just as at the object self-consciousness realizes itself in a universal manner through an enduring and secured possibility of its self-satisfaction (through acquisition and possession), the movement and activity of the subject too ceases to have the satisfaction of the singular self alone as its end, and this rather becomes, as common satisfaction, the end and object of self-consciousness. For the independent subject, at first the subjugated and serving I was the middle term through which it related itself to external objectivity and closed together with it; but since this objectivity is now itself subjugated by the subjugated I, it becomes rather for the two extremes of self-consciousness — the independent and the dependent — the identical middle term in which they mediate themselves with one another and balance their inequality in a higher, mutual recognition that goes beyond the singularity of the self. This, however, is brought about from the side of the dependent subject, which is yet to be more closely contemplated.

§ 146. The dependent self-consciousness, which became dependent precisely by divesting itself of its singular being-for-itself and of the claim to the validity of its immediate self for the preservation of its immediate existence, which counted for it as the essential, and preserved itself only as abstract self-consciousness, has as the I that has become selfless and negated by the other actual self, in this negation indeed a valid and thereby as essence actual independent self-consciousness for its object, but not as its own — rather outside itself, in the lord, whom it has recognized as such, and whose will it carries out. But inasmuch as this self-consciousness, on the one hand divested, on the other hand driven back into itself, in the service of the lord works off its singular and self-will — in general its entire inner and outer, sensuous and natural immediacy, already inwardly dissolved through the fear of the absolute lord, death — in every particular, and makes the alien sense its own; and therewith at the same time brings about the sublation and overcoming of the objective immediate existence, and in the working over and cultivation of the same — wherein through its form at the object it becomes conscious of this negation of immediacy as "its own" — cultivates itself (acquires skill and dexterity in the particular): it is precisely the dependent self-consciousness that, through the fear of the lord, makes the beginning of wisdom, and, by realizing the concept of self-consciousness more truthfully, also turns itself around into true independence.

Anmerkung. Evidence of how the bondsman rather, through his cultivation, through his skill and adroitness — in general through his becoming the more universal self-consciousness against the singular and one-sided one of the lord — knows how to make himself the essential and valid one, can be found everywhere in the historical appearance of lordship and bondage. Essential, however, for the emergence of this cultivation, which is at the same time the transition to the true liberation of self-consciousness, is absolute fear and the discipline of service and obedience in it, since only thereby is natural consciousness shaken through and through and its immediacy dissolved, and self-consciousness gains the negativity of its pure being-for-itself (i.e., its freedom). Only thereby does it also become capable, in its spreading out over the conscious objective actuality and in its shaping of the object, of becoming conscious of its pure essence. Without passing through discipline and obedience, no cultivation is possible at all, and no absolute cultivation so long as the immediacy of consciousness and self-consciousness is not overcome from all sides. Without this inner penetration and overcoming, the external world and its own pure essence remain external to it, and the shaping of the objective according to one's own sense becomes vanity and obstinacy. "Von der Gewalt, die alle Wesen bindet, / Befreit der Mensch sich, der sich überwindet." ["From the power that binds all beings, / The human being who overcomes himself is freed."] Goethe.

§ 147. For the dissolution and becoming-fluid of everything fixed and immediate that subsists for itself was what, following the emergence of self-consciousness, constitutes the simple essence of its concept and its foundation, or the negativity of the I in which it has and knows its pure being-for-itself and self. Since now the servile consciousness, which has passed through the fear of death and in the service of the lord through discipline and obedience, carries out and accomplishes this negativity on its own as well as on objective immediacy, and, just as it surrendered and divested itself of its own immediate and merely singular being-for-itself to the lord, so conversely in its service and activity for the common and in the certainty of its validity through the same, compels and wins its recognition from the lord: this self-consciousness rather turns itself around into the true and essential one, and into that which, in the consciousness of an in-and-for-itself-being and enduring reality, not only goes together with its other — whose independence at first negated its own — into a common self-consciousness, but thereby also in general makes the transition to the reciprocally certain, universal recognition mediated through the sublation and negation of the immediate (merely singular) being-for-itself, or to universal self-consciousness.

Anmerkung. In the transition from the contemplated division of self-consciousness to universal self-consciousness, there are contained some moments that could be brought forward and presented as particular shapes of consciousness, as they have also, as such, fallen into historical appearance. The independent self-consciousness is indeed independent, but has realized itself only as abstract I, and not also as consciousness of objective actuality, and therefore, in the lack of cultivation and of liberation from its own immediacy, has not preserved its pure being-for-itself. The dependent or servile consciousness, on the other hand, has indeed made up the deficiency of cultivation and begun to fulfill the condition for the true freedom of self-consciousness, but it has not become an actually independent one that knows itself as being-in-itself and in all otherness in general is also for itself. Its shaping and working over extends in part not to the whole of conscious actuality, but as a manifold, internally differentiated, and therefore also particularized activity, only to the occurring objects of life and its concrete entanglements, and can therefore be taken as universal only in its significance; in part it is also only the form through which it gives itself the consciousness of itself at the object, but does not also overcome the otherness of the same in its essence. But the form is the same as being-for-itself, and the being-in-itself was for the independent self-consciousness — which as such is indeed being-in-itself but had not yet become so also as consciousness — still contained in the object of consciousness itself. Now since both fall together into a unity, I as being-in-itself is object to itself, and its comportment to the objective essence such that it is therein for itself, or knows it as itself. But this pure movement of consciousness, which itself constitutes the essence for it, is thinking, and the thinking I free self-consciousness. Thinking distinguishes itself from other modes of comportment to the object, especially from mere representing, in that the latter lets the object subsist as a being or immediate that is an other than consciousness, whereas for thinking the object is indeed also a being, but a sublated and mediated one, or one in whose knowledge I is immediately at home with itself, or with which, as one not distinguished from it, it knows itself in unity; thinking therefore moves in concepts. Since now self-consciousness has hereby also in its element as thinking become just as truly free as it has restored itself to the negativity or pure concept of consciousness and to equality with it, so according to this development the goal of self-consciousness — precisely this, to become equal to its consciousness again, or for itself what it already was in itself through that — is achieved, and the unity of both is already present from this side. The particular historical appearance, however, in which the next unity of the moments still falling apart in the relation of lordship and bondage appeared according to phenomenological progression as a particular new shape of consciousness, was the Stoic self-consciousness, driven back into its pure I and absolutely free in its being-for-itself, which, elevated above lordship and bondage, knew itself free against both, and against every dependence and externality of its immediate and singular existence, held fast alone to its pure thought and what through this counted for it as true and good; and then in further development Skepticism, the completed negative thinking, which completely sublated and destroyed all certainty and truth and everything counting for consciousness in any way. But Stoicism, only according to the universal essence, or in itself, the unity of being-in-itself and being-for-itself, and therefore again corresponding to the moment of the abstract independent self-consciousness in the relation of lordship and bondage — just as in Skepticism the completion of the negative direction against everything objective corresponds to the other moment in that relation — also remained standing only at the concept of freedom, or the mere abstraction of the same, without carrying it through on a positive way by the content of life and elevating it, through the fulfillment of the same, to an actual freedom at home in the necessary relations of life in a self-conscious way and thus itself living. And just as in action it did not get beyond this negative comportment toward the external, in which from everything it withdrew only into the empty consciousness of its pure freedom, so too in thinking it remained standing only at the pure and empty thought and at the formal; only what was thought had validity for it, but since for the content of the same only the universal demand that it be a rational one, i.e., only its form, was asserted, and instead of its fulfillment and spreading over the world only abstractions and particular determinacies, not something truly universal, came forth: so here too it was rather the content that was lacking, and the Stoic doctrine has, for all the sublimity of its talk, remained something empty and tedious. In Skepticism (cf. Anm. to §§ 12 and 64), which completely carried out from its negative side the freedom of thought at whose mere concept Stoicism remained, what is remarkable is that in it the dialectic — which showed the untruth and unindependence of things in the manner in which they appear to sensuous certainty, to perception, and to the understanding, and the untruth of these modes themselves — itself appeared as an immediate shape of consciousness and constituted the essence of a self-consciousness; only that this, precisely because only this was its essence and it had no positive content whatsoever, in the empty night of its negativity and simple withdrawal into itself and in its insensibility [ataraxia] toward everything, set its dialectic in motion only more contingently for its armament and assertion, and thereby for its entire comportment in general was given over to contingency and to its own contradiction with itself and with life. On this and on the further development see Hegel's Phenomenology, p. 129 ff.

### C. DAS ALLGEMEINE SELBSTBEWUSSTSEIN. — C. UNIVERSAL SELF-CONSCIOUSNESS.

§ 148. Self-consciousness, which at first only as singular gave itself existence at the selfless object, then as self-consciousness for self-consciousness on the one hand as selfish I in another self through its negation and suppression likewise realized itself only as singular and immediate being-for-itself, but on the other hand as dependent, through the sublation of its immediacy and the divestment of its particular being-for-itself, worked itself up to a substantial reality and a positive mediation with the former in reciprocal recognition and self-certainty of the one in the other, has herewith passed over into universal self-consciousness. This, already thereby determined as what it is, is accordingly no longer the unequal self-consciousness that negates another self, but as self-consciousness, i.e., as actual knowledge that in its other goes together with itself, the positive and universal certainty of itself in the other. Each self is therefore this universal self only insofar as it continues itself through another self and is identical with it, and in its subjectivity, raised above immediate and singular being-for-itself, itself objective, knows itself in the other and the other in itself. Universal self-consciousness is therefore indeed still a singular self and subject, but as such in its universal knowing and willing absolutely free and independent, and the reciprocal self-certainty in which each rests as in its own and in its truth is so universal that each recognizes the other just as much as itself in the other, and equally knows itself recognized by the other, and so each in this knowing and recognizing has the objectivity and reality perfectly equal to its subjectivity.

§ 149. Self-consciousness as universal has herewith arrived at the existence of its concept. According to this it had determined itself to be the self-equal knowledge of itself as I in all other, or in all objectivity. As this it has now actualized itself, inasmuch as its consciousness has again become equal to self-consciousness, or its conscious actuality and objectivity to its subjectivity, and I as I has become equal to all I's. Universal self-consciousness is therefore also the absolutely universally self-knowing, since in its knowing it knows all knowing as equal to it; it is free self, for it has subjected its sensuousness and naturalness to itself, and divested itself of its immediacy as singular being-for-itself and of validity as a singular or particular, and thereby restored itself to the pure negativity of the self-conscious concept, or to the pure I and self. It is precisely for that reason absolutely independent, because in its pure being-for-itself it also knows itself as being-in-and-for-itself, and against this no other subsistence is any longer able to assert the significance of an independent. This universal self-consciousness is the realm of every higher spiritual community and universality, the element of the substantiality underlying all essential spiritual and ethical relations, in which alone every true ethical substance — such as family, people, fatherland, state — as well as all virtues and ethical relations, such as friendship, love, honor, etc., have their subsistence and their truth. For since only in this universal self-consciousness is I in truth itself and in all others its self-equal object (I = I), and each I has the significance of all I's as all I's have the significance of being I: this one and universal self-conscious essence, which passes without distinction of the singular selves through all and is reflected in each self-consciousness, is already also the essential element in which the concept of spirit takes root and its realm begins.

§ 150. At first, however, the truth of universal self-consciousness, after every inequality of an otherness has vanished for it and the object to which it relates as consciousness is no longer an other for it, is only this: that it is one with its consciousness, or the unity of consciousness and self-consciousness in general is herewith posited. For also for self-consciousness as such, which in the objective world of consciousness at first gave itself the self-certainty of its being-for-itself only through its form, but beyond this sublation of their form and immediacy could do nothing over their substance, there has in the negativity of free universal self-consciousness — which is its pure being-for-itself equally also as pure knowing and free thinking — restored the absolute concept and the entire result of consciousness as such. Universal self-consciousness is, however, essentially still a singular self, which as such itself claiming validity and recognizing others relates itself to other singular selves — which for their part are likewise universal self-consciousness and are valid only in this universal element — and in them has its identity with them present to intuition and certainty. But since these various selves, still subsisting for themselves against one another in the universality of their self-consciousness, are in essence and truth no longer different: this contemplation and the sublation of this indifferent diversity as a distinction that is no longer a distinction elevates self-consciousness as I flowing together into one, an in-and-for-itself universal I, to its final stage of its in-and-for-itself-being universality and objectivity, i.e., of reason.

## III. DIE VERNUNFT. a) Die Vernunft als solche, und das vernünftige Bewusstsein. — III. REASON. a) Reason as such, and rational consciousness.

§ 151. Reason, as its concept has here initially resulted for us, is the unity of consciousness and self-consciousness, and as this, according to its emergence, mediated for us through the foregoing; in itself it is immediate and solid, self-mediating only through itself unity. Consciousness as such had developed through the various stages and shapes of appearing knowledge to the point where its object ceased for it to be an other as such, and consciousness rather recognized in its object and its determinations only its own pure essence and the proper movement of its pure knowing I. As this truthful cognition, which in its other is only at home with itself, it became infinite knowledge, comprehending consciousness, pure concept, but in this knowledge that goes together with itself, through its inward recollection (reflection upon itself), as the I that recognizes itself and is equal to itself, self-consciousness. For this, for which already as consciousness the objective other had determined itself in truth as no other or as something sublated, or which already in itself had the certainty of itself in all other, this certainty has also been elevated to truth, inasmuch as through the liberation from its natural concretion and one-sidedness as immediate singularity it elevated itself to purified universal self-consciousness, or to the actualized certainty of being, as I, indistinguishable from all I's, or in general only pure, universal I, i.e., rational consciousness.

Anmerkung. The significance with which the word reason enters here can, as also previously what was comprehended under understanding, seem to deviate greatly from what one is otherwise accustomed to represent to oneself under this name, especially if one is accustomed to taking both only as mental faculties — the understanding as the faculty of concepts, reason as that of syllogisms. And indeed in psychology, where these names appear again as distinguished activities of spirit as intelligence (but also there such that the understanding sublates itself in its higher, reason), their mode of consideration is other than here, where their significance is brought forth and determined through the phenomenological progression, without their essence being thereby a different one. Reason, to which is equally ascribed the faculty or cognition of what is in and for itself, of the infinite and unconditioned, is one and the same with the reason that syllogizes, for everything rational, in that it is a rational, is also a syllogism, and the syllogism the absolute form of rationality. But likewise the concept is the rational and different from the syllogism only in that the concept-moments, which have stepped forth from the unity of the concept in apparent independence, are through the syllogism mediated back to their unity and concluded together. But in the manner in which one is accustomed to treat concept and syllogism in ordinary logic, where they come into consideration only from a merely formal, entirely external side, without regard for the in-and-for-itself rational of their content, one has let not only the concept but also the syllogism fall back under the mode of the understanding, and such syllogizing is no longer a matter of reason. It has already been recalled earlier (Anm. to § 26) the obstacle to philosophical cognition stemming from the psychological mode of consideration, according to which reason is customarily regarded only as a faculty of the thinking subject, but not as the in-and-for-itself-being substance itself, which is equally the object to be cognized and thereby object of itself, as it is the cognizing or subject, and in its own knowing closes together with itself. With the word reason all manner of misuse is in general committed by common consciousness as well as in science. Much is given out as pronouncements or commands of reason that is merely cognition of the understanding, or also only subjective opinion and certainty — not to mention that in the manner in which such assurances are given in the name of reason, the rational form of mediation, through which something brings the proof of its rationality with it, is in any case dropped. What in mathematics at the boundary of the quantitative and thereby of mathematical science itself passes over into the qualitative and here can arrive at its correct cognition only through the rational concept, is according to an inherited usage called irrational. That the so-called rationalism in theology, a merely understanding-based and formal thinking procedure in the mode of external reflection, precisely suffers from a lack of reason and rather comports itself negatively against the deep rational content of its science, has already been remarked earlier. Utterly absurd, however, is in philosophy the establishment of two kinds of reason, a theoretical and a practical, if this separation, in which it shows that the one reason has not yet come to its consciousness, is supposed to have the sense that what in both constitutes precisely the content of reason and its essentiality is in the one something other than in the other. At the same time, in this separation the practical reason is supposed to make good and complete what has gone awry for the theoretical. What a superficial and external reflection, which also underlies this misunderstanding, means to say by this amounts to nothing other than that reason as a subjective faculty, i.e., as the faculty of those philosophizing in this manner, has been more fortunate in its practical determinations as a domain lying closer to common consciousness than in its theoretical ones, and trusts itself to cope with those more readily. But a reason that as theoretical has not known how to find its concept is precisely thereby also incapable of finding it as practical, and of shaping and executing itself as practical science in accordance with its concept and truth. The rational actuality of life has therefore also gone further than this practical reason, which has only a very insignificant practice, has been able to bring it scientifically.

§ 152. Reason (in the phenomenological progression at first as merely appearing knowledge of reason, only as yet rational consciousness) is now the immediate unity and the truth of consciousness and self-consciousness, or the ground into which both — which at first appeared for themselves as distinguished modes or shapes of knowledge in general and were so contemplated — now return as into their true unity. Reason is accordingly 1) on the side of consciousness the knowledge that in all other only sojourns at home with itself, is only self-equal and certain of itself; it is consciousness and thereby has an object, an other; but this other is for it no other, it is pervaded by it, it is itself the rational; it is in this other absolutely only at home with itself and has and recognizes therein only itself, its pure movement, its determinations expressing only it and its essence, its rational thinking; it is the other itself. Thus it is 2) pure self-certainty, consciousness knowing itself as subject, self-conscious I, but by no means singular or particular I; from this immediacy and one-sidedness of mere existence, from this subjectivity that is only subjectivity, it has liberated and purified itself; rather only pure, absolutely universal, in-and-for-itself-being I in general; and 3) since it is this in an immediate and solid unity, as subject it is absolutely nothing other than what it also is as object, and conversely: subject-object.

§ 153. Although reason is the self-equal essence passing through and present in all singular self-conscious I's, it is yet as reason different from universal self-consciousness — which as such also does not have reason in and for itself as its foundation or substance — in that the latter still essentially rests on the singular, immediate I and self, which knows the other singulars as equally such self-conscious I's and selves, and only so has its reflection as the same consciousness in all singulars; reason, by contrast, is self-conscious I only as the in-and-for-itself-being universal, as the absolutely rational, and the rational I therefore as such, or in the knowledge of its rationality as its substantial foundation and truth, knows itself not as singular but as truly universal, and as in-and-for-itself-determined (i.e., objective) subjectivity. In the cognition of the rational, therefore, the conscious I also arrives at home with itself as at a final, beyond which no further going-beyond can take place (§ 25 and Anm.), and inasmuch as herein it is absolute knowledge, it at the same time has the certainty that in this cognition every rational I is equal to it.

§ 154. In this its absolute universality, wherein reason is equally self-knowing concept, I, as self-conscious cognizing and comprehending of all other, and both in a unity, it has therefore also completely overcome and sublated the foreignness of the object as an other for I (§§ 26 and 32). Recognizing all other in itself and itself in all, its subjectivity is itself its objectivity and conversely; its determinations are absolutely universal and objective, and rational thinking is the thinking of the matter as it is in and for itself, and the matter is in and for itself as rational thinking has and knows it. Reason is therefore also, in the consciousness of its identity with the other, the certainty that the essence of things, the world, everything objective in general, is rational and thought, just as conversely its pure thoughts are also the determinations of the essence of things. As this identity and objective universality it is the in-and-for-itself-being truth itself, which knows itself as truth, as knowing subject, I, as what is known object and essence, and both the same. (Cf. §§ 9, 29–32, and 118.)

§ 155. For consciousness, in its first universal consideration (cf. § 17 ff.), a distinction and separation of being-in-itself and being-for-consciousness had resulted, such that the being or being-in-itself of the object still showed itself to consciousness as something other than what it was at first for it, and consciousness therefore — which recognized the object, as its knowledge of it, in the determinate manner in which it had first presented itself to it, as mere appearance — was precisely for that reason led beyond the determinate manner of its knowing through itself. The sublation of this separation has already come about in the consciousness of the infinite, or in the knowledge that became concept (§ 118), but is now completed in the absolute knowledge of reason; for reason, whose certainty is no longer different from its truth (cf. §§ 29, 30), is the absolute certainty, certain at itself, that what the object is in itself, it also is for it, and what it is for it, is also in itself, since it in general has the certainty of being all reality and presence, and is the in-and-for-itself-being truth itself. It is therefore also to be regarded only as a misunderstanding and as an untrue representation of a consciousness that, to be sure, has reason, but also still essentially misjudges reason and the nature of knowledge in general and of its relation to objects, if this consciousness, against the certainty and unity of reason, still lets an unknown and unfathomable in-itself of things step forth, as has occurred in the common critical idealism. (Cf. Anm. to §§ 9, 13, 20, pp. 37 ff., and to § 88.) For us, the critique of consciousness through its various stages has furnished the executed proof of what the presupposed being-in-itself of the object is everywhere; it has been shown that it too was only a presupposition of which the consciousness that recognized itself therein in its determinacy (§ 25) made the experience that it belonged only to its appearing knowledge, and which it therefore in its further progress also itself sublated (§§ 19, 20 ff.); it has in general become manifest that there is no being-in-itself that would be something other than the pure thought and concept of reason, but that this is not merely a being-in-itself but the being-in-and-for-itself.

§ 156. Consciousness as such, in its determinate significance, is essentially the knowledge of an other, i.e., of an object that is still something other than the knowing subject. (Cf. §§ 10, 36, 37 Anm. 2.) In this sense, reason as such, for which no other is any longer present and which is only its own object, has ceased to be consciousness. (Cf. § 37.) It is only the pure knowledge of itself and its own pure thinking, and moves as this only in its own realm, in order to actualize itself as reason and to become for itself the reality that it is in itself. Reason is, is being pure and simple, or rather the sole being-in-and-for-itself and all reality; only the rational is what truly and actually is: but it is also the movement directed by it upon itself, to become for itself what it is, or to actualize its concept. This movement is its thinking; but reason is only in thinking, or its pure being is in the same unity also the pure thinking through which it becomes its own pure knowledge, and only this its absolute self-apprehension and self-presentation is also its absolute self-actualization. In this movement, which it accomplishes in order to reach itself as its goal and to know itself as all reality, it is at first presupposed for itself, or only as yet its concept, and therefore distinguished within itself into itself as its being-in-itself reality and objective substance, and into itself as the proper activity of its becoming-for-itself, or the execution and actualization of its concept. The goal of this its execution, in which it as subject reaches itself entirely as object, is accordingly itself in its unity and totality, or as the pure idea, which is the absolute knowledge of the in-and-for-itself-being truth that knows itself as all reality.

§ 157. For us, however, the concept of reason, as it has emerged from the dialectical movement and development of consciousness and self-consciousness as the truth of all other modes of knowledge, is also as yet nothing more than only this concept, which accordingly still lacks its execution and actualization for us. Yet this execution, which also for us (i.e., in our pure and rational self-consciousness) is to elevate the certainty of reason, certain of itself as all reality, to truth and absolute actuality, or to develop and set forth the essence and substance of reason completely and in the method adequate to its concept, and which in the one and same movement of this systematically progressing development and cognition is at the same time equally the path of reason recognizing itself in its substance, apprehending and comprehending itself — this execution is nothing other than the science of philosophy itself (cf. §§ 1–3 and 9), and initially, i.e., in the distinction from the concrete realities, nature and spirit, in which reason realizes its knowledge of itself, insofar as reason as its own pure thinking is directed only to its immediate substance or its pure essentiality, the pure science of reason, or logic, which is the pure science in general and as such.

§ 158. But precisely for that reason, for us also the purpose of the propaedeutic, which was to mediate for us the concept and element of pure knowledge in which alone the actual science of philosophy has and can have its movement, extension, shaping, and completion (§§ 4–6, cf. § 118), has already been attained herein — in that the knowledge into which we have entered with the cognition of the concept of reason is precisely this pure element. After the untrue modes of consciousness — of opining, representing, mere abstraction and reflection, in all of which knowledge in its object was not yet the self-equal, or the certainty of truth was still separated — have through their own dialectic, rooted precisely in this separation and inequality (§§ 25 and 27), sublated themselves for the knowing subject as untrue modes of cognition; after the I as self-consciousness, through the liberation and purification from its own untruth, i.e., its subjective immediacy and singularity, has obtained the certainty of being as I all I's, whereby as pure universal I it entered into the element of a substantial, in-and-for-itself-being, and therefore for every I absolutely certain content: so too the element of pure knowledge of philosophical science is no other than this absolute knowledge itself, which immediately carries with it the certainty of the in-and-for-itself-being truth. It is the pure universal self-consciousness of reason, or I, insofar as, purified from every untrue mode of a concrete and unscientific comportment, hence as only pure, universal, absolutely self-equal I, in its thinking and knowing it is immediately only at home with itself, with its pure and absolute essence, and as this pure, self-equal activity is therefore immediately pure cognizing, knowledge of truth, and absolute comprehending. Since in this pure element certainty and truth are in immediate unity and the object of cognition is the already being-in-itself reality of reason, which through cognizing realizes itself also for itself and brings itself to its absolute actualization in knowledge: so too the determinations and productions of this thinking and cognizing, which considered as particular cognitions are the pure thoughts, are not merely forms of a content but also this content already itself — and indeed pure, absolute content of the in-and-for-itself-being — and likewise pure thinking in general, unseparated, is also pure truthful being, against whose absolute truth and reality everything that might otherwise be taken for being disappears in untruth and nullity as a being of mere opining and representing, i.e., of untrue knowledge or non-knowledge.

Anmerkung. Since with objective truth one had in mind only an extant existence, the content of representing and common experience, and with the content of logic one thought only of the given contingencies, e.g., of a Cajus or Titius — according to which it can indeed easily happen that something extant of this sort does not agree with the content of some logical proposition, and all the more so since the logical form grasped only in an abstract, i.e., merely formal manner is as such already something untrue in itself — common thinking was indeed right to say that logic has to do merely with the forms of thinking, but abstracts from all content and all objective truth. For it has in fact also abstracted from all content — not merely that of its opinion — and from all truth, and has made its logic into something very empty and superfluous. That the logical determinations themselves already constitute the content and contain the pure truth of reason, and that everything true, only insofar as it falls under this pure content, can also actually be truth — this has not occurred to this consciousness. But that logic has the pure substantial truth of reason itself for its content, the total execution of this science has to show.

§ 159. However much reason in and for itself is the in-and-for-itself-being truth and its absolute knowledge, and in this sense mere consciousness has sublated itself in it (§ 156), yet it too, according to the mode of appearance, enters into the series of shapes of phenomenological consciousness, insofar as it, at first only inwardly or in itself present and before its absolute self-apprehension, presents itself in rational consciousness, or consciousness that has reason. This consciousness, which as such without the dialectical-scientific path that we have traversed — the result of which was the concept of reason for us — appears immediately alongside other shapes of consciousness, has indeed, in accordance with the concept of reason, the certainty of itself as all reality for its principle, but this certainty is for it only at first an immediate or inner one, not yet a self-conscious one, or the consciousness is only at first a being of reason, not yet its knowledge (cf. § 7), and therefore shows itself in its knowing and doing initially only as rational drive. On account of this immediate certainty, reason also appears in this consciousness in the mode of assurance; but as mere assurance of its truth it experiences that other assurances with equal justification place themselves beside it or against it, and thus is rather the opposite of its rationality, which demands mediation through cognition for every self-consciousness. But this mediation is for us precisely contained in the path traversed, through which the concept of reason, as the certainty of being all reality, has produced itself of its own accord and with the insight into its necessity. Likewise, therefore, in the consciousness that is at first only rationally being, reason has to produce itself as the knowledge of itself and its absolute reality through the dialectical movement of this consciousness, in order to arrive at the same goal.

§ 160. The path on which appearing or existent reason moves toward the universal goal of its self-certainty of being all truth and reality, and on which also the one-sided and common idealism (cf. § 155) with its categories of the pure I falls in as a particular appearance, is accordingly itself again a phenomenological development and dialectic of consciousness that has reason for its inner principle. In its immediate certainty, however, that the objective essence is no other than it and that its determinations are also immediately determinations of the matter itself and of the essence of things, reason, seeking itself in its otherness and actualizing its self, moves and develops itself, in general, again under the determination of consciousness and self-consciousness through various stages and shapes of its existence and comportment in appearance: but no longer as before the abstract consciousness and self-consciousness, but as rational, a consciousness and self-consciousness already carrying a substantial content in itself, and as reason already in itself certain of its object, rather such that it spreads itself over the entire natural and spiritual universe as its realm, in which it knows itself at home, pervades everything objective and particular of the same, comports itself in it as self-determining, and in everything gives itself the knowledge of itself, and thus arrives at a wealth of manifold laws and determinations that immediately count for the rational consciousness — having and knowing itself in its objectivity — as the expression of the essence and as truth. But the goal at which, through this phenomenological execution, reason dialectically progressing from the abstraction of its reality through its concrete otherness to its fulfillment and universal actualization finally arrives, is none other than the already designated goal of reason in general, with which it receives back not something other but itself from its world, and reaches itself in the unity and totality of its concept, or, apprehending itself as its own concept and producing itself as its own absolute knowledge of itself and its infinite reality, becomes for itself the in-and-for-itself-being truth that it previously was only in itself. As this self, however, which knows itself as infinite universality, it is spirit. b) The concept of spirit.

§ 161. Spirit is in general infinitely universal self, absolute vitality, the knowing (for only to spirit does knowledge belong, as that through which and wherein it immediately has its existence), and therefore the infinitely free; it is the concept — not as nature presents it in an external, immediately being mode, or only objectively — but the concept that as object and subject is concept, or that has its existence as concept. Spirit is therefore absolute negativity or self-relating activity, whereby its essence is precisely freedom; it can therefore renounce all that is external and its own externality, in order to be infinitely for itself in this negativity and to remain therein identical with itself. Against this abstract side of spirit, however, which constitutes the simple self and being-in-itself of its concept, its truth is rather not only to be infinite universality but also to know itself as the same (i.e., as spirit), and to be for itself the absolute idea, which is the reason that knows itself as all truth and reality. (§ 156, at end.)

Anmerkung. As for the distinction of spirit from nature, it does not lie in the fact that nature had another idea as its absolute ground — nature is rather the same pure idea and reason that spirit also is, and which spirit as such recognizes again in it. But nature is this idea merely being, as the unfree, or in the element of immediacy and externality, wherein necessity and contingency govern its existence. Spirit, by contrast, as the absolutely free, is the idea in that it produces itself as it through knowledge, and so becomes for itself the infinite universality and in-and-for-itself-being truth that it is. Spirit is therefore also the truth of nature, or the ground into which it returns, sublating itself.

§ 162. The concept of spirit was therefore for us already contained in the foregoing (§§ 154, 156, and 160), inasmuch as spirit is precisely the achieved goal of reason, or reason itself, which has elevated its certainty of being all reality to truth and infinite actuality. Reason is thus spirit, and spirit the truth of reason. Or, since it was noted (§ 160) that the reason that is only at first existent, or only in itself certain of its reality, goes through the relation of consciousness and self-consciousness again in order to become for itself what it is in itself, so spirit as this that has become is the unity of rational consciousness and self-consciousness, or reason itself, insofar as it has equally sublated its mere existence and found itself, and actualized its self, and so is conscious of itself as its world and all reality, as of this as itself. Just as therefore all earlier modes of consciousness, or rather the whole relation of consciousness and self-consciousness, at first distinguished, already returned into reason as their truth, so these modes now find themselves all the more sublated in spirit, yet with this difference: that whereas reason as their nearest negation negated them as they were for themselves and in their truth rather dissolved and carried them in itself as its own moments, spirit in its unity and truth has indeed likewise negated and dissolved them in itself, but in its infinite universality is at the same time also their positive subsistence. For they are not outside spirit but rather belong to spirit, yet in their subsisting-for-themselves separateness are also nothing more than one-sided and untrue abstractions of its objective or subjective side, to which spirit in its freedom equally determines and resolves itself, as it also again sublates and dissolves them in itself, in order to be actual as spirit.

§ 163. But spirit as spirit, or spirit in its truth, is the in-and-for-itself-being essence that as such actually knows itself, or the absolute substance of the in-and-for-itself-being truth and its own absolute knowledge, infinite universality that is its own self-certainty. As this essence certain of itself in its infinity, it indeed distinguishes itself within itself as subject or pure self of spirit, and as object, but in such a way that as the being-for-itself pure concept that it is as subject, it is equal to its object, and this as being itself is for itself and knows itself as itself. But since spirit is thereby simple totality, it is equally necessary to grasp this totality as one that is neither subjective nor objective, because only so can spirit be true and actual totality. As this self-equal infinite universality, however — neither as consciousness related to any other any longer, nor limited by any object, nor as what is known being anything other than itself, nor standing in relation or in a relationship to any otherness at all — spirit is only infinite negativity and distinction within itself, pure comportment only to itself; it is accordingly the absolute self-activity, which begins only from itself and its being, produces only itself, determines nothing other than itself, and relates only to its determinations.

§ 164. As spirit according to its concept was just now grasped as the absolute totality, so it is as absolute and infinite spirit. Yet these determinations contain for us also as yet nothing more than only the concept of spirit, not yet its execution and self-actualization, as it comes into consideration partly phenomenologically or as the history of spirit in its existence, partly as science in the third main part of philosophy, in the doctrine of real spirit. For just as spirit has resulted as the truth of reason, i.e., as its final determination, which is precisely this: to produce itself as the knowledge of itself as all reality through its infinite actualization, and thereby to be spirit, so conversely the truth of spirit is also reason, or to know itself as reason, in order to be spirit in its truth. Spirit is indeed according to its concept in and for itself the absolutely infinite, objective reason; yet as infinite universality that still has its concept as its presupposition, it also has an immediate existence, or exists, without yet having known and actualized itself as reason and as self. But this reality it obtains only through knowledge, or through itself as intelligence, which is precisely the knowledge of spirit. Before it has therefore itself found the reason that it is, i.e., grasped it through its knowledge, or before this has reached in it its infinity and absolute universality and comprehended itself as such — wherein it as the infinitely universal and actual self is precisely spirit — it is indeed immediate and existent spirit, or is itself an immediate and external being that as being of spirit is however already in itself higher than the immediate being of nature: but it is thus not yet infinite, not absolute, but only finite spirit.

§ 165. Spirit is finite through the limit posited for it, which is nothing other than the inadequacy of its existence or its immediate reality to its concept: hence not in the sense that this limitedness were something fixed and belonged to its essence and concept. It is rather in itself infinite spirit, and finite only in that it does not yet know itself as infinite and has not yet itself sublated its limit. Spirit is therefore finite only insofar as it immediately finds itself and immediate determinacies in itself, in general insofar as it has an immediate existence and finds a world presupposed for it as its limit. This finitude is therefore also not its truth but, as inadequacy to its concept — according to which it is rather the opposite of immediacy and the absolutely free and negative — its untruth. But as in itself infinite spirit it is also itself the activity of sublating this its untruth and limit, which is immediacy and finite determinacy, through knowledge, and of developing itself through its intelligence and self-determination to its absolute liberation and infinity as the fulfillment of its concept. But apprehending itself in its infinity and totality, it also recognizes how the earlier limit of its finitude and its determinacy was one posited by itself. (Cf.

§ 162 at end.)

§ 166. The liberation of spirit from its finitude is its own activity and a development of its essence through various stages of the same, each of which contains a particular liberation from one side and determinacy of its immediacy and is a step nearer to its universal liberation. The most important distinction, grounded in the progressive development of spirit itself, within the sphere of its finitude is that it is first subjective spirit, then becomes objective. Inasmuch as it still has its concept entirely as its presupposition and therefore relates itself to itself as to an immediacy to be sublated by it, it has — just as the sublation of nature in general is the emergence of spirit — the immediacy as its nature and the determinacy thereof for its beginning and point of departure (anthropology), from which it initially as singularity becomes its concept for itself, or subject and conscious I (thus the object of phenomenology and this propaedeutic), and in this sphere of its subjectivity develops itself already as spirit into intelligence and self-determining will (the doctrine of subjective spirit as such, psychology). But as spirit that brings the concept of its in-and-for-itself-being reason — with which it has at the same time found the truth of its self-determination as absolutely universal and objective will — to spiritual existence and realizes it as universal actuality, inasmuch as it founds the realm of right and ethical life and its rational order and organization (natural right, ethics, and the state), it is objective spirit. But the spiritual substance that has realized itself as subjective and objective spirit, and as the knowledge of its infinite universality becomes the object of both immediate intuition and representation and of self-conscious and comprehending knowledge (art, religion, philosophy), completes itself again into the totality as the idea of absolute spirit. End of the first division of the propaedeutic.

§ 167. How the proximate purpose of the philosophical propaedeutic — to mediate for the consciousness not yet partaking of philosophical cultivation the element of pure knowledge, in which alone the actual science of philosophy has and can have its movement, extension, shaping, and completion — has already been attained through the foregoing execution, has already been more closely indicated earlier, § 158. It has been shown sufficiently that natural and ordinary consciousness cannot so directly and without ceremony proceed to the cognition of philosophical truth, i.e., of that truth which is the ground of all truth and the pure, in-and-for-itself-being truth itself, and through which alone all other truth partakes of actual truth; but rather that it needs first to purify and liberate itself from a multitude of opinions, representations, and presuppositions — in general from its entire natural manner and immediate mode of comportment, indeed from the mode of representing itself, as from the mode of merely reflecting and formal thinking, and from all mere subjectivity and its limited views and presuppositions — in order to be, as the pure, universal I of reason or as pure rational thinking, both capable of the cognition of truth and assured of it. At the same time, on this path of its own liberation for truth and science, the subject was already offered and acquired a not insignificant content of philosophical cultivation and cognition, of thinking and knowing, along with many individual pieces of knowledge and observations from the domain of philosophy and the history of its treatment.

§ 168. Insofar, however, as preliminary knowledge from psychology (the doctrine of subjective spirit), as well as a provisional survey and general development of the system of philosophy itself and its inner articulation and division, together with an assessment of the various systematic modes of comportment toward philosophy, especially the various dogmatic ones, were reckoned as belonging to the propaedeutic of philosophy, or at least deemed serviceable — which was to constitute the content of the second division — the transition to its first section has already been made through what was adduced at the close of the present one concerning the concept and essence of spirit. It should be noted here, however, that the explanations and determinations given concerning the concept of spirit, as also earlier those concerning reason, could only remain at the general level of the subject matter, and therefore all the more — since the earlier critical-dialectical treatment of appearing knowledge no longer obtained here — have their scientific grounding and production to await only in the executed science.
